### J. v. VONDEL’S
# JOHN
## THE MESSENGER OF REPENTANCE.

->*Contained in six books.*<-

An arched metal frame supports a central pulley with a chain and a small bucket suspended above the well. At the very bottom of the engraving is a rectangular plaque with the motto: TO EACH HIS TURN <note>original: "ELCK ZYN BEURT"</note>.</image-desc>

->at AMSTERDAM,<-

->For the widow of ABRAHAM de WEES, at the<-
->Middeldam. 1662. <note>The Roman numerals "CIƆ IƆ C L X II" represent the year 1662.</note><-

<summary>This is the title page for Joost van den Vondel's 1662 epic poem "John the Messenger of Repentance," a religious work centered on the life of John the Baptist.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory front matter, following the title page. It contains a formal dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, including an analysis of his coat of arms and a poetic tribute to his name.</meta>

<image-desc>A circular heraldic seal of Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen. The center features a quarterly shield: the first and fourth quarters depict a lion rampant (the arms of Nassau), while the second and third quarters also feature a lion rampant. The shield is encircled by a border containing the Latin motto "As far as the world extends" <note>original: "QUA PATET ORBIS"</note>. The entire seal is surmounted by a large princely crown and flanked by stylized foliate flourishes.</image-desc>

->To the most illustrious Prince and Lord<-

# JOHN MAURICE,

Prince of Nassau, Count of Katzenelnbogen, Vianden, and
Diest, Lord of Beilstein, Grand Master of the Order of Saint John <term>Order of Saint John (Johanniter Order): A Protestant chivalric order; Johan Maurits served as the Bailiff of Brandenburg.</term> at
Jerusalem, in the March, Saxony, Pomerania, and the Wends, Governor of the Duchy of Cleves, the Principality of Min-
den, and the Counties of Mark and
Ravensberg, etc.

> If it ever happened before, as people say, that names
> Accorded with those who bore them, and their character and deeds
> Were in harmony: so it happens still in our own day.
> If I am permitted by your grace, and if one
> May speak, as the truth is confirmed in you:
> For if one begins to analyze the Prince’s name from its roots,
> Then MAURITIUS is imprinted in our minds,
> The brave prince of the pious Theban regiment, <note>This is a play on words regarding the Prince's name. The author is comparing Johan Maurits to Saint Maurice, the legendary leader of the Theban Legion, a group of Roman soldiers who were martyred for their Christian faith.</note>

<summary>This page is a formal dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, listing his extensive noble titles and beginning a poem that compares his character to his namesake, the martyr Saint Maurice.</summary>
<keywords>Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, dedication, heraldry, Katzenelnbogen, Order of Saint John, Saint Maurice, Theban Legion, titles</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen. The previous page established a parallel between the Prince and his namesake, Saint Maurice (Mauritius), the leader of the legendary Theban Legion.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N.

*A hero, in the Emperor’s service, who, calm and upright,*
*Acquitted himself in armor, and showed that he carried his weapon*
*Not out of a desire for glory, but for the honor of the Empire,*
*As a terror to the wicked, and a refuge for the pious:*
*But when Caesar* <note>original: "Cezar"; here referring to the Roman Emperor in the legend of Saint Maurice</note>*, to appease Heathendom,*
*Ordered him to swing the censer for the idols,*
*And to serve the godless altar, according to Numa’s blind style* <note>original: "Numaes blinden stijl"; Numa Pompilius was the legendary second king of Rome, credited with establishing many pagan religious rites</note>,
*He offered up his life to the sword, and the axe,*
*And the horse’s hoof, and let it be entirely crushed to dust,*
*Also without setting himself against the court of Rome*
*With outward violence. Thus he gave, simply and honestly,*
*Like a crusader-hero* <note>original: "kruishelt"</note>, *to God and Caesar each their due,*
*With seven thousand soldiers, brave souls,*
*Who all fell patiently in one troop,*
*And won the noble martyr’s crown through a struggle*
*In which one suffers for the name of the great Savior.*
*So stood your bravery, always encouraged to strive*
*For the honor of the holy empire, even at the loss of life,*
*To support the Emperor in his right with your sword;*
*As your prudence guards the electoral authority,*
*Wields a free scepter, and defends Christ’s kingdom,*
*When the Elector* <term>Elector (Keurvorst): One of the German princes entitled to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</term> *bestows favor upon you in his name;*
*Where LEOPOLD* <note>Leopold I (1640–1705), Holy Roman Emperor</note> *is hallowed, and, like*
*A golden morning sun and light, comes forth from Austria* <note>original: "Oostenrijck"</note>
*To nourish all that draws breath.*
*With what wisdom you knew how to smooth troubled minds,*
*Burning like a sea stirred by storms,*
*And gently snatch from Discord the prey*
*For which she flamed, so as not to leave Europe,*
*And so many souls, without hope*
*Of their preservation! Such a laudable deed*
*Follows the example of Maurice, who gave his life for God’s name.*
*The name JOHN* <note>original: "JOANNES"</note> *also does your highness no harm.*
*This name brings grace, and you shine through God’s grace,*

<meta>catchword: Ge-</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the poetic dedication to Johan Maurits, drawing a direct parallel between his diplomatic and military service to Emperor Leopold I and the martyrdom of Saint Maurice. It praises the Prince for his wisdom in maintaining peace in Europe and his devotion to the Christian faith.</summary>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen. The previous page compared the Prince’s military and civic virtues to the martyr Saint Maurice, emphasizing his loyalty to both the Holy Roman Emperor and the Christian faith.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N.

*Just as a prince and member of the blessed realm,*
*Which strikes the hereditary enemy’s host along the Danube’s flow,*
*And dictates the law to old Byzantium* <note>original: "Byzantium"; here referring to the Ottoman Empire, whose capital was Constantinople/Byzantium</note> *and its troops.*
*No less did this name honor you, when you, called with joy*
*By Elector FREDERICK, at the voice of FERDINAND,* <note>Frederick William, the "Great Elector" of Brandenburg, and Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand III.</note>
*Were consecrated Grand Master of ancient Jerusalem,*
*And the Order of Saint John, in which the German lords,*
*Knights of the Cross, defend the rights of the hospital and Christ’s kingdom,*
*Under the shadow of God and His messenger of penance,* <note>original: "boetezant"; likely referring to John the Baptist, the patron saint of the Order, who preached repentance.</note>
*Upon which my Muse, with her heroic stride,*
*Gallops magnanimously, as if she, with the help of the choral hymns*
*Of heaven, could capture that light as required.*
*But it pleases me to see the Order of Saint John* <note>original: "heiligen Joan"</note>
*Arise, and its team of knights of the cross,*
*Rejoicing that Nassau, elevated to the Grand Mastership, / Bestows an even richer luster upon the first ancestor.*
*It was founded about six hundred years ago,*
*From distinct tongues* <note>original: "onderscheide tael"; referring to the "Langues," the administrative divisions of the Order of Saint John based on the languages spoken by the knights</note>, *for the service of the common good,*
*Chartered and expanded, to protect the faith of the cross* <note>original: "kruisgeloof"</note>
*From the encroachment of the Turk, and the yoke of Barbary.*
*It first planted the seat of war in Jerusalem,*
*By the authority of the holy church, upheld by the voice*
*Of all princes, and bound to its law and rules,*
*To be sealed in public by a martial vow.*
*The sword is hung at the knight's side,*
*To chain the hereditary enemy’s fury with might*
*Throughout his life, and to follow Christ’s*
*War troops wherever the head* <note>The Grand Master of the Order</note> *shall call him.*
*All ownership of property is strictly denied him,*
*And only the bare use for necessity is granted:*
*Just as marriage remains forbidden to the knight at all times,*
*To fight more unburdened for the faith of the cross,*
*And to turn Heathen unbelief away from God’s house.*
*The black mantle, and the white knight’s cross.*

<summary>This page describes Johan Maurits' appointment as Grand Master of the Order of Saint John (Knights Hospitaller) and recounts the Order's history, mission to fight the Ottomans, and its strict vows of poverty and celibacy.</summary>
<keywords>Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, Order of Saint John, Knights Hospitaller, Grand Master, Brandenburg, Crusades, Elector Frederick William, Emperor Ferdinand III, Jerusalem, Ottomans</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, Grand Master of the Order of Saint John (Bailiwick of Brandenburg). It shifts from the Prince’s personal virtues to a historical account of the military orders—specifically the Teutonic Knights and the Knights of Saint John—and their role in defending Christendom in both the Holy Land and the Baltic regions.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N.

It fulfills its purpose and mark: yet trotting in God’s ranks,
So the armor and the red surcoat cover the loins.
Thus they shed their blood for many years in the field,
And repelled lawful, savage violence with force,
Out of compassion, to quench the rising flame,
Stoked by the son of Hagar, <note>original: "Agars zoon"; a biblical reference to Ishmael, traditionally used here to refer to the Ottoman Empire.</note> strengthened by Tartary and Russians,
And grown to the very peak of supreme command,
To endure, as our punishment, until it grieves God.

The imperial chronicles, come into our hands, report
How Germans, in the army of Christian heroic warriors,
In service of the Holy Sepulchre, acquitted themselves blow upon blow,
As a comfort to the brothers, and a terror to the enemy.
Thus they fought eastward: but when Heathendom in the North
Held the coasts, along the Baltic Sea, in extreme danger
With burning, scorching, murders, and shameful plundering,
The Order took the field, to cover Germany
Against such a heavy hailstorm with their cross-shields. <note>original: "kruis-rondas"; a rondas is a circular shield, here specifically one marked with the knight's cross.</note>
One saw the Weser stream, and Elbe, and Trave pull
Through Prussia, and the harvest of Livonia at the drum
Of Salza, <note>original: "Saltze"; referring to Hermann von Salza, the influential Grand Master of the Teutonic Knights who led the expansion into the Baltic.</note> to bring the unenlightened and raging Heathendom
To reason, which teaches them, with changed minds,
To offer their weapons to God and the standard of Jesus:
For watchful Rome had, in the face of envy,
Consecrated the German Knightly Order <note>The Teutonic Order.</note> out of zeal
With that Grand Master, to lead the campaign,
And to extend Christ's kingdom of the cross wide to the north.
It succeeded also all along the Baltic, when God gave His blessing;
It burned down the "church-forests," <note>original: "kerckwoudt"; referring to the sacred groves of pagan worship.</note> and broke down the bush-altars:
And Livonia, which, to the scorn of the Savior, girded the sword,
Voluntarily surrendered itself into the Order of Saint John:
After which Prince Albert in Brandenburg, <note>Albert of Brandenburg-Ansbach (1490–1568), the last Grand Master of the Teutonic Knights who converted to Lutheranism and became the first Duke of Prussia.</note> a head
Of the holy order, as a vassal prince, promised loyalty
To King Sigismund, <note>Sigismund I the Old, King of Poland.</note> for the fief of fruitful Prussia.
This peaceful treaty settled the fearsome rushing

<meta>Catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page provides a poetic history of the German military orders, detailing their transition from fighting in the Crusades to their northern campaigns in Prussia and Livonia against pagan forces, eventually leading to the secularization of Prussia under Albert of Brandenburg.</summary>
<keywords>Order of Saint John, Teutonic Knights, Baltic Crusades, Hermann von Salza, Albert of Brandenburg, Prussia, Livonia, Ottoman Empire, Sigismund I</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen. The previous page discussed the Teutonic Knights' defense of Northern Europe and the Baltic; this page focuses on the history of the Knights of Saint John (the Hospitallers) in the Mediterranean and the specific legendary contributions of the Counts of Holland and the city of Haarlem to the Crusades.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N.

*The storms of war, and the knightly cross-fief, hang*
*Upon the Margrave’s <note>Referring to Johan Maurits, who held the title of Prince of Nassau-Siegen and served as the Herrenmeister (Grand Master) of the Bailiwick of Brandenburg of the Order of Saint John.</note> will, from whom the Order receives its command:*
*Just as MAURITIUS, in service of his lord,*
*Was also graced with this immortal honor.*
*If the discord between the Roman and Greek church,*
*And the Empire of the East and West, in the wrestling ring*
*Had not struck against one another, like rams with their horns;*
*The power of the faith of the cross, born for love and peace,*
*Would have been mighty with heaven's help, through the leadership*
*Of the Knightly Order of Saint John, prepared for death,*
*To blunt the sharp horns of the crescent moon*
*Upon the four pairs of points of this cross, <note>The eight-pointed Maltese Cross; the eight points are traditionally said to symbolize the eight beatitudes.</note> and, before the closing*
*Of those horns, to have forbidden their growth in time:*
*Now Jerusalem remained—which Dirk, <note>Likely Dirk VII, Count of Holland, or referring generally to the Dutch counts' involvement in the Crusades.</note> once revered*
*As Count of Holland, lastly saw the crown shine upon its head—*
*Left wild: for the storm of the cruel Saracens*
*Continued: therefore the Order by necessity replanted its seat of war*
*At Rhodes, which held the crown upon the sea for two centuries,*
*For the salvation of Christendom: yet even there, in mournful form,*
*However piously it fought, it was driven out; thus Emperor Charles*
*Granted Malta to the Knight, as an invincible rock,*
*From where the Grand Master's heart, armored in defiance*
*Of the Turkish Ocean—which tears the earth to tatters*
*And swallows kingdoms—could endure all those storms.*
*When the Order, after much strife, suffered shipwreck at Rhodes,*
*It escaped the bloody sword to this refuge in the sea.*
*Ptolemais <note>Acre (Akko), the last major Crusader stronghold in the Holy Land.</note> dared in vain, strengthened with German powers,*
*To await God’s enemy twice with shield and spear:*
*The Count of Holland placed himself in the balance in vain*
*With King Louis, <note>King Louis IX of France (Saint Louis) during the Fifth or Seventh Crusade.</note> where the ship from the Spaarne <note>A river in Haarlem.</note> with the saw*
*Bolted before its bow, came rushing up the Nile stream,*
*And crashed the chain of strong Damietta <note>The Siege of Damietta (1218–1219). Legend says a Haarlem ship equipped with a saw on its bow cut the harbor chain, allowing the Christian fleet to capture the city.</note> into pieces:*
*For which the Emperor, who rewards such bravery,*
*Placed the bright sword of Haarlem in the light of the stars,*

<summary>This page recounts the history of the Knights of Saint John, tracing their journey from Jerusalem to Rhodes and finally to Malta. It highlights the legendary role of the Count of Holland and the city of Haarlem in breaking the siege of Damietta.</summary>
<keywords>Order of Saint John, Knights Hospitaller, Rhodes, Malta, Damietta, Haarlem, Count of Holland, Crusades, Saracens, Ottoman Empire</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen. While the previous page established the Order of Saint John’s transition from the Holy Land to Rhodes, this page focuses on the desperate defense of Christendom against the Ottoman Empire, highlighting the fall of Constantinople (Byzantium) and the heroic stand of the knights.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N.

And Jerusalem’s ancient patriarch still blessed that sword
With his cross—a knightly benediction:
In vain stands Scanderbeg <note>Gjergj Kastrioti, known as Skanderbeg, the Albanian national hero who led a rebellion against the Ottoman Empire.</note>, like a rock of steel,
Crushing the enemies of God thirty times over:
In vain he defends Albania, like Hector defending the kingdom of Troy,
And endures Sultan Murad’s <note>original: "Amurats", referring to Murad II.</note> army before the walls of Krujë <note>original: "Kroie".</note>:
While the Empire of Constantine <note>The Byzantine Empire under its last Emperor, Constantine XI.</note> and Rhodes still stood,
All that savage tumult fell upon the Knights of Malta,
And the Order of Saint John, which against the plagues of Mecca <note>A poetic reference to the Islamic Ottoman forces.</note>
Has worn the sword at its side for many centuries.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;What a mournful day it was, a day worthy of tears,
When the Ottoman host, enraged, by land and sea,
On foot and on horseback, came to curse Byzantium,
Launching assault after assault, and driving from seven directions
His "elephants" <note>Likely a metaphor for massive siege engines or the heavy, lumbering force of the sultan's army.</note> against the walls and towers,
With all his military might, proud of their crescent moon,
Seeking to extinguish the "mother-light"—the sun of the world <note>Byzantium/Constantinople was often envisioned as the light of the Christian East.</note>.
How that great city stood, like the fiery furnace of Mount Etna,
Engulfed in smoke and smog, fire and flame on every side;
Where lightning flashed upon the walls and thunder rolled along the ramparts.
The Tower of Saint Romanus <note>The gate and tower where the final, fatal breach of Constantinople's walls occurred in 1453.</note>, shaken by such thunder,
Finally collapsed with a roar that made the earth tremble beneath,
Falling down, it filled the moat with stone and rubble,
And invited the enemy into the gaping void,
As if over a bridge made of ruins and fragments,
To rush through the breach and charge straight into the city.
Eight hundred knights of Latin and Greek descent,
All equally resolute and proud of spirit,
Reconciled to death for God's sake, took their place in the breach,
And cheered at the sound of drums and trumpets,
Amidst the storm of heavy cannons <term>cannon: originally "kartou," a large-caliber siege gun</term> and terrifying gunpowder <note>original: "donderkruit," literally "thunder-powder."</note>.
Thus was Mehmed <note>original: "Mahomet," referring to Sultan Mehmed II.</note> frustrated and held at bay.
To crush the spirit of the defenders, he immediately 
Commanded an assault on the walls from yet another direction.

<summary>This page depicts the heroic but tragic defense of Constantinople (Byzantium) in 1453 against the Ottoman Sultan Mehmed II, focusing on the bravery of the Knights of Saint John in the breach of the Saint Romanus Tower.</summary>
<keywords>Scanderbeg, Sultan Murad II, Krujë, Constantinople, Byzantium, Fall of 1453, Knights of Saint John, Mehmed II, Siege warfare</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, moving from the general history of the Order of Saint John to the specific tragedy of the Fall of Constantinople in 1453. It contrasts the heroic defense by the Greeks and Italians with the absence of the dedicatee's own knightly order, which the author suggests could have saved the city.</meta>

-># D E D I C A T I O N.<-

He builds a bridge of barrels across the stream <note>This refers to the bridge built by Sultan Mehmed II across the Golden Horn to facilitate the final assault on the city's walls.</note>,
And gives the bands on the water’s edge free rein
To storm, and all at once set upon Pera <note>Pera, also known as Galata, was the Genoese colony across the Golden Horn from the main city of Constantinople.</note>.
Now God’s knighthood cannot prevent this breach,
Where Palaiologos <note>Constantine XI Palaiologos, the final Byzantine Emperor.</note> and the hero Giustiniani <note>Giovanni Giustiniani Longo, the Genoese commander who led the defense of the land walls.</note>,
Stand so undaunted at the front, like two lions,
Who glare across at Mahomet’s <note>Sultan Mehmed II, the conqueror of the city.</note> brood with wide eyes,
Terrifying them with their claws,
Until Giustiniani, wounded, leaves his post
To staunch the blood, though the Emperor advises against it <note>Historical accounts suggest Giustiniani’s withdrawal due to injury caused a fatal panic among the defenders.</note>.
Through this, the courage of the knighthood begins to sink.
Thus the arch-enemy bursts in, just as a spring tide
Fears neither pilings nor dike nor breakwater,
And roars across the length and breadth of the land,
Turning the flat fields everywhere into a vast sea.
If Prince **M A U R I T S** <note>Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen (1604–1679), the Dutch prince and Grand Master of the Order of Saint John in Brandenburg to whom this work is dedicated.</note> and his German Knightly Order
Had been summoned to pull the broadsword [which they gird
At their side, only to honor the name of God
And the repentant messenger Saint John <note>John the Baptist, the patron saint of the Order.</note>, against
That cruel and circumcised <note>original: "voorhuitloos" (literally: "foreskin-less"). A 17th-century reference to the Islamic practice of circumcision.</note> band]
From its sheath: Mecca’s bands <note>The Ottoman forces.</note> would never have plunged
That imperial palace into a pit of endless misery,
Down to the very ground; and Constantine’s City might,
Raising its head far more proudly than it ever stood,
Hear the Mother Church of Sophia <note>The Hagia Sophia (Holy Wisdom), then the largest cathedral in the Christian world.</note>
Even today, in the name of Jesus and Mary,
Resound with a heavenly choral song;
Where now the curse of the cross <note>original: "kruisvloeck". The author uses this term to describe the Islamic call to prayer or the presence of the Ottoman crescent replacing the Christian cross.</note> roars, and nothing but the stench of slander,
Instead of the censer and the scents of sweet incense,
Swings toward heaven—to be mourned by God’s angels.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The last Constantine gives up the ghost in the midst of that crush,
And unrecognized <note>According to legend, the Emperor Constantine XI died fighting in the streets and his body was never officially identified.</note>. The rage continues its course.
Constantinople seems a tragic stage full of plagues.
After a siege, a storm of fifty days,
She is overwhelmed, exactly on the High Feast of Pentecost <note>Constantinople fell on Tuesday, May 29, 1453, shortly after Pentecost.</note>.
That teaches one to deny how God the Holy Spirit

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the fall of Constantinople and the death of the last Byzantine Emperor, Constantine XI. It shifts from the historical tragedy of 1453 to the contemporary threat posed by the Ottoman Empire to Central Europe (Austria, Transylvania, and Germany), serving as an exhortation to the dedicatee, Johan Maurits, to recognize the ongoing danger to Christendom.</meta>

-># D E D I C A T I O N<-

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Simultaneously shining forth from the Father and the Son.* <note>original: "Al teffens voortstraelt uit den Vader en den Zoone" - A reference to the *filioque* clause in the Nicene Creed, a theological point of contention between the Western and Eastern Churches that the author suggests may have played a role in the city's divine abandonment.</note>
*Now the Emperor lies, with his purple staff and crown,*
*Wallowing in blood, trodden in bloody dust.*
*No respect is shown for churches, nor monasteries, choir nor court,*
*Consecrated or unconsecrated. That desecration, that violation*
*Ignites a despair that seeks in vain to defend itself.*
*The Bosphorus raises its chill, and foams into the sea,*
*Smoking with frothing blood. Thus howls with heartache*
*A woman in the throes of labor, like this city in mourning.*
*She cries out: let no one trust too much in arrogant fortune.*
*A city that for a thousand years, and a hundred, and even more,*
*After her origin, stood, and rivaled Rome in honor,*
*Like a boundary pillar between Asia and Europe,*
*Now falls as prey to vengeance, and laments without hope.*
*The Emperor's head is thrust upon a spear in mockery,*
*Also the crucifix, with a shout: "This is the Christians' God."*
*The Empress, together with her son and daughter,*
*Is dragged by her hair to the court banquet, and without shame*
*Violated, in the sight of that bloodhound Mehmed <note>original: "Mahomet" - Sultan Mehmed II (1432–1481), the Ottoman ruler who conquered Constantinople.</note>,*
*Then put to the saber, amid the cheers of the murderous feast.*
*The remnants of the city are led away into slavery.*
*Thus did this ancient dominion sink into blood,*
*Where the old wisdom of the Greeks suffers shipwreck.*
*It is in vain to fight, when God fights against you.*

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*The scourge of the Savior, having climbed onto the high throne,*
*Wins Trebizond <note>original: "Trapezonte" - The Empire of Trebizond, a Byzantine successor state on the Black Sea, which fell to the Ottomans in 1461.</note>, and boldly fastens two empires*
*And twelve kingdoms, like garnets, to his crown.*
*Had Austria <note>original: "Oostenrÿck"</note> not continually offered him resistance,*
*The Turkish army would have long since burst through the borders of the Cross,*
*Leading the Emperor, with the procession of the seven Prince-Electors <note>original: "Keurevorsten" - The high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who held the right to elect the Emperor.</note>,*
*In a mocking triumph through the cities.*
*Now one hears how he madly stirs trumpet and drum,*
*And pierces through the mountains and forests of Transylvania.*
*If Christendom <note>original: "Christenrÿck"</note> does not wake up, he will soon drift into Germany.*

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page depicts the brutal aftermath of the Fall of Constantinople, describing the death of the Emperor and the desecration of the city, before warning that the Ottoman threat now moves toward Austria and Germany.</summary>
<keywords>Constantinople, Mehmed II, Byzantine Empire, Trebizond, Holy Roman Empire, Prince-Electors, Transylvania, Ottoman-Habsburg wars, martyrdom</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication, shifting from the historical fall of Constantinople (1453) to the contemporary threat posed by the Ottoman Empire in the mid-17th century. The author appeals to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, highlighting his past military and administrative successes in Brazil ("another world") as proof of his fitness to defend Christendom now.</meta>

-># D E D I C A T I O N<-

&nbsp;

*Down the imperial stream it flows, gathering strength in truth, not just in appearance,*
*The omen, as his horse goes to race in the Rhine* <note>The Rhine is personified here as the "Imperial Stream" (*rijxstroom*), the heart of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>
*Before Cologne, daughter of the church, mother of heroes;*
*Then the Cologne Cathedral becomes a stable, to provide fodder* <note>original: "Keulsche Dom een paerdestal" — A recurring apocalyptic image in European literature where the conversion of a great cathedral into a stable symbolizes the ultimate desecration by "barbarian" invaders.</note>
*To feed his horse, which lays waste to so much land,*
*And with bloodied hoof tramples the golden harvest,*
*The sweat of the farmers, and Christian subjects,*
*Who, having fled to the mountains, behold this, wet with tears,*
*And turn their eyes toward heaven, west and east,*
*To see if God might raise up a Maccabee, to comfort the whole empire.* <note>original: "Machabeeu" — Referring to the Maccabees, the Jewish rebel army who liberated Judea from the Seleucid Empire; a common 17th-century metaphor for a divinely-appointed military liberator.</note>
Come now, MAURITIUS, let the war trumpet be sounded.
Do not allow this curse to henceforth deprive us of more cities
Through its own bravery.
Another world has long known how to speak of your leadership, <note>A reference to Johan Maurits’ time as Governor-General of Dutch Brazil (1637–1644).</note>
Where your virtue tamed the cannibals,
And savages, wild by nature, devourers of strangers,
Were driven into wildernesses, and roaring deserts;
Though they cunningly tipped their arrows with poison,
And resembled no humans but rather wild beasts,
Cast in the nest, and deprived of the use of reason.
Wake up, wake up, and call your knighthood together,
The current crisis demands a general relief.
The island of Crete, with its hundred cities, is being emptied. <note>Refers to the Cretan War (1645–1669), specifically the brutal Siege of Candia by the Ottomans.</note>
The Sea-Lion of Saint Mark and never-violated Venice <note>The Lion of Saint Mark is the symbol of the Republic of Venice.</note>
Can hardly resist, and Hungary cries
For urgent help in the fire; which grazes down the corn-land.
Summon your Order of the Cross, to fight for the faith <note>Johan Maurits was a high-ranking member (and later Master) of the Order of Saint John (the Johanniter Order), a Protestant chivalric order.</note>
In the shadow of God’s standard. ALEXANDER the Seventh <note>Pope Alexander VII (reigned 1655–1667), who sought to form a Christian league against the Turks.</note>
Boldly offers you his hand from afar, out of Castel Sant'Angelo. <note>original: "Engleborgh" — The papal fortress in Rome.</note>
The head of Austria, armed with his concern, <note>Refers to the Holy Roman Emperor (Leopold I), a Habsburg.</note>
Laments that people linger, and that he, the guardian of the Germans,
Is set with traps from behind, instead of receiving support;
As if the faith, the faith of the Cross, found no loyalty,
No love on earth, and as if one wished Christ...

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Not</meta>

<summary>The author calls upon Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen to lead a defense against the Ottoman Empire, citing his previous "civilizing" missions in Brazil and the dire threats currently facing Crete, Venice, and Hungary.</summary>
<keywords>Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen, Cologne, Rhine, Brazil, Cannibals, Crete, Venice, Hungary, Pope Alexander VII, Order of Saint John, Holy Roman Empire</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Johan Maurits van Nassau-Siegen. Having established the Ottoman threat in the previous section, Vondel now frames Johan Maurits as a classical and Christian hero—a modern Hercules or Perseus—destined to liberate Europe and defend the Holy Roman Empire. The poem concludes with a nod to his role as governor in Cleves under the Elector of Brandenburg.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N

&nbsp;

*Not like the Heathen, with a hellish spear from the front,*
*But pierced straight through the heart, while fixed upon the cross.* <note>Vondel likely contrasts the spear that pierced Christ's side with the "hellish" weapons of the Ottoman Turks, or suggests that the hero's heart is protected by his devotion to the Cross.</note>
*Prevent that parricide, O hero, you who never*
*Rest from doing good. Protect the Emperor, your lord,*
*And in the Emperor all of Europe, and so many people,*
*Too faint and exhausted to offer resistance.*
*If Perseus protected a maiden, bound to the rock,*
*From the sea monster; if Hercules with his club*
*Could crush a cattle-thief, a snake, a lion, a beast:*
*How many snakes, how many robbers, how many giants,*
*Will you destroy on this noble military campaign?*
*How many maidens, brought naked and bare to the market,*
*And auctioned off, like cattle, for the pleasure of tyrants:*
*How many slaves, banished to the pirate galley,*
*Will be delivered by the sword that hangs at your side,*
*To cut down all that is hideous and cruel,*
*And to drag such a hell-hound, possessed by blasphemy,*
*Into the light of day, shackled in his chains.*
*I hear God’s knighthood, like streams through a sluice,*
*Bursting out, and following you into the field upon your cross;* <note>original: "uw kruis" — Referring to the white cross of the Order of Saint John (the Johanniter Order) which Johan Maurits wore as a high-ranking member.</note>
*It seems to me I see the heart of the Ottomans pounding,*
*And, driven from the Danube all the way to Constantinople,*
*Looking back, as if weary of war, he seeks to hide himself*
*At the grave of his prophet—that scourge of the Christian world—* <note>original: "grave of his prophet" — Referring to Muhammad; the "scourge" is a common 17th-century epithet for the Ottoman Empire's expansion into Europe.</note>
*Out of fear for the Emperor’s eagles,*
*Ready to carry the half-moon upon his shield.* <note>The "Emperor's eagles" represent the Holy Roman Empire; the "half-moon" or crescent is the symbol of the Ottoman Empire.</note>
*Thus may Nassau be seen long honored in the yoke of the cross.*
*Thus may your breast-cross shine beautifully in the Order of Saint John.*
*Thus my muse desires to lead your triumphal chariot,*
*And to welcome you to the court of Cleves with her songs,* <note>Cleves (Kleef) was where Johan Maurits resided as governor for the Elector of Brandenburg.</note>
*To the praise of* **FREDERICK** *and* **LOUISE**, *great in name,* <note>Frederick William, the "Great Elector" of Brandenburg, and his wife Louise Henriette of Nassau.</note>
*While two worlds speak of your fame.* <note>The "two worlds" refer to Europe and the Americas (specifically Dutch Brazil), where Johan Maurits had previously served.</note>

->Your Princely Grace's humble servant<-

->**J. v. V O N D E L.**<-

<summary>In this concluding poetic dedication, Joost van den Vondel exhorts Johan Maurits to lead a Christian crusade against the Ottoman Empire, comparing his future victories to the labors of Hercules and Perseus. The poem highlights Johan Maurits' membership in the Order of Saint John and his service to the Elector of Brandenburg in Cleves.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page marks the transition from the formal dedication to Johan Maurits of Nassau-Siegen to the beginning of the epic poem itself. The previous page concluded the panegyric regarding the Turkish threat and the hero's virtues; here, Vondel begins "John the Messenger of Repentance," a six-book religious epic detailing the life and mission of John the Baptist.</meta>

# J O H N
## THE MESSENGER OF REPENTANCE.

-> Contained in *six books*. <-

### The first book.

#### A R G U M E N T.

> *The Father of grace beholds from heaven’s throne*
> *The miseries of the circumcised and the uncircumcised Heathen,* <note>Referring to the Jews (the circumcised) and the Gentiles (the uncircumcised).</note>
> *And dispatches Gabriel, herald of the highest crown,*
> *To His messenger of repentance, John, down below,*
> *To call him, in His name, out of the lonely wilderness,*
> *That he might prepare a path for the Son.*
> *The trumpet of salvation spreads word that He is at hand.*
> *The teacher Nicodemus and counselor Joseph both*
> *Enter into secret conversation. People from everywhere*
> *Gather in the desert to hear the dweller of the wilderness,*
> *Who steps forward, where he shall reveal to each*
> *How great a salvation is ordained for them in the Savior.*
> *John takes his stand, like a noble teacher.*
> *All ears listen to Christ’s messenger of repentance.*

---

<margin>Proposition of the subject of this intended work.</margin>
It is my pleasure, through the son of the priest Zechariah,
The stern messenger of repentance, to pave the highway for the Messiah,
High priest, king, and true prophet,
To wash the stain of Adam's apple-bite, <note>original: "Adams appelbeet" — A poetic reference to the Original Sin committed in the Garden of Eden.</note>
Acquired in paradise, out of the souls
Who kneel down repentantly before God's holy Lamb.
It is my pleasure to sing of the hero who, so great
Before God and angels, shed his pure blood,

<summary>This page introduces the epic poem "Joannes de Boetgezant," providing a verse summary of Book One and beginning the formal invocation, which focuses on John the Baptist's role in preparing the way for Christ.</summary>
<keywords>John the Baptist, Messenger of Repentance, Gabriel, Zechariah, Messiah, Original Sin, Nicodemus, Joseph of Arimathea</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the epic poem "John the Messenger of Repentance." The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing John as a hero who shed his pure blood for God. This page explains the reason for his martyrdom—his confrontation with King Herod—and contains the poet's formal invocation to the heavenly muses (the angels) to guide his writing.</meta>

# JOANNES.

Because he, without bending justice out of favor or hate,
Dared to confront the lustful king <note>Referring to Herod Antipas.</note> to his face,
Convicting him of foul incest, committed in his brother's bed. <note>Herod Antipas married Herodias, the wife of his half-brother Philip, while Philip was still living.</note>
Thus from the night of the prison, the day of the age of salvation broke forth.
The court shuts its eyes to the rising of God's brilliance,
And stops its ears to the truth’s trumpet of repentance.

My muse, innocent and without guilt,
Shall be seen waging war in the vanguard of the Messiah,
With watching, and prayers, and fasting, and patience,
And truth; not armed with fire, like Elijah, <note>In the Bible, the prophet Elijah called down fire from heaven; John the Baptist is often seen as a "new Elijah" who fought with spiritual weapons instead.</note>
The battlefield on earth shall cover a day's journey of ground,
From Zechariah's house to across the Jordan of the land,
Where the royal Machaerus, <note>The desert fortress where John the Baptist was imprisoned and eventually beheaded.</note> having reached the stars,
Casts the shadow of its peak into the dead waves.

<margin>Invocation of the heavenly muses, the choirs of angels.</margin>
You angel choirs, who on high, from sphere to sphere,
Honor the Lamb, who leads the row of virgins in dance,
Who crown the faithful bridegroom of pure souls
Through the new song and unsearchable tones;
Guide my heavenly-heroic melody with your song.
I know no mountain of song <note>A reference to Mount Parnassus, the traditional home of the Greek Muses.</note> but the heavenly paradise,
Where, from the throne of God and the Lamb, through a thousand veins,
The living water, to the rustle of palm leaves,
Comes springing up, clear and pure as crystal.
That is my horse-spring, <note>original: "paerdebron" — This refers to Hippocrene, the spring on Mount Helicon struck open by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus. The poet is claiming a Christian source of inspiration rather than a pagan one.</note> my forest, and waterfall,
From which the kings and God's anointed drank.
If your heavenly spring refreshes me, the shadows,
Deserts, and caves of John, and his prison, shall
Change into light and paradise. Then my lowly
Desert-poem, about a desert-lover, shall stride
As proudly as the ancients ever did over a conqueror
Of Troy, or Latium: <note>Referring to the epic heroes Achilles (Troy) and Aeneas (Latium/Rome).</note> if one wisely perceives
How God humbles the high, and exalts the low so high.
Let whoever wishes choose the noblest of all subjects:
Whoever chooses what God chooses has best hit the mark.

<summary>The poet describes John the Baptist's courageous confrontation with King Herod over his incestuous marriage and invokes the heavenly choirs of angels to inspire his epic poem, favoring Christian revelation over classical Greek mythology.</summary>
<keywords>John the Baptist, Herod Antipas, Herodias, Elijah, Machaerus, Jordan River, Angel choirs, Parnassus, Hippocrene, original sin, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening of the epic poem. The previous page concluded the poet’s invocation to the angelic choirs, seeking their guidance to tell the story of John the Baptist. On this page, the poet turns to dedicate his work to a contemporary religious figure, Anna Bruining, before setting the historical and political scene of the Holy Land under Roman occupation at the time of John’s birth.</meta>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

<margin>To Anna Bruining at the Bethlehem convent in Brussels.</margin>
And you, O **ANNA**, who in the Dutch "Bethlehem," <note>Anna Bruining was a nun at the Bethlehem convent of the Augustinian Canonesses in Brussels. The poet compares her cloistered life to the birthplace of Christ.</note>
Followed the trumpet of repentance and the divine voice of John
In poverty, far removed from all that is vain,
Receive, if it pleases you, alongside your faithful choirs,
My song, which strives for no prize of worldly honor,
And strengthen me with your prayers when you go to the choir,
So that we both, lifted high above the earthly realm,
May behold God’s messenger <note>John the Baptist.</note> standing with God in the light.

<margin>Introduction and beginning of the corrupted state of affairs in the church and the world, in Judea, Galilee, and neighboring lands.</margin>
The Jewish land was now the slave of the uncircumcised; <note>A reference to the Roman occupation, as the Romans did not practice circumcision.</note>
Jerusalem, once a warlike heroine, was now the slave of Rome.
Tiberius, the descendant of Augustus, triumphed.
Judea, under the yoke of the Roman Emperor, honored
Pilate, who was installed as governor by Tiberius.
Herod <note>Herod Antipas.</note> laid down the law from his throne over all Galilee,
Philip ruled over Iturea and the harsh Trachonites,
While Lysanias trod upon Abilene and helped to suppress all virtue.
Thus things went in the worldly realm: while the high priesthood was in strife,
Now divided between Annas, the father-in-law,
And his son-in-law Caiaphas, who each took their turn in power.
The staff and the censer <note>Symbols of royal and priestly authority.</note> were no longer bound
To Judah’s lion-tribe, nor to Aaron’s tribe and blood.
The lust for gold and power insolently kicks
The miter and the crown, the holy and the unholy alike.
The book of law is crumpled; loyalty is nowhere safe.

<margin>God looks down, takes pity on the human race, and remembers His promise.</margin>
In that corrupt state, the Most High looked down from the throne
Of heaven, and remembered the kingdom of David, and the Son
Promised from eternity, who—still quietly here on earth—
Had not yet revealed Himself in His full radiance and day;
Because the morning star, the herald of the sun,
The true light of salvation and source of the heavenly lights,
Had to rise first. Just as the far-seeing heroes <note>The prophets of the Old Testament.</note>
Driven by the Spirit, had for so many centuries foretold
To the fathers, the kings, and the priestly tribe,
Longing, each competing with the other, for Him to appear,

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<summary>The poet dedicates the work to Anna Bruining and describes the bleak political and religious landscape of Judea under Roman rule and corrupt leadership, setting the stage for God's intervention.</summary>
<keywords>Anna Bruining, Roman Occupation, Tiberius, Pontius Pilate, Herod Antipas, Annas and Caiaphas, corruption, prophecy, morning star</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues from the description of the political and religious state of the Holy Land under Roman rule and the divided high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas. Here, the poet transitions from the historical setting to the divine plan, describing how God decides the time has come for the "Word made flesh" to be revealed and for John the Baptist to begin his mission.</meta>

# JOANNES

To bring prophecy, promise, and the law written in stone <note>A reference to the Ten Commandments given to Moses on Mount Sinai.</note>
Into full force, and to give them their true color:
Just as a draftsman completes a planned image
Until the work is indistinguishable from life.
Everyone sighed for a power that, in these final times,
Would once and for all release and free them from the Heathen yoke: <note>The "Heathen yoke" refers to the Roman occupation mentioned on the previous page.</note>
Though they wandered in their expectation of a leader:
For they were promised no worldly kingdom, but a heavenly one,
And a redeemer for so many lost souls
As formerly fell into Adam’s debt of death when Adam fell: <note>Referring to the concept of Original Sin and the fall of man in Genesis.</note>
That was a dominion defined by neither borders nor centuries,
The most beautiful field in which my poetic spirit takes breath.

<margin>He concludes that Christ, born long ago, should fully reveal himself, and that John, the Messenger of Repentance, should prepare the way for him.</margin>
Then the Father of Grace spoke, moved in His heart
By human miseries, out of pure compassion:
“My only heir, and chosen son,
The glory of my kingdom, and eternal rich crown,
The Word made flesh <note>The "menschgeworden Woort" refers to Jesus Christ (the Logos).</note> remains hidden, faint of glow and luster,
Known by few, still low, quiet, and obscured
By a span of years: it is time, and more than time,
That he should come forward, and finally devote himself
To the soul-saving office, for the salvation of grieving mortals,
Which has been laid upon his shoulders: let all that wishes to, give way
And waver in its loyalty: let our word stand eternally.
Let the intended salvation begin with heaven’s envoy, <note>John the Baptist.</note>
Appointed by the heavenly council while yet in his mother’s body
To prepare the entrance for our beloved son
At the gate of the kingdom that he must possess.”
Thus speaking, and ignited by unquenchable fervor
To strengthen his promises, long confirmed by oath,
And to perfectly work out the eternal salvation of mankind,
He quickly summons Gabriel, who, in a garment of starlight,
Keeps himself constantly ready at the high command.

<margin>Archangel Gabriel is [addressed]</margin>
“Archangel,” He says, “who previously to both the cousins <note>The "two cousins" are Mary (mother of Jesus) and Elizabeth (mother of John the Baptist), whom Gabriel visited to announce their pregnancies.</note>
Promised each her fruit, and were never lacking in your duties,

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page describes God the Father’s decision to reveal Jesus Christ to the world and fulfill the ancient prophecies. He summons the Archangel Gabriel to initiate the mission of John the Baptist, who is tasked with preparing the way for the Savior.</summary>

<keywords>Prophecy, Roman occupation, Adam's fall, Original Sin, Jesus Christ, The Word made flesh, God the Father, Archangel Gabriel, John the Baptist, salvation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the divine narrative from Page 16, where God the Father decides to end the "hidden years" of Jesus. The text transitions from God's decree to the specific mission of John the Baptist and the descent of the Archangel Gabriel to Earth.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK

<sig>C</sig>
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<margin>John is summoned.</margin>
Do not neglect the task assigned to you by heaven;
Go toward the world: descend directly,
Where you now faithfully fulfill your mission for the second time,
And the waters of the Jordan flow around the holy landscape,
Long protected by David when he shone upon the earth.
You shall urge the desert-dweller <note>Referring to John the Baptist, who lived an ascetic life in the wilderness.</note> in his rocky cave
To enter the office assigned to him,
As the Spirit teaches him; and, instilling discipline and morality
In mankind, he must fearlessly testify of the Hero <note>Jesus Christ.</note>
Who comes to deliver them from the inherited power of the abyss. <note>The "inherited power of the abyss" refers to Original Sin and the dominion of Satan.</note>
The night of the Law <note>The "night of the law" refers to the Old Testament era, which the poet suggests is a period of shadow compared to the coming "light" of the Gospel.</note> fades, and demands that, without delay,
He immediately behold—before the stronger light begins to shine—
The Sun of Equity and of Righteousness. <note>A traditional metaphor for Christ, derived from the Book of Malachi.</note>
Announce this by order of the majesty of heaven.

<margin>Gabriel descends into the world.</margin>
So spoke the Almighty, and the Archangel prepares
Himself to race, and unfolds—as beautiful as phoenix feathers—
His wings, dappled with heavenly blue, and gold,
And purple, in the light where the Godhead dwells.
One sees the colors change and shift,
Like the rainbow, or beautiful peacock feathers
In the light of the sun that stands directly opposite.
Ready for his journey, he raises himself in flight and strikes
His wings together three times, so that the ranks
Of angels look back, and follow his flight
With their eyes; while the flyer descends,
And swings from sphere to sphere, and as he drops, catches
Jerusalem in his eye, which lifts its crowned peaks
Toward heaven from the surrounding dunes,
By which the royal city seemed encircled.
Then he followed the path toward the great river <note>The River Jordan.</note>
That showed him the desert of Quarantania, <note>The desert of Quarantania (Mount Quarantania) is traditionally identified as the location of the Temptation of Christ.</note> not rich in greenery or foliage.
Here the Archangel hung upon his wings, then swept forward
Toward the cave of the desert-dweller: just like an eagle,
<margin>A Comparison</margin>
Who from the heights finally catches sight of a spring,

<meta>This page serves as an introduction to the divine intervention that transitions the narrative from the Old Testament Law to the arrival of Christ. It establishes the theological context of the "Word made flesh" and the specific mission of John the Baptist as the precursor.</meta>

# JOANNES

->John<-

To empower the prophecy, the promise, and the Law—
Once written in stone—and to give them their true hue;
Just as an artist completes a planned portrait
Until the work is indistinguishable from life itself.
Each soul sighed for a power that, in the final days,
Would one day release and liberate them from the heathen yoke <note>Refers to the Roman occupation of Judea and the spiritual oppression of paganism.</note>:
Yet they strayed in their expectation of a leader,
For no worldly kingdom, but a heavenly one, had been promised to them,
And a redeemer of so many lost souls—
As many as had fallen into the debt of death <term>Original: "dootſchult." The theological concept of the inherited guilt of Original Sin from Adam.</term> when Adam first fell.
This was a dominion bounded by neither border nor era,
The fairest field in which my poetic passion draws breath.

<margin>He concludes that Christ, born long ago, reveals himself perfectly, and John, the Messenger of Repentance <note>John the Baptist.</note>, prepares the way for him.</margin>
Then spoke the Father of Grace, moved in His heart
By human misery and out of pure compassion:
"My only heir and chosen Son,
The glory of My realm and eternal, rich crown—
The Word made flesh <note>Original: "menſchgeworden Woort." A reference to the Incarnation of Jesus.</note>—remains hidden, His brilliance and luster dimmed,
Known by only a few, humble, still, and obscure
For a span of years. It is time—more than time—
That He comes forward and at last devotes Himself
To the saving office, laid upon Him for the salvation
Of sorrowful mortals. Let all that will succumb
And waver in its loyalty: Our word shall stand eternal.
The intended salvation shall begin with heaven's messenger,
Destined by heavenly counsel while yet in his mother’s womb
To prepare the entrance for Our dear Son
To the gates of the kingdom that He must possess."
So He spoke, and, ignited by unquenchable fervor
To strengthen His promises, long confirmed by oath,
And to perfectly achieve the eternal salvation of mankind,
He quickly summons Gabriel, who, in his starlight raiment,
Holds himself constantly ready at the high command.

<margin>Archangel Gabriel is sent to...</margin>
"Archangel," He says, "who previously promised
To both cousins <note>Mary and Elizabeth, the mothers of Jesus and John the Baptist.</note> their own fruit, and never in your duties...

Go to the world: descend directly down,

Where you now for the second time faithfully fulfill your embassy,

Long protected by David, when he shone upon the earth.

To enter into the office assigned to him,
As the Spirit teaches him; and, impressing discipline and morals
Upon mankind, to testify undaunted of the Hero,
Who comes to deliver them from the ancestral power of the abyss <note>Refers to the hereditary grip of Original Sin and the power of Hell over humanity since the Fall of Adam.</note>.
The night of the Law <note>The era of the Old Testament Mosaic Law, which the poet views as a "shadowy" or "night" period compared to the "light" of Christ's Gospel.</note> vanishes, and demands that, without delay,
He immediately behold—before the stronger light comes to shine—
The Sun of fairness and of righteousness.
Announce this by command of heaven's majesty.

So spoke the Almighty; and the archangel, ready to race,
Prepares himself, and unfolds—as beautiful as phoenix feathers—
His wings, variegated with heavenly blue, and gold,
And purple, in the light where the Godhead dwells.
One sees the colors change and shift,
Like the rainbow, or beautiful peacock feathers
In the light of the sun, when it stands directly opposite.
Ready for travel in his flight, he rises, and beats
His feathers together three times, so that the ranks
Of angels look back, and accompany his flight
With their gaze; while the flier descends,
And sways from circle to circle, and during the descent catches
Jerusalem in his eye, which lifts its crowned peaks
Toward heaven from the surrounding dunes,
By which the royal city seemed encircled.

Pointed him to the wild Quarente <term>The Wilderness of Quarantania, traditionally associated with the site of Jesus's temptation and John's preaching.</term>, not rich in greenery or foliage.
Here the archangel hovered on his feathers, then glided down

Upon the desert-dweller's cave: just like an eagle,
Who from the heights finally perceives a spring,

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the divine narrative where the Almighty Father, moved by the plight of humanity, formally commissions the transition from the Old Law to the New Covenant. Having previously prepared the Archangel Gabriel to descend, the text now explains the theological purpose behind John the Baptist’s mission as the precursor to Christ.</meta>

# JOHN

<note>Catchword: The</note>

To bring prophecy, promise, and the law written in stone
Into force, and to give them their true color:
Just as a draftsman completes the intended image
Until the work does not differ from life itself.
Everyone sighed for a power that, in the latter days,
Would once release and deliver them from the Heathen yoke <note>A reference to the Roman occupation of Judea; many contemporary Jews expected a political or military Messiah.</note>:
Although they wandered in their expectation of a leader:
For they were promised no worldly kingdom, but a heavenly one,
And a savior for so many lost souls,
As many as fell long ago in Adam’s debt of death <note>The theological concept of Original Sin inherited from the Fall of Adam.</note>, when Adam fell:
That was a dominion bounded by neither border nor age,
The fairest field in which my poetic spirit <note>original: "zangluſt"; the poet’s drive to sing or write this epic.</note> breathes.

<margin>
He decides that Christ, having long since been born, shall fully reveal himself, and John, the Messenger of Penance, shall prepare the way for him.
</margin>
Then spoke the Father of Grace, moved in His heart
By human miseries, out of pure compassion:
My only heir, and chosen Son,
The glory of my kingdom, and eternal, rich crown,
The Word made flesh <note>original: "menſchgeworden Woort"</note>, hides, faint of brilliance and luster,
Known by few, low and still and obscure,
For a series of years. It becomes time, and more than time,
That he should come forward, and once fulfill <note>literally "acquit himself of"</note>
The salvific office, for the salvation of sorrowful mortals,
Laid upon his shoulders. Let all that will give way,
And falter in its loyalty: our word holds fast forever.
The intended salvation begins with heaven’s envoy,
Appointed by heavenly counsel while still in his mother’s womb
To prepare the entry for our dear Son
At the gate of the kingdom that he must possess.
Speaking thus, and ignited by an unquenchable glow,
To strengthen His promises, long confirmed by oath,
And to perfectly work out mankind’s eternal salvation,
He quickly summons Gabriel, who, in his starlight garment,
Constantly holds himself ready for the high command.

# JOANNES

To put prophecy, promise, and the law, written in stone,
Into power, and to give them their true color:
Just as a draftsman completes the intended image,
Until the work does not differ from life itself.
Everyone sighed for a power that, in the latter times,
Would once release and free them from the Heathen yoke <note>A reference to the Roman occupation of Judea, which many contemporary Jews hoped a Messiah would overthrow militarily.</note>:
Although they wandered in their expectation of a leader:
For no worldly kingdom but a heavenly one was promised to them,
And a savior of as many lost souls,
As fell in Adam's debt of death, when Adam fell:
That was a dominion, bounded by neither border nor age,
The most beautiful field in which my poetic soul breathes.

<margin>He concludes that Christ, born long ago, shall fully reveal himself, and John, the Messenger of Repentance, prepares the way for him.</margin>
Then the Father of Grace spoke, moved in his heart
With human misery, out of pure compassion:
"My only heir, and chosen son,
The glory of my kingdom, and eternal rich crown,
The Word-made-flesh <note>original: "menschgeworden Woort"; the theological concept of the Incarnation.</note>, hides, faint of brilliance and luster,
Known by few, still low and quiet and dark,
For a row of years: it is time, and more than time
That he comes forward, and fulfills
The salutary office, for the salvation of grieving mortals,
Laid upon his shoulders. Let all that will fail,
And waver in its loyalty: our word stands eternal.
The intended salvation begins with heaven’s messenger <note>Referring here to John the Baptist as the precursor.</note>,
Destined by heaven’s counsel while in his mother’s body
To prepare the entry for our dear son
At the gate of the kingdom that he must possess."
Speaking so, and kindled by unquenchable fire,
To strengthen his promises, long confirmed by oath,
And to fully work out humanity's eternal salvation,
He quickly summons Gabriel, who, in a garment of starlight,
Keeps himself constantly ready at the high command.

<margin>Archangel Gabriel is addressed</margin>
"Archangel," says he, "who previously promised both cousins <note>Mary and Elizabeth, the mothers of Jesus and John the Baptist respectively.</note>
Each her fruit, and never failed in your duties—"

The most beautiful field, in which my poetic passion breathes.

### HET EERSTE BOECK.

Den last verzuimde, u van den hemel opgeleght;
Ga naer de weerelt toe: stijgh neder regelrecht,
Daer gy nu tweewerf trou bekleede uw afgezantschap,
En 't water der Jordaene omspoelt het heiligh lantschap,
Van David langh beschut, toen hy op d'aerde blonck.
<margin>Concerning John</margin>
Gy zult den woestynier <note>original: "woestynier"; referring to John the Baptist living as a hermit in the wilderness.</note>, in zijne rotspelonck,
Aenmaenen in het ampt, hem toegekeurt, te treden,
Gelijck de geest hem leert, en, 't menschdom tucht en zeden
Inschærpende, onvertzaeght te tuigen van den helt,
Die hen verlossen komt van 's afgronts erfgewelt.
De nacht der wet <note>A common theological metaphor where the Mosaic Law is the "night" or "shadow" that must fade before the "daylight" of the Gospel.</note> verdwijnt, en eischt, dat, zonder draelen,
Hy daetlijck aenblicke, eer het stercker licht koom' straelen,
De zon van billijckheit en van rechtvaerdigheit.
Verkuntschap dit uit last van 's hemels majesteit.
<margin>Gabriel descends into the world.</margin>
Zoo sprack d'almaghtige, en d'aertsengel, om te rennen,
Bereit zich, en ontvout, zoo schoon als fenixpennen,
Zijn vleugels, geschakeert van hemelsch blaeu, en gout,
En purper, in het licht daer zich de godtheit houdt.
Men ziet de verwen zich verandren en schakeeren,
Gelijck de regenboogh, of schoone paeuweveêren,
In 't licht der zonne, die recht tegens over staet.
Reisvaerdigh in zijn vlught verheft hy zich, en slaet
De pennen tegens een wel drywerf, dat de reien
Der englen ommezien, en zijne vlught geleien
Met hun gezicht; terwijl de vlieger nederstijght,
En zwaeit van ronde in ronde, en onder 't daelen krijgt
Jerusalem in 't oogh, dat zijn gekroonde kruinen
Ten hemel opwaert heft uit d'omgelege duinen,
Waer van de koningsstadt in 't ronde omcingelt scheen.

# JOHN.

And gliding down, he slakes his thirst at the sound
Of the fresh waterfall, at the heart-refreshing water.
<margin>John's cave at Quarantania in the wilderness.</margin>
Here the prayer-cave <term>prayer-cave: a natural grotto used by the hermit for solitary worship and meditation</term> crouches low, sought by Gabriel—
A work of wonder, a true chapel of God,
Granted to him by nature for the service of Jacob's God,
Or struck from the rock by the hammer of God's word.
One descends by steps; below it is tightly enclosed,
Both around and within its hollow. From above it draws light
Through a cleft, when the sun comes to shine directly overhead,
Before which the shadows transform and vanish.
A table stands in the east, likewise carved from pure stone.
<margin>John's life and spiritual discipline.</margin>
In the portal, God’s prophet rests his limbs
Upon a mat of rushes, woven by his own hand.
The camel-hair garment clothes him when assailed
By cold and rain, fastened with a leather belt.
Hunger occasionally drives him out into the storm.
Wild honey and the lean locust cover
His table. The spring, trickling from a rock, provides drink.
He counts the hours of life—narrowly limited,
Short and uncertain—measured by the shifting shadows.
He hears no cock-crow, nor the barking of dogs,
But lions roar, yet they are shy and fearful of harming him;
Just like the field-snake, which slithers here with its mouth,
Though sometimes he treads upon its neck with his foot;
While the lions wag their tails and lick him,
As if they are awestruck by the faith of this pious hero <note>original: "vroomen helts"; here referring to John the Baptist as a spiritual champion.</note>.
A lamp hung from the vault of this divine chapel and shone
When he kindled it, and fire was struck from flint
Into tinder of foliage and dry, withered leaves.
Thus he spent his years, keeping watch and praying to God
In solitude; he waited, humble and prepared
To follow at God’s call, wherever the Spirit led him.
Now he pondered the life of Elijah <note>original: "Helias"</note>,
And the words of Isaiah <note>original: "Izaias"</note>, and what Malachi <note>original: "Malachias"</note>...

### THE FIRST BOOK.

So long had he been foretold, and he stood enraptured, transported,
Stiff as a pillar, and mute, like a statue that does not move.

<margin>*Comparison*</margin>
Who can grasp how his senses went wandering
Through all the heavens, and what contemplations
Of God and His Son illuminated the enraptured hero <note>original: "opgetogen helt"; referring to John in a state of spiritual ecstasy.</note>!

<margin>*Comparison*</margin>
Just as in the Chaldean field,
Upon the rushing of the Euphrates, visions appeared
To Ezekiel—each more beautiful than the last—and then vanished.

At last, coming to himself, he cried aloud:
"Hallowed be God's name, and the divine decree.
Blessed is he who appears to the honor of God's name.
Let the Savior come; that is all and the only thing I desire."

<margin>*Gabriel appears.*</margin>
At such a voice, the heavenly envoy appeared,
And stood in this cavern directly before the youth,
Ready as a traveler, and shone with his brilliance
Upon the secluded one: whereupon immediately the gloom
And shadow vanished, and the lamp lost its luster.
The desert-dweller gazed at the angel through and through,
From top to bottom; and the flickering light
That usually shone upon John's own head now seemed
To turn dark, compared to the radiance and light of God’s herald,
To whom the Supreme Authority entrusted this high task.

John gave way at first, when he perceived that majesty;
But the pleasant fragrance of the envoy strengthened
His heart, and spread itself throughout the chapel of God.

<margin>*Gabriel’s message.*</margin>
At last he spoke: "Son, you see Gabriel here,
Who thirty years ago, descending from heaven’s gate,
Announced your birth to your father at the altar.
Take heart, and fear not: for you share by heavenly lot
The same name <note>The angel likely refers to John’s role as a "messenger" (Greek: *angelos*), a title he shares with the heavenly hosts.</note> as us, not unworthily. Jacob’s God
Granted you that title through the mouths of His prophets,
Whom you do not yield to in honor, though you sit low;
No greater man ever came forth from a woman."

John bows his head as he hears these words.

## JOHN.

And swooping down, he slakes his thirst at the clatter
Of the fresh waterfall, at the heart-quickening water.

<margin>John's cave at Quarantania <note>The mountain traditionally identified as the site of the Temptation of Christ.</note> in the wilderness.</margin>
Here the prayer-cave dives deep, sought by Gabriel,
A miracle, a true chapel of God,
In the service of Jacob's God, granted to him by nature,
Or forged from the rock by the hammer of God's word.
One descends by steps. Below it closes tight,
Around and within its belly. From above it draws light
Through a cleft, when the sun shines upon its head,
Before which the shadows transform and vanish.
A table stands in the east, also made of pure stone

<margin>John's life and spiritual practice.</margin>
Hewn. In the portal, God’s prophet rests his limbs
Upon a rush mat, woven with his own hand.
The garment of camel hair clothes the one assaulted
By cold and rain, where the leather belt fastens.
Hunger occasionally drives him out into the storm.
Wild honey and the lean locust cover
The table. The spring provides drink, trickling out of a rock.
He counts the hours of life, narrowly limited,
And short and uncertain, marked by the shadow.
He hears no cock-crow, nor any barking of dogs,
But lions roaring, shy and afraid to wound him,
Like the field snake, which slithers here with its mouth,
Sometimes trodden on the neck by his foot;
While the lions fawningly wag their tails and lick him,
As if they are awestruck by the faith of the pious hero.
A lamp hung from the vault of the chapel of God and shone,
When he lit it, and fire rang out from flintstones
Into the tinder of foliage and dry, withered leaves.
Thus he spent his years in solitude, waking and praying to God,
And waited, humble and ready
To follow God's call, wherever the spirit might lead him.
Now he contemplated the life of Elijah <note>original: "Helias"</note>,
And the word of Isaiah <note>original: "Izaias"</note>, and what Malachi <note>original: "Malachias"</note>

# J O H N.

The roses open up, and blush upon his cheeks,
And, crimson with shame for that praise received from the angel’s mouth,
He modestly denies it with a humble countenance:
But Gabriel continues. You must by no means deny the state
In which the Most High placed you, solely out of His pleasure,
[Even before Elizabeth felt you and came to carry you
With joy beneath her heart, for many months in succession,]
To step before <term>Emmanuel<note>A Hebrew name meaning "God with us," referring here to Jesus Christ.</note></term>,
You were, in the body of your mother, long secluded,
Gifted, and richly, from heaven’s lap, from above
Anointed with heaven’s Spirit, the divinity that drives you.
The divinity does not wish for you to remain any longer indebted
To pave the way for Emmanuel before his coming.
You hear my high command, which is to urge you
To immerse the people, to wash them in the flood,
To bring them to repentance, so that, with evil atoned for,
And new morals introduced, humanity may receive
The great Savior of the world with longing.
Heaven gave the law, engraved in stone, to its people
In the wild wilderness through <term>Amram’s son<note>A reference to Moses, the prophet who received the Ten Commandments (the Old Law) in the desert of Sinai.</note></term>, God’s interpreter:
It is therefore fitting that the new law, written
In the human heart, be given in a desert.
What God predicted also clearly applies to this case.
Oracles foretold how desert and heath and valley
And solitude will in the end flow with water,
The white lilies, having opened, shall bloom magnificently,
The parched region, and infertile heath and air,
Shall outshine the fertile lands with their long-desired fruit.
O chosen one, wake up then, and come forth,
So that a greater brilliance may shine through your own luster.
Thus Gabriel spoke, and the other answered him,
Humbly, and politely, with a timid voice:
<margin>John’s answer.</margin>
Would I even dare to undertake such a deed,
I, who am not even worthy to loosen his shoelace? <note>original: "schoenriem"; a reference to John’s famous biblical declaration of humility (Mark 1:7) regarding his unworthiness compared to the Messiah.</note>

<summary>Gabriel commands John the Baptist to begin his ministry of baptism and repentance in the desert, explaining that just as the Old Law (Moses) was given in the wilderness, the New Law must also begin there. John responds with deep humility, famously declaring himself unworthy to even untie the Savior's sandals.</summary>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

What is nothing compared to something, the hollow naught against the all?
But now that God commands, forgive me from on high. I shall
Obey. Let all that heaven has ordained
Happen in God's name. I wish from this moment to prepare myself
To break forth, like a star before the dew and the dawn,
On hope, and nothing else, if it may please God.
The Most High could well, through an ass's jawbone,
Crush the strength of the Philistine in a thousand men.
<note>John references the biblical story of Samson, who defeated a thousand enemies with only the jawbone of a donkey, signifying that God can achieve great things through humble instruments.</note>

<margin>Gabriel departs, and John prepares himself to come forth.</margin>
So spoke the humble one, and turned his gaze upward,
As God's herald vanished from the hero's sight, and flew
To the place from whence he came, carried on the wing.
John praised God, and fell down to the earth,
And lay there for a while with his face in the dust,
Sunken into himself, swallowed up in the praise
Of the Most High, who came to strengthen the covenant of salvation,
Established with Abraham, and now wished to work this out
Through his servant, who, for seven days long,
And seven nights, strictly prepared himself, to the festive song
Of heavenly choirs, which shone from above into the mountain cleft,
Followed by echo upon echo, from rock and cavern.
That gave the zealot courage to begin the high command,
Faithfully received from the mouth of Gabriel,

Meanwhile let himself be heard in David's kingdoms.
He rested neither night nor day on mountain or tower-top
To fly, and everywhere the silver trumpet
To blow across the land, and all cities and villages,
To awaken the reckless and the misled
From their deep sleep and imagined dream.

### J O H N.

Whether it signified good or evil, and whether one should prevent it,
It did not matter to Pilate whether he supported the Jewish state,
Or let it be shaken, as he sat upon his high throne,
For the honor of Tiberius <note>The Roman Emperor Tiberius (reigned 14–37 AD).</note>, merely holding the governorship
In the kingdom conquered by Pompey <note>Pompey the Great, who brought Judea under Roman influence in 63 BC.</note> with the sword;
He occupied the seat and preserved the Roman authority entrusted to him.

<margin>A conversation between Nicodemus, the legal scholar, and the councilor Joseph of Arimathea regarding these rumors.</margin>
A leader of the Jews, and a head of the Pharisees,
Learned in the Law of Moses, venerable among the Hebrews,
Named Nicodemus, visited Joseph of Arimathea,
A member of the Sanhedrin <note>The supreme council and tribunal of the Jews during the Roman period.</note>, to enter with him into a free
Discussion on this new report
That ran through the cities. This zealous pair
Firmly expected the "comfort of the holy generations" <note>A poetic reference to the coming of the Messiah.</note>,
Yet hardly dared to modestly speak the truth they felt,
Seeking to escape the hatred of the other Pharisees.
And thus Nicodemus began his address to Counselor Joseph,
Where they sat together in a spacious hall, eager for holy things:
"What a rumor runs through the city, and all the streets
And regions of the land? What do they cry out, without end,
Of Zacharias’ son, who has been for so long almost unknown,
Hidden away in a corner on the banks of the Jordan?"

<margin>A Simile</margin>
Fickle fame resembles the changeable moon,
Now full and round, then half, then small, as one sees.
First it rises small, grows, diminishes, and vanishes to nothing.

<margin>John’s birth, and the circumstances surrounding it.</margin>
What a stir it caused throughout these mountains all around,
When Zacharias could not speak at the altar,
And stood mute, because he doubted the miraculous word
Of the spirit <note>The Archangel Gabriel, who appeared to Zacharias in the Temple.</note> who appeared to him, and announced the late birth
Of a coming child, even when his years,
Long grey with age, had reached their peak,
And his Elizabeth, in her childless state,

# THE FIRST BOOK.

And in her time she bore the God-consecrated son,
A pure pearl upon the priestly crown.
The tribe of Aaron <note>The lineage of the Jewish high priesthood, to which John’s father Zacharias belonged.</note> rejoiced at such a rich blessing,
Obtained quite unexpectedly by this God-fearing pair.
They named this dear fruit John <note>original: "Joannes"; the name signifies "The Lord is gracious."</note>, according to the destiny
Of the influence of grace, flowed to them from God:
For the angel Gabriel had given her that name.
What did he not speak of the child, the parents’ joy and life!
How the child’s birth would lead to joy everywhere
For thousands of thousands. How he, faithful to God,
To the praise of the Highest’s name, would shine with holiness,
Shunning all delicacies, drinking neither wine nor strong drink <note>original: "boomſap" – literally tree-sap, referring to fermented fruit drinks other than grape wine, consistent with the Nazarite vow.</note>,
Radiant with the Spirit of Heaven from his mother's womb,
In the end converting those who fear God back to the Godhead;
Also brave and stern, like a second Elijah <note>The Old Testament prophet known for his zeal; John was seen as the fulfillment of the prophecy that Elijah would return before the Messiah.</note>,
Beloved, and also feared, before the face of the Messiah
He would shine forth, and as God's spokesman, dedicate to God
A modest, well-mannered, and God-surrendered people.
Such a high angelic word, decreed according to God's purpose,
Was quickly sealed by the father’s muteness;
Since he doubted whether the Almighty, in the faithfulness
Of His promise, which was so profound, would yield.
Everyone noticed this, those who, before the altar of incense and the threshold,
Waited for him and saw him emerge mute from the temple,
And concluded, at the priest's gesture, that he had been enlightened
By a vision in the sanctuary <note>original: "koorgezicht" – a vision received within the inner choir or holy place of the Temple.</note> from a messenger of heaven:
But this is not all. It lives on many tongues
How the child leapt in the mother’s womb,
And cheered his Lord and God—hitherto only faintly acknowledged—
Upon the greeting of the cousin, also pregnant,
When Joseph's pure bride <note>Mary, who was then pregnant with Jesus.</note> greeted Elizabeth,
And met her within her door in the hill country <note>The mountainous region of Judea where Zacharias and Elizabeth lived.</note>.
Such a great wonder, scarcely heard of or reflected upon,
Vanished like a mist, and remained uninvestigated.
