# JOHN.

<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<margin>The Massacre of the Innocents by Herod the Ascalonite. <note>Herod the Great, called the "Ascalonite" because his family originated from the city of Ascalon.</note></margin>
As soon as Herod sought to protect his right to the crown,
And around Bethlehem ordered the fruit <note>A poetic term for children.</note> under two years
To be slaughtered; so that he, for the safety of the court,
Might strike the innocent heart of the Jewish King,
In the midst of that murder, followed by a thousand curses;
He found the cousins <note>Referring to Jesus and John the Baptist, traditionally considered cousins.</note> in no shops or corners,
No matter how sharply they were sought. In the end, Joseph's son
Appeared now and then, with a sound and a tone,
And then disappeared again: but as if he lay sunken,
Zacharias' son dove into shadows and caves,
And the deadly wilderness. He is neither heard nor seen.
He shuns the company of the people. What this means
Is too high for my understanding. Now all the pious hope
That he, upon this rumor, will one day come forward.
Please, advise and teach me in this case,
How the matter stands <note>original: "Hoe 't leght gefchoren" – a Dutch idiom meaning "how things are currently arranged" or "the state of affairs."</note>, and what I should conclude.

<margin>Arimathea's answer.</margin>
Thus speaks Nicodemus; and Lord Arimathea,
Who saw the depicted image more clearly through the foliage
Of dark prophecy than one who is practiced in the old pages
Yet has understood and perceived them wrongly,
Addresses him thus: let go of your mistrust
And hesitation: we shall soon behold
The bright dawn, promised to us for many centuries.
The cities <note>original: "fteên" – could also refer to the "stones" or foundations of the faith standing firm.</note> stand upright. The pious lift their heads
On every side. Godly generations,
Who wait for the precursor-glimmer of the great Savior,
Rejoice, and see him coming with jubilation,

<margin>That great matters proceed slowly is proven by examples:</margin>
However long the time lingers. In great matters,
Heaven hastens slowly: that has been proven more than once.
Though we may lack patience, God will not fail
To keep the covenant of salvation, sealed to the patriarchs
With an oath time and again. Read the prophet Moses.
God needed but a moment for the work of the six days <note>original: "drijpaer dagen" – literally "three pairs of days," referring to the six days of creation.</note>,
Yet He hastened slowly, according to His eternal pleasure.

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Joseph of Arimathea to Nicodemus. Arimathea is explaining why John the Baptist has remained in the wilderness and has not yet begun his public ministry, framing this delay as part of a recurring pattern of divine preparation seen throughout sacred history.</meta>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

D

To shelter the survivors of the earth from the danger
Of the great world-flood, Noah built for a hundred years
Upon the wooden vessel, hoping mankind would repent. <note>Tradition often holds that Noah spent a century building the Ark, during which time he acted as a "preacher of righteousness," warning the world of the coming Deluge.</note>
Before Joseph rose in status, and Pharaoh wished to honor him,
And all his father’s house, threatened by the pangs of hunger,
There passed—since he first saw the light from beautiful Rachel’s womb—
Thirty years: and forty years wandered
The six pairs of tribes, <note>A reference to the twelve tribes of Israel, the descendants of the twelve sons of Jacob.</note> before they drew a free breath
In fruitful Canaan, which was so firmly promised to them.
It proceeds thus in nature, if you consider it.

<margin>Also through parables.</margin>
A cedar seedling must long wrestle against the gusts of wind,
Before it comes to prop up the clouds with its crown,
And shoots tough roots into the deep abyss:
Then it is a majestic cedar, which neither beech nor oak
May equal in honor. The moon completes her rounds
Indeed twelve times a year, bound to her tides:
The golden sun rolls once a year around the sphere
Of the heavens: but how piercing, how great
Is her heat and radiance, compared to faint moonbeams!
What life she nurtures in fields and wildernesses!
She creates a lush paradise out of the wild,
Whenever it pleases God. If food and drink are required:
She sets a rich table, filled with wine and fruit
For the hungry, who sigh in sorrow for their daily needs.
Await the time, appointed by God, with patience then,
Assured that you shall praise Him at the last,
Who can guide all matters beyond our understanding,
Just as it lies decreed in the Counsel above.

<margin>The reason why John’s appearance is delayed.</margin>
There is a reason why John hides and dives away,
Just as a flower does within its bulb, before it blossoms open,
And comes to refresh hearts with its noble scent—
Hearts which, faint and wasted away, are in danger of suffocating.
The teacher, who shall teach the people according to the law,
Needs to [protect] himself from the world and its stain...

<meta>catchword: To-dis- <note>The catchword "t'Ont-" in the original Dutch likely refers to "t'ontdecken" (to discover) or "t'ontvouwen" (to unfold) on the following page.</note></meta>

<summary>Arimathea uses biblical examples like Noah and Joseph, along with metaphors of the slow-growing cedar and the sun, to explain that great divine works require long periods of hidden preparation. He argues that John the Baptist’s seclusion in the wilderness is a necessary period of growth before his public mission begins.</summary>
<keywords>divine timing, patience, Noah's Ark, Joseph in Egypt, twelve tribes of Israel, Canaan, cedar tree metaphor, sun and moon allegory, John the Baptist, spiritual preparation</keywords>

Having previously alluded to the need for patience, Joseph now provides historical and natural examples to explain why great divine works—including the emergence of John the Baptist—require long periods of preparation and hiddenness.</meta>

### THE FIRST BOOK.

To shelter the remnants of the earth from the danger
Of the great world-flood, Noah built for a hundred years
Upon the wooden vessel, <note>Noah's Ark.</note> hoping mankind would repent.
Before Joseph attained high office, and Pharaoh wished to honor him,
And all his father's house, threatened by the distress of famine,
Thirty years had passed since he first saw the light
From beautiful Rachel's womb: and forty years wandered
The six pairs of tribes, <note>The twelve tribes of Israel.</note> before they could draw breath
In fertile Canaan, so firmly promised to them.
<margin>Also through analogies.</margin>
It happens naturally thus, if you reflect upon it.
A cedar sapling must struggle long against the gales,
Before its crown comes to support the clouds,
And shoots tough roots into the deep abyss:
Then it is a cedar tree, with which neither beech nor oak
May compare in honor. The moon completes her cycles
A full twelve times a year, bound to her tides:
The golden sun rolls once a year around the sphere
Of heaven: but how penetrating, how great
Is her heat and brilliance, compared to the faint moonlight!
How much life she nurtures in fields and deserts!
She creates a lush paradise out of the wilderness,
When it pleases God. If one needs drink and food:
She spreads a rich table full of wine and fruits
For the hungry, who sigh in grief for their basic needs.
Await the time, appointed by God, with patience then,
Assured that you shall praise Him in the end,
Who can guide matters beyond our understanding,
Just as it is determined in the counsel above.
It has its reason that John <note>John the Baptist.</note> hides and ducks away,
<margin>Reason why John's coming is delayed.</margin>
Like a flower in its bulb, before it blossoms out,
And comes to refresh hearts with its noble scent,
Which, faint and wasting away, are in danger of suffocating in distress.
The teacher, who shall teach the people according to the law,
Needs [to distance] himself from the world and its stain

<meta>catchword: t'Ont- (to Dis-)</meta>

<summary>Joseph of Arimathea uses the examples of Noah, Joseph in Egypt, and the Israelites' wandering to argue that God's greatest works require long preparation. He explains that John the Baptist remains hidden like a budding flower to prepare himself for his holy mission.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Joseph of Arimathea to Nicodemus. Joseph concludes his explanation of why John the Baptist remains in seclusion, using the examples of Moses and Samuel to show that a divine calling requires patience and internal preparation before one enters public ministry.</meta>

# JOHN.

To remember, and to pair his teaching with his deeds.
That is edifying. He who will teach another
Needs first to learn what he wishes to show to others.
The sound of words, if it be otherwise, flies all too quietly
And fruitlessly past the ear. He must not, unrestrained,
Step into the teaching office of his own accord, but sent by God Himself
Break forth with his light, so that whoever lacks light
Might then kindle the wick of his soul at that guiding light.
Thus Moses did not go toward Memphis <note>Memphis was a major city in ancient Egypt; here it refers to Moses's mission to confront Pharaoh.</note> and God’s troops,
Before God had called that shepherd from the flames of the bush,
And strengthened him with a power through which men could know
Who had sent him: and Samuel began
To prophesy, so that the six pairs of tribes <note>The twelve tribes of Israel.</note> saw
How he accepted that burden at the Highest’s pleasure—
He who, before he lived, through the heavenly decree,
Was promised to his mother Hannah <note>original: "Anna"; Samuel's mother.</note> so long in advance,
In order to, following the godly trail of other messengers of the law,
Suppress evil and plant righteousness.
John, in conversation with God through his prayers,
Wastes no time. The man’s zeal only waits
Until the spirit urges him to enter into the office
For which he was destined. I can conceive of
No other reason. Wait for the time and hour with patience then.
If the time is delayed, it is perhaps due to our own fault.

<margin>Conclusion of the conversation.</margin>
Thus spoke Arimathea, and in mutual trust
They promised to keep this conversation only to themselves,
And to serve God piously, godly, and faithfully,
By the heavenly light that would henceforth appear to them.
So they parted from one another comforted, and reflected upon
The prophecies that related to the Savior’s coming.

<margin>The people gather to hear John.</margin>
Meanwhile, the eighth day, appointed for divine service, dawned.
Jerusalem emptied out before the light had risen.
The people from both sides of the river <note>The Jordan River.</note> could be found
At Quarantania, <note>A mountain or region in the Judean desert traditionally associated with the wilderness.</note> out of a desire to now find the beloved of God—

<meta>This page continues the narrative provided by Joseph of Arimathea. Having explained the necessity of John the Baptist’s long preparation in the wilderness, the text now shifts to describe the immense and diverse crowd gathering to finally witness his public emergence. It paints a vivid picture of the social and religious tensions present in Judea at the time.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

To finally hear and see the lover of the wilderness <note>Referring to John the Baptist, who lived a solitary life in the desert before his ministry.</note>.
One saw a swarming of all sorts of people,
Old and young, of every sex and rank, flowing in from every region:
<margin>Comparison</margin>
Like an abundance of brooks in the springtime,
When the mountain snow melts into a sunken valley
And basin, gathering from everywhere into a single lake.
One saw them here and there, out of zeal and longing,
Hanging from a steep rock or in the trees,
Or sitting on the crest of a hill; some low,
And others higher; everyone appeared equally eager.
Each has his own aim. The devout heart is fiery,
While the worldly-wise are less religious than they are curious.
The criminal fears discipline, and seeks in this man
Something punishable, that he might condemn with a pretense of justice.
The repentant look mournful; the reckless laugh.
Vagabonds burn with the desire to beg for alms.
One saw soldiers there, long-tested by many dangers,
And householders, citizens, and hated tax collectors.
Levites <note>Members of the Hebrew tribe of Levi who served as assistants in the Temple.</note> mutter about what will come of this work,
And whether this can stand, according to the style and order of Moses.
Here sit scribes, Essenes, and Pharisees,
Whose nature is a sham-virtue, dressed in long robes
Hedged with letters and precious sayings <note>A reference to phylacteries (tefillin) and the fringed edges of garments (tzitzit) containing scripture, which the Gospel writers often described as being worn ostentatiously by the Pharisees.</note>.
They tickle themselves; their donkey-ears <note>A derogatory metaphor for stubbornness or foolishness.</note> itch
To sift out what serves them and what does not.
The book rests in their arms. They treat as a friend
Anyone who holds them in honor and listens to their judgment,
And they play with the law for their own profit and advantage.
<margin>Comparison</margin>
The place, hemmed in by hills, where this multitude
Awaits the arrival of the man who shall speak,
Resembles a theater, where every eye expects
The performer to appear upon the stage.
<margin>John appears to teach.</margin>
At long last the young man came, now encouraged,
Through all the throng of the people; his face turned toward the east.

He turned, and positioned himself to lean while speaking
And to stand firmly where two rock-palms supported
A crossbar on either side. Everyone honored and greeted him;
The hero <note>original: "helt". The author refers to John the Baptist as a "hero," a common 17th-century poetic convention to denote a person of great spiritual courage or significance.</note> did the same to them in return, yet modestly, without a word
Or speech, until he had first cast his gaze all around the circle.
All eyes thought they saw an angelic countenance,
And a glow of holiness around his head. Everyone in the circle fell silent.
Then the Spirit of God opened the oracles of that mouth.

# JOHN
## THE PREACHER OF REPENTANCE <note>original: "Boetgezant". This 17th-century term combines "boete" (penance/repentance) and "gezant" (envoy/ambassador), identifying John as a messenger sent to call the people to return to God.</note>.
### The second book.

->C O N T E N T S.<-

*The preacher of repentance seeks favor, and addresses the ancestral house,*
*Unfolding his state and commission, and the purpose of his teaching,*
*What Moses’ law demands of its subjects;*
*How it reveals failings, and how beneath its leaves*
*And ceremonies love is preserved,*
*As a longed-for fruit: how shadows and images*
*Look toward a redeemer, to know his nature and character*
*And office, upon which the oracles long played:*
*How worldly wisdom must bow before God’s wisdom,*
*And Moses’ glory must yield to Christ’s luster.*
*He demands and counsels that each repent their misdeeds in time,*
*And offer their service to him who wishes to use them.*
*Thus the desert-dweller <note>original: "woestynier". Referring to John the Baptist's ascetic life in the wilderness.</note> concludes with humility and awe,*
*And departs, to come forth again the following day.*

<margin>John begins to teach, and addresses the lineage of Abraham:</margin>
Lineage of Abraham, sacred and unsanctified,
Circumcised according to the law, in those patriarchs’ <note>original: "aertsvaders". The biblical forefathers, specifically Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.</note> times
First introduced, and later strengthened by heaven’s interpreter; <note>original: "’s hemels tolck". A poetic reference to Moses, who served as the intermediary or "interpreter" between God and the Israelites.</note>
When heaven called you, and chose you for His people,
Out of all nations, and through the power of miracles

Having addressed the lineage of Abraham on the previous page, he now identifies himself and his divine mandate before transitioning into a theological explanation of the necessity of the Law of Moses as a prerequisite for the new "age of salvation."</meta>

# JOHN.

You fruitful stems <note>Referring to the tribes of Israel as branches of a family tree.</note>, who walk the path of the fathers,
With whom divinity speaks, and acts through archangels,
Or messengers, and prophets in a row:
Wake up: it is now time: wake up, and listen to me.
May my teaching flow to you like a mild blessing,
And dew upon the parched field: just as the fresh rain,
After a long drought, gives life and refreshing relief
To a meadow, stifled and thirsty unto death.

<margin>He describes his descent and status, and testifies to his commission from a higher hand, and his purpose.</margin>
I am John himself, born a priest's son,
And, from my mother’s side, chosen from Aaron’s tribe <note>The priestly lineage of Israel. According to the Gospel of Luke, John's mother Elizabeth was a descendant of Aaron.</note>
To be a prophet, and even the last of the covenant,
So strongly sealed by the mouth of the Most High.
The spirit of divinity drives me to come forth
From the silent wilderness, and, to the joy of the pious,
To the terror of the wicked, to unfold to each their duty.

<margin>The age of salvation is at hand.</margin>
The shadows of the law <note>A common theological concept (typology) suggesting that Old Testament rituals were mere "shadows" or precursors to the "light" of Christ.</note> vanish. The heavenly light,
Promised to the fathers for comfort in foreign lands,
Approaches. A golden time, an age of salvation <note>original: "heileeu". A term for a momentous era of grace or spiritual prosperity.</note> is at hand,
For which the ancient kings, and priest, and prophet,
And Abraham before them, strengthened by heaven’s oath,
And all the saints strove with a single wish:
Though none of them lived to see that joyful day.
Happy are you, if you realize your good fortune,
At which my voice rises like a trumpet of repentance <note>original: "boetbazuin". An instrument used to sound a call to penance and spiritual awakening.</note>.

<margin>Without law there is no covenant.</margin>
Let the body of my discourse neither fear nor shrink from dissection.
Where no boundary marker stands, there is no transgression <note>A paraphrase of Romans 4:15: "where there is no law there is no transgression."</note>.
So it goes, where no law, command, nor any prohibition
Is heard from nature, or from the mouth of God,
Or one authorized and empowered by His might;
A truth, never denied, but century after century confirmed
By all the wise, who stand upon justice and reason.
No pious conscience can therefore escape the worm <note>The "worm of conscience"—an image of the persistent, internal gnawing of guilt.</note>,
Which gnaws the heart within, and convicts one of failing,
Should it recklessly stray from that rule.

So <note>Catchword: "Zoo".</note>

<summary>John the Baptist identifies himself as a descendant of Aaron and the final prophet of the old covenant, announcing that the long-awaited "age of salvation" has arrived. He establishes the necessity of the Law, arguing that without a defined law, there can be no recognition of sin or transgression.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sermon of John the Baptist. Following his introduction of his own lineage and divine commission, he now transitions into a theological discourse on the purpose of the Law (the Torah) and the internal requirements of faith versus outward ritual, leading toward the core commandment of love.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

<margin>The law of nature and Moses bind mankind, each in their own way.</margin>
Thus nature comes, and shows the uncircumcised their stain <note>A reference to the "natural law" or conscience that affects those outside the Jewish faith (the "uncircumcised").</note>,
And Moses convicts the circumcised through his law;
A bond of debt, which holds them bound as debtors.
Thus, in the accounting, the imbalance is found,
And most especially in Jacob’s seed <note>The people of Israel, descendants of the patriarch Jacob.</note>, enlightened more clearly than others,
And for this reason more closely bound to that strict law,
With hope of blessing, and a dread of the curses,
Threatened in Moses' pages, those never-failing books,
Pointing out that whoever transgresses a single point
Is guilty of everything before God, who knows all,
Knows the heart, and comes to shine a light behind the mask
Disguised and beautifully varnished with ceremony,
And outward appearance: for whoever travels through the scriptures,
<margin>Outward ceremonies and obedience must go hand in hand.</margin>
Learns throughout how God indeed demands offerings,
Yet first obedience: as Abraham showed us,
When God accepted the will, and spared the sacrificing
Of Isaac, his son; satisfied with a ram,
Entangled in the thicket, and joyfully at the altar-flame
Sanctified, to the praise of God, by that faithful father.
Thus one reaches God’s heart: thus no one lies closer to Him.

***

<margin>The law demands love for God and one's neighbor.</margin>
You who receive your light from Moses’ glow for so long,
Answer my question briskly. Upon what hangs
The law entrusted to you, and the teaching of the prophets?
What do they demand of you? You have worn out the scriptures,
Reading them through and through: and do you stand dazed now,
You who amaze all the world with the building of the temple?
The Knower of Hearts regards no feigned virtue, no glossing over.
You shall now name for me, concisely, the sum
Of hundreds of commandments, in Moses’ pure pages
Contained. Do you remain mute and stiff and red with shame?
I shall name them for you. The Most High demands that you with all your senses,
With all your might and strength, shall love the deity,
And your neighbor as yourself. That is everything.
The single word *love* alone comprises the count

He argues that God desires internal obedience and love over outward ritual, citing the sacrifice of Isaac as proof that the heart's intent matters most.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the sermon of John the Baptist, moving from the general obligation of the Law to a specific critique of empty ritualism. He contrasts the outward "sign" of religious ceremony with the internal "truth" of a compassionate heart, preparing the audience for the arrival of the Messiah.</meta>

### JOHN.

Of many commands and prohibitions given to you.
That is the straight line and rule of the life
<margin>It reveals the defects and teaches us to pray for grace.</margin>Of the believer. Compare here the crooked line
Of your conduct. Oh, the truth and the appearance,
How they differ from each other—the signified and the sign! <note>A theological distinction between the outward ritual (the sign) and the spiritual reality it is meant to represent (the signified).</note>
How sharply they stand out against one another!
How great is the creditor's demand! How great is the debtor's debt!
How this teaches one to cry: Lord, mercy, patience, patience.
Have compassion on your poor debtors.
Where has your idle glory, your boasting now gone,
<margin>Ceremonies, without the weight of conduct and love, are fruitless.</margin>Concerning ceremonies of the Sabbath, the new moon,
And sacrifices, and circumcision? How do the
Abstaining from unclean and forbidden foods,
The cleansing of the body and vessels, and the manner
Of fasting now stand ashamed; also the alms, practiced
With no heart, but with a sounding trumpet <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 6:2, criticizing those who perform charitable acts publicly to gain praise.</note>
Displayed before the people, to boast with that appearance!
The love for God’s honor, and the service of one's neighbor—the sparks,
The fire, the life of religion is missing here.
The Deity was long ago satiated with sacrificial meat, and sinew,
And ram. The Creator does not need to trouble the creature
For the fat of lambs, for calf, and ox, and cows.
He is weary of the scent of the censer and incense bowl.
Let one offer Him a heart, full of compassion.
The time approaches and demands another sacrifice
Than what Aaron <note>The first High Priest of Israel; his name here represents the entire system of Levitical priesthood and animal sacrifice.</note>, for centuries, scorched and burned upon the altars.
It is a waste for you to wear out sacrifices any longer:
The Lord of the temple is at hand, and not far off.
I now herald in advance, just as Malachi <note>The prophet Malachi (Malachias), who prophesied a messenger would prepare the way for the Lord.</note>
Has foretold, the day and the rising of the Messiah,
Who drives all shadows from before your sight,
Fulfills the law, and illuminates what was hidden in shadow.
After severity follows grace, after shadow the truth,
After darkness the light, and living clarity.

<summary>John the Baptist condemns hollow religious rituals, such as animal sacrifice and public fasting, calling instead for internal compassion and love. He announces that the era of the Old Testament Law is transitioning into the era of the Messiah, who brings grace and truth.</summary>
<keywords>John the Baptist, Legalism vs. Grace, Rituals, Sacrifice, Aaron, Malachi, Messiah, Compassion, Shadows and Light</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of John the Baptist. Following the previous page’s critique of hollow rituals, he now begins to explain how historical figures from the Hebrew Scriptures were "types" or "shadows"—prophetic precursors—of the coming Messiah.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

Such a rebirth differs as night and day.
Who is there who can rightly complain about this?

<margin>The images and shadows point to the Messiah, and his attributes, office, and works.</margin>
As many images as step before God as the Messiah,
As if in a grand review, they look upon his attributes,
Or office and services. Each image depicted something special,
Which can comfort you, and support you like a staff,
To walk firmly when you follow the trail of his life.
And observe to what end—driven by love alone—
The Father bestows upon you his priceless pledge,
The firstborn son: anointed by a higher hand,
Much richer than the <term>patriarchs</term> <note>original: "aertsprieſters" (arch-priests) and "profeeten" (prophets)</note>, and prophets,
And kings, who sweated in the long racecourse
Of the old <term>covenant of salvation</term> <note>The "Heilverbont" refers to the theological concept of the agreement between God and humanity regarding redemption.</note>, which now gives way to the new:
Just as the shadow retreats before the all-seeing sun,
Death flees before life, and the letter before the soul
Yields and gives up. I want to show you the Word for which I kneel,
The depicted Word, so that you may see
Clearly before you what was previously seen only in the distance.

<margin>Adam.</margin>
Patriarch Adam, before his spouse misled him,
Modeled an Adam upon whom all descendants rested,
In his perfection, so divinely ennobled,
As the Almighty sculpted and ensouled him in His image,
So like the Creator that son and father together
Never came closer to one another in their likeness.

<margin>Abel.</margin>
Innocent Abel, because of the rising altar-flame
Slain by his brother's hatred, murderously angry at him,
Looked upon the innocence of this one, who shall patiently
Suffer out of envy—innocent, and guiltless.

<margin>Noah.</margin>
Patriarch Noah, who, constantly at work,
Built the mighty vessel <note>original: "drijftuigh," literally "floating-thing," referring to the Ark.</note>, a prototype of the church,
Looked upon the Master Builder, who, to save humanity,
Is at hand and, to the praise of God, shall build a church,
In which is saved—by the lamb, attached to the wood—
Whoever flees the <term>tower of the Highest</term> <note>Likely a reference to worldly pride or the Tower of Babel, contrasted with the safety of the Church/Ark.</note> and trusts firmly in God.

<meta>This page continues John the Baptist's sermon on the "types" or prophetic foreshadowing of the Messiah found in the Hebrew Scriptures. Having previously discussed Adam and Abel, he now moves through the patriarchs Melchizedek, Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Moses, and Aaron.</meta>

### JOHN.

<margin>
Melchizedek.
</margin>
Then Melchizedek steps forward in a presentation,
Like God’s own priest, and a true King of Peace,
Who provides wine and wheat <note>original: "weite"; here referring to the bread and wine Melchizedek offered Abraham, often seen by Christian theologians as a precursor to the Eucharist.</note>, and blesses the warring people.
He appears as if from a dark cloud,
And represents the divinity and humanity of an
Arch-priest <note>original: "Aertspriester" (High Priest)</note>, already present and appeared,
And also the sacrifice, which, when the bloody offering falls silent,
Shall speak longer than the sun rises above the horizon.

<margin>
Abraham,
Isaac and
Jacob.
</margin>
In Isaac you see a son of Abraham before the sword,
Obedient unto death; in Jacob, who secures the blessing
And the birthright before his brother,
A shepherd, richly blessed and enriched by God.

<margin>Joseph.</margin>
In Joseph's conduct—in his oppression and his exaltation
At Pharaoh's right hand—you can encounter the Savior <note>original: "heilant"</note>,
Who, long slandered, rejected, and hated,
Shall finally shine as God's secret counsel,
Whose all-seeing providence protects countless thousands of souls
From the sharp sword of hunger, and tramples
The destruction of irreconcilable death with his foot.
Wake up: wake up, and run to meet this Savior.

<margin>Moses.</margin>
Faithful Moses, who delivers the people from slavery,
And leads them out to the promised dominion,
Through the Red Sea, the grave of the kingdom of darkness,
And shows them the law, which is written by God
On stone tablets; he points to a more faithful leader
Of the chosen host; and a more righteous mediator <note>original: "scheitsman"</note>,
Who, between God and the people, in the midst of the raging strife,
Speaks for each, and intercedes, and finally obtains
Reconciliation through intercession, and leads them into heaven’s heritage,
A more beautiful Canaan, where no one shall die anymore.

<margin>Aaron.</margin>
The brother Aaron, in his high-priestly vestments,
Shows you a higher one, who stands before your temple,
Ready—not through the blood of calf, and goat, and rams—
To offer himself upon the altar, in smoke and haze and flames,

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>John the Baptist explains how Old Testament figures like Melchizedek, Joseph, and Moses serve as prophetic models for the Messiah's life and sacrificial mission. He emphasizes the transition from physical sacrifices and laws to a spiritual, eternal salvation.</summary>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic discourse on the "types" or biblical figures of the Old Testament that foreshadow the coming of the Messiah. The previous page concluded with Moses; here, the speaker transitions through the High Priest (Aaron), Joshua, Samson, David, and Solomon, eventually moving to specific prophetic signs regarding the Messiah’s lineage and birth.</meta>

# THE SECOND BOOK.

But through his own blood, innocent, silent, and mute,
And thus sanctified, not to enter into the sanctuary
On earth, but high above into the vast regions of heaven,
And there, in the Holiest of Holies, to speak
With God the Father, interceding for everyone, and finally
To set you at his high hand <note>original: "hooge hant"; usually meaning the right hand, the position of honor and authority</note> in the kingdom.
What shall I say of Joshua, and the heroes,
Who, for the people’s salvation, so readily donned their armor?
A row of deliverers, bravely and courageously riding out,
Portray for you the Hero who comes marching here;
And Samson, so famous, whenever he puts his strength to work,
Was the image of God’s son, in invincible strength.
<margin>David and Solomon.</margin>
King David, so praised in war,
After whom your Savior is also called the Son of David,
Represented him everywhere through his constant suffering,
And in oppressions, he showed the entrance to joy;
Just as Solomon, in full glory,
And luxury, and abundance, and rich in majesty,
Offered you a sight of him, when he shone, by all cities
And kings everywhere celebrated and adored.
<margin>The prophecies refer to Christ.</margin>
The prophecies proceed on a firm footing,
Bringing the approaching one to the mind through signs,
So that you may unfailingly know him as he draws near.
They convince you as clearly as if they spoke aloud.
He springs from Judah’s tribe, and King David’s seed,
Also at his rightful time. The scepter <note>original: "staf" (staff/scepter)</note> of Judah stands
With foreigners, just as the patriarch <note>referring to Jacob in Genesis 49:10</note> said,
When he prepared himself for his final journey to God.
He was born of a pure virgin,
As Isaiah, the glory of royal blood, mentioned;
Just as Ezekiel, with his closed gate,
Saw such a miraculous birth from afar <note>The "closed gate" of Ezekiel 44:2 was historically interpreted by theologians as a symbol of the Virgin Mary.</note>:
And the hero’s birthplace clearly agrees with Micah’s voice,
Who brings forth the Eternal One from poor Bethlehem.

# JOHN.

The East brought him gold, and the scent of frankincense, and gifts,
As seen in the writings of *David* and *Isaiah*:
And grandmother *Rachel* had heard that cruel massacre of children <note>A reference to the "Massacre of the Innocents" by King Herod, traditionally linked to the prophecy in Jeremiah 31:15 of Rachel weeping for her children.</note>,
So many centuries before *Herod*.
*Hosea* told you how God’s dear son escaped,
And God called the exile back again from *Egypt*.
If one follows his entire life's path from now on,
Then you shall see how all the oracles <note>In this context, "oracles" refers to divine prophecies from the Old Testament.</note> apply to him.
Who closes their eyes in the clear brightness of noon?
Who stops their ears now for the trumpet of repentance of the truth?

<margin>*Daniel* indicated the time of *Christ's* coming.</margin>
And as for the time: now the prophecy
Of the wise *Daniel* quickly speeds past the milestone.
He showed you the king’s dream, and the shifting ages
Within the statue, portraying the four chief empires,
Through the golden head, the chest of silver metal,
The belly of copper, and of iron, clay, and steel
The legs, and the foot, too weak to hold its ground.
Thus he depicts the empire of *Ninus* <note>original: "Ninus"; the legendary founder of Nineveh, representing the first world empire, the Babylonian/Assyrian.</note>, and that which the *Persians* hold,
And *Alexander’s* throne, and *Caesar’s* great authority,
Whereupon the mountain-stone strikes, and rings with a single blow
That magnificent statue to pieces and to dust,
Indicating the head of the kingdom, which shoots its root
As deep into the earth as its head rises to heaven.
What kingdom? The kingdom of the Anointed One <term>Messiah: from the Hebrew 'Mashiach', meaning the Anointed One</term>, to which my voice points you.
The foundation stone of this kingdom *Daniel* sets before your eyes.
The *Messiah* comes already, appeared from on high.

That you do not too recklessly despise that foundation stone,
The true cornerstone, of which *David* prophesied,
How God honored him, when malice dishonored him.

He builds on sandy ground, who does not build on this foundation.
When the pouring rain comes, and the water-streams roar,
And the storm-winds, fiercely howling, rustle,

# THE SECOND BOOK.

And strike against the loose building from all sides,
Then it collapses, and cannot withstand any force:
Then the fall is as great as the structure itself,
While a rock foundation stands, and can hold up against the weight.

<margin>The nature of Christ’s kingdom.</margin>
Though he appears only poor and defenseless,
And not as the descendant of *David* used to,
Stepping forward armed with proud lion-banners <note>A reference to the "Lion of Judah," the traditional symbol of the Israelite royal tribe.</note>:
He comes to accept no worldly kingdom nor earthly crown,
But to establish a heavenly kingdom in the mind
Of man, without battle or the shedding of much blood.
He seeks to build a throne in the heart, and from within
To rule by his law, and to tame untamed
Senses. This is the mark where his power begins,
A power that overcomes all the violence of the world,
Through suffering and patience. When he begins to teach,
Then one shall see swords turn into plowshares,
The weaponsmith reforge the spear into a scythe <note>These lines reference the famous prophecies of peace in Isaiah 2:4 and Micah 4:3.</note>,
Peace, like grass in the field, increasing in the cities,
The tame wolf by the lamb, the forest snake in the hedges
By turtledoves, and the lion lying by the oxen.
He finds a wilderness, and beneath his tread
A paradise blossoms, tainted by no poison.
Misunderstanding vanishes where the light of the world shines through,
And the first innocence <note>original: "onnozelheit"; here referring to the state of humanity before the Fall in Eden.</note> comes forth once again.

Set the worldly-wise and the divinely-wise against each other.
<margin>The difference between the worldly-wise and heavenly wisdom.</margin>
This one looks only to wealth, splendor, and name and fame,
And the other to humility and hidden poverty.
This one looks to descent from noble blood,
And the other neither to blood nor status, but unfeigned virtue.
This one prizes all that pleases the eye and delights the body,
And the other the delight and luxury enjoyed within the heart.
This one wants to measure and enlarge himself broadly,
And the other to pass by narrowed and forgotten.
This one burns for revenge and plunder, regarding neither right nor wrong,

### JOANNES.

The East brought him gold, and the scent of frankincense, and gifts,
As seen in the writings of David and Isaiah:
And grandmother Rachel <note>A reference to Jeremiah 31:15, later applied to the Massacre of the Innocents in Matthew 2:18.</note> had heard that cruel slaughter of the children,
So many centuries before Herod.
Hosea told you how God's dear son escaped,
And God called the exile back again from Egypt.
If one follows His whole life's path from now on,
Then you shall see how all the oracles apply to Him.
Who closes their eyes in the clear brightness of midday?
Who now stops their ears to the trumpet of repentance of the truth?

<margin>*Daniel pointed out the time of Christ’s coming.*</margin>
And as for the time: now the prophecy
Of the wise Daniel speeds quickly past the landmark.
He showed you the king's dream <note>Refers to King Nebuchadnezzar's dream of a great statue in Daniel 2.</note>, and the unequal times
In the image, depicting the four chief empires:
Through the golden head, the chest of silver metal,
The belly of copper, and of iron, clay, and steel
The legs, and the feet, too weak to hold their ground.
Thus he paints the kingdom of Ninus <note>Ninus was the legendary founder of Nineveh; he represents the Assyrian/Babylonian Empire here.</note>, and that which the Persians hold,
And Alexander’s throne, and Caesar’s great authority,
Upon which the mountain stone strikes, and rings with a single blow,
Shattering that magnificent image to pieces and to dust,
Indicating the head of the kingdom, which shoots its root
As deep into the earth as its head rises to heaven.
What kingdom? The kingdom of the Anointed One <note>original: "’s gezalfdens" (the Messiah's)</note>, to which my voice points you.
Daniel sets the foundation stone of this kingdom before your eyes.
The Messiah comes already, appeared from on high.

Take heed, builders, proud of your temple’s splendor,
That you do not too recklessly despise that foundation stone,
The true cornerstone <note>A reference to Psalm 118:22, "The stone the builders rejected has become the cornerstone."</note>, of which David prophesied,
How God honored him when wickedness dishonored him.

<meta>This page continues the poetic reflection on the nature of Christ’s spiritual kingdom, specifically focusing on how divine power manifests through the humble and the overlooked, contrasting worldly pride with spiritual contentment.</meta>

-># J O H N .<-

And he who forgets his suffering, is rich in his conscience.
This one toils; that one remains still, seated in contemplation.
This one hungers eternally; that one <unclear>becomes</unclear> easily satisfied.
Happy is he who understands his own well-being.

<margin>
God’s
workings
through
disregarded
things and
persons.
</margin>
When Supreme Wisdom wishes to show its glory,
What does it not accomplish, through materials and persons,
Quite lowly in appearance, in the eyes of mortals,
So that it may humble the high, and raise the low to the peak!
When this Wisdom confirms its aim with active proof,
It can strike down the peacock-tail of the boaster, <note>The peacock tail is a traditional symbol of vanity and pride.</note>
And raise to salvation what sinks in despair.
Thus all that is seen remains within its scope and measure,
Set by a higher hand, while the wise are sleeping.
What is more: this universe was created out of nothing. <note>The theological concept of *creatio ex nihilo*.</note>
Out of darkness rose the never-before-seen radiance,
And out of that bare shimmer, the stars in the heavens’ sphere.
The human body allowed itself to be molded from a lump of clay,
The woman from a rib-bone. How gloriously triumph
The lamp and jars of the hero Gideon, and how <note>original: "held Gedeon." Gideon defeated a vast army using only 300 men carrying torches hidden inside clay jars (Judges 7).</note>
Many plagues does a chest bring to the uncircumcised! <note>Refers to the Ark of the Covenant (the "chest") which caused tumors and plagues among the Philistines who had captured it (1 Samuel 5).</note>
What power a man brewed in the breeding-ground of his locks! <note>A reference to Samson, whose supernatural strength was tied to his hair.</note>
A loaf of bread, a water jar, in the parching heat and resentment
Of King Ahab, nourished Elijah with a strength, <note>During a Great Famine in the reign of King Ahab, Elijah was sustained by divine provision.</note>
That stayed with him for forty days, day and night:
And to speak of small things, of no reputation:
How could a foundling plague the court of Memphis, <note>Refers to Moses, the "foundling" rescued from the Nile, who brought the ten plagues upon Egypt (represented here by the capital, Memphis).</note>
Who, fished out of the water, so early trod the crown underfoot!
The lad of Jesse, who barely possessed a stable, <note>King David, the son of Jesse (Izai), who began as a humble shepherd.</note>
A shepherd won the kingdom, and ruled for forty years.
Elisha, following his master’s path, famed for wonders, <note>original: "Helize." Elisha was the successor to the prophet Elijah.</note>
Raised a dead man through his corpse in the grave. <note>In 2 Kings 13:21, a dead man was restored to life when his body touched the bones of Elisha.</note>
Thus it appears clearly what God’s omnipotence can work,
Through lowly materials, and how it knows how to serve itself
With the disregarded, and those unseen by the world:

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the theme of divine reversal, arguing that God chooses lowly people and mundane objects—like Gideon’s jars, Moses the foundling, and David the shepherd—to demonstrate His supreme power and humble the proud. It frames these biblical examples as evidence of a "Higher Hand" that works through what the world considers insignificant.</summary>
<keywords>divine providence, humility, Gideon, Samson, Elijah, Moses, King David, Elisha, creation from nothing, biblical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic narrative regarding the manifestation of divine power. While the previous page focused on how God uses the lowly and mundane (like Gideon’s jars or Samson’s hair) to achieve great ends, this page transitions to the specific miracles of Christ and the fulfillment of biblical promises made to the patriarchs.</meta>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

And what aspires even higher; its glory wishes everywhere
Shortly to arise out of human misfortune.

<margin>Christ's miracles.</margin>
Afflictions approach, joined together like a chain.
Whoever is a leper, or sick, or possessed;
The mute, and deaf, and blind, and lame, and those
Tormented by gout; each seeks the power that relieves him,
Which can wake the dead so that they move their limbs,
Stills the storms, knows how to create wine from water,
And tames the elements, as if bound by His oath.
Then the divinity draws honor from humanity's pain and sorrow,
Persuaded by good deeds and many a miraculous sign
That would break hearts harder than a diamond. <note>In 17th-century literature, the diamond was the ultimate symbol of impenetrable hardness, often used to describe a stubborn or unrepentant heart.</note>

<margin>Promises of salvation before and under the Law.</margin>
There is no reason for an honest heart to deceive,
And less reason for God’s mouth to lie in promises of salvation,
Sworn to the patriarchs from the cradle of the world.
Thus the Truth let itself be heard very early by Adam:
How the seed of the woman would crush the serpent's head. <note>A reference to Genesis 3:15, traditionally called the *Protoevangelium*, the first gospel promise.</note>
Thus He swore to Abraham, faithful to Heaven’s charge,
To bless the whole world in his unique seed.
Thus He came to meet Isaac and Rebekah’s son, <note>Referring to Jacob.</note>
And strengthen him with that comfort. King David knows
That the Almighty swore to him with a high oath
How his wise offspring would reign without end,
And he knew that dear son, just as a father does;
A son whose dominion, defined by neither border nor age,
Would last longer than the sun rises and falls.
This cannot apply to Solomon, nor to the subsequent heirs:
For the Savior’s throne shall neither totter nor decay.

<margin>Witnesses that Christ has appeared and is at hand.</margin>
Alongside promises of salvation, now place types and prophecy, <note>The author refers to "images" (beelt), meaning "types"—Old Testament figures or events believed to foreshadow Christ.</note>
Previously contemplated and heard in a row,
And then the constant expectation of all the pious,
Now springing forth from that dream: just as has been heard
From the song of angels, heard by shepherds; from the star
That awakened kings in the East from so far away;

<summary>This page outlines how Christ's miracles and the fulfillment of ancient covenants (from Adam to David) prove His divinity. It marks the transition from the "dream" of Old Testament prophecy to the physical reality of the Nativity.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, healing, salvation, Adam, Abraham, David, prophecy, Star of Bethlehem, Messiah, covenants</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic history of Christ, transitioning from the general promises made to the patriarchs (Adam, Abraham, David) to the specific events of His early life and the spiritual richness of His arrival, which the poet compares to the legendary wealth of King Solomon.</meta>

# J O A N N E S. <note>John.</note>

From the Angel Gabriel, who appeared to Zacharias,
And brought to Mary the message of the Messiah;
From the gray Simeon, and Anna, the old widow,
Whose holy old age neared a full century. <note>In the Gospel of Luke, Anna is described as being eighty-four years old, or having been a widow for eighty-four years; the poet here rounds her age upward for poetic effect.</note>
No more than eighteen years ago he trod upon Aaron’s threshold, <note>A reference to the Temple in Jerusalem, the domain of the Aaronic priesthood. The "eighteen years" refers to the time elapsed between Christ's appearance in the Temple at age twelve and the start of His ministry at age thirty.</note>
And sat, as a teacher, in the midst of the temple,
Where everyone questioned him, and heard him, and stood amazed and mute
Before his wisdom in the law; and in the sanctuary
He was so well-schooled by Aaron’s line that the whole choir wondered.
Since then, he kept himself secluded at Nazareth,
Except that he, being strictly devoted to his parents,
Visited Jerusalem three times a year for the festivals,
So as not to profane Moses’ laws or God’s commandments:
Just as the eighth day piously witnessed his circumcision;
Thus the strict law early found a voluntary subject,
To release his companions from the curse of the law.
Thus he comes lovely, not inclined toward vengeance or punishment,
But with mercy and favor and love and full blessing,
Like the rain and the cool dew upon the field,
As David long ago sang upon his harp. <note>Likely a reference to Psalm 72:6, "He shall come down like rain upon the mown grass: as showers that water the earth."</note>

<margin>A parable, expressing the worthiness of the good offered to the Jews through Christ’s coming.</margin>
Then, once every three years, the gold fleet to the shores
Of Ezion-Geber <note>original: "Aziongabar"</note> came rushing, and arrived
From Ophir, with flags of purple, proud and gay,
With cables of gold, and sails of fine silk;
Like a golden mountain most magnificently displayed,
In the age of Solomon: how the court trumpets
Began to sound! How the rejoicing people joined the dance,
To song, and trombone, and drum, and shawm!
How the Red Sea rustled with a sailing forest! <note>A metaphor for the many masts of the fleet.</note>
How the coast and city triumphed with festive stages!
How many spectacles, where the wealthy eye might graze,
Unfolded Solomon’s prophesied glory.
In Jerusalem, one trod upon no stones in the streets,
But they shone in gold, and people danced upon silver plates.

<summary>This page recounts Christ's early life, focusing on his wisdom in the Temple at age twelve and his adherence to Jewish law, before using a lush description of Solomon's gold fleet as an analogy for the spiritual treasures Christ brings.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from Christ's private life in Nazareth to the public announcement of His arrival. The poet contrasts the earthly majesty of Solomon’s temple with the spiritual grace of Christ, and then introduces the voice of John the Baptist calling for repentance and the leveling of human pride.</meta>

## THE SECOND BOOK.

F

A forest of cedars cast its shade everywhere,
As first the fig tree shaded the surrounding valley:
The temple with its court, blessed by a higher hand,
Shone just as if gold had rained down from the sky.

But all this golden splendor, as the wisest king <note>King Solomon, traditionally considered the wisest of kings and the builder of the First Temple.</note> says,
Was nothing else than a vanishing vanity,
A mere glance in the eyes; he was less glorious than a lily
In its natural dress <note>A reference to Matthew 6:28-29: "even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these [lilies]."</note>: yet if you can trust the gospel,
The glad tidings, of which my mouth bears witness to you;
If you bow before that Anointed One; <term>Anointed One</term> <note>From the Dutch *gezalfden*, a translation of the Hebrew *Messiah* or Greek *Christos*.</note>
One finds in him alone all the heavenly realm enclosed.
The Father has anointed the Son and bathed him
With the balsam of grace. In him rests the destiny
Of all riches and the knowledge of God.
So rich an anointing flows from the head downward,
And shares itself, as it flows, with all his members. <note>In Christian theology, the "members" refers to the individual believers who make up the 'body' of the Church.</note>
Receive him then, when he comes, with an open gate.
Whoever receives him enjoys the saving word.

<margin>The end of John’s service, a preparation to receive the Messiah.</margin>
To lead all nations to such a great salvation,
And to prepare the highway <term>highway</term> <note>Original: *heirbaen*, a military road or main thoroughfare, here referring to the spiritual path for the Lord.</note> for that anointed Lord,
I appear hither, driven by the Spirit.
Awake! Prepare a level path for the Lord in the heart.
Let hills be leveled: let sunken valleys be raised. <note>A reference to the prophecy of Isaiah 40:3-4, famously applied to John the Baptist.</note>
In this way, one used to welcome a prince and king
With all reverence, into the enclosed court.
Let everyone wait in order, so that the king’s eye finds no dust
To cause displeasure in the neighborhoods and the streets.
Let everyone stand, for heaven’s service, obedient and composed.
Neither one’s own wisdom nor righteousness, which are highly
And gloriously esteemed in the eyes of the conceited,
Please him. One must level the mountain of pride,
To receive heaven’s wisdom and the laws of the Most High.
He who appears as a transgressor to everyone,
Let him step forth obediently, and humbly, worn down

<summary>The poet contrasts the temporary material wealth of King Solomon with the eternal spiritual grace of Christ, the truly "Anointed One." John the Baptist then appears, calling on the people to prepare their hearts for the Messiah by humbling their pride.</summary>
<keywords>Solomon, Temple, Jesus Christ, Anointed One, John the Baptist, Repentance, Humility, Grace, Isaiah 40</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic description of the transformation brought about by the arrival of the Messiah. Following the previous page’s introduction of John the Baptist’s mission to prepare the way, these verses describe the universal nature of the salvation being offered—moving beyond ethnic or social boundaries—and contrast the fading glory of the Old Law (Moses) with the bright, penetrating light of Christ.</meta>

### JOHN.

Through remorse for one’s sins, into heaven’s fellowship,
Comforted by hearing this most joyful news:
That, without distinction of lineage, sex, or rank,
The gate to the rich court of heaven stands open
For everyone who no longer takes counsel from flesh and blood, <note>In a biblical context, "flesh and blood" refers to human nature, instincts, or earthly wisdom as opposed to divine revelation.</note>
But accepts the unearned salvation and the offered grace
Of the Savior, keeps God’s commandments,
And trusts in Emmanuel, <note>original: "Emanuël"; a Hebrew name meaning "God with us," used as a title for Jesus.</note> the eternal word.
A heavenly age dawns; the worldly one has passed away.
The despairing heart begins to hope for the eternal good.
Those who relied on themselves now renounce their own will.
Those who seemed wise now thoughtfully set out on different paths,
Becoming fools in the eyes of the world; those who seemed unwise before
Have now chosen the wisdom of the heavenly kingdom.
The idolater, once blinded by his false gods,
Seeks the fountain of life, which he finds with full joy.
The slave to the world, once shackled by many vices,
Goes out loose and free, seeking to cultivate the virtues.
He who once seized another’s goods as his own prize
Now pours his wealth into the laps of the poor.
The Savior comes and reinforces the call of Isaiah, <note>Referencing the prophet Isaiah, who foretold the coming of a Messiah and the voice crying in the wilderness.</note>
And as the world watches the rising of the Messiah,
The dark night vanishes; now a bright age shines forth,
A light of salvation for both Greek and Hebrew, <note>Refers to the two main groups in the early Christian world: the Gentiles (Greeks) and the Jews (Hebrews).</note>
For the circumcised and the uncircumcised, and for all peoples.
The sun of salvation drives the shadows and clouds
From the face of the earth, and seats itself upon the throne,
Equally near to everyone, and equally beautiful to all.

<margin>The difference between the glory of Moses and Christ.</margin>
From Moses’ countenance there once shone a faint clarity, <note>A reference to Exodus 34, where Moses’ face shone after being in God's presence, though it was a glory that faded and required a veil.</note>
But the radiance and beam of this truth shines differently:
Like a sun that reveals even the smallest grain of dust,
Shunning any stain, it comes to cleanse the heart of every flaw.
The great Savior comes with his lap full of blessings;
Repent! The time has come. Abandon all your wandering paths.

<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

<summary>This page describes the universal nature of Christ's salvation, which levels all social and ethnic distinctions and calls for a complete transformation of character. It concludes by contrasting the temporary glory of the Mosaic Law with the all-revealing, heart-cleansing light of the Messiah.</summary>
<keywords>Repentance, Emmanuel, Isaiah, Salvation, Universalism, Moses vs. Christ, Virtue, Messiah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse following the arrival of the Messiah and the ministry of John the Baptist. While the previous page focused on the opening of heaven’s gate and the transition from the Old Law to the light of Christ, this page shifts to a stern warning. It uses the biblical example of Nineveh’s repentance to call the current generation of Jerusalem to similar penance before judgment falls.</meta>

# THE SECOND BOOK.

F 2
<meta>catchword: To</meta>

### Concerning false teachings and flaws. Keep to the path,
### The highway straight to God. It points you thither.

<margin>Exhortation to penance through the example of Nineveh.</margin>
Jerusalem now resembles Nineveh, once situated
So high in the east, and ascended to the stars,
A royal city, before its downfall,
Where all wickedness, right and wrong, held sway.
Everyone followed what their lustful brain imagined,
And gave their sensuality free rein in luxury,
Scarcely able to bear "strong legs": <note>A Dutch idiom, "sterke benen kunnen de weelde niet dragen," meaning it is difficult to remain humble and virtuous during times of great prosperity.</note> as one could
Observe in the wise Solomon. <note>Referring to King Solomon, who, despite his God-given wisdom, was led astray by his many wives and earthly splendor in his later years.</note>
Their wickedness climbed so high that, finally top-heavy,
It threatened to collapse, unless God’s goodness, ever-watchful,
Moved by compassion for their suffering,
Had dispatched the penitent prophet <note>The prophet Jonah.</note> from the east in time,
Who, reborn from the whale’s belly, came to awaken them.
They listen to his voice, and wake up, and put on
Hair-shirts now instead of festive garments.
The king leads them, and, having risen in haste
From his ivory throne, tears his purple clothes,
Puts on coarse sackcloth, and fasts, and pours out prayers,
And sits sadly in ashes, broken and submissive.
Thus the entire city fasts, even the hungry, lowing cattle.
The Divinity soon allows Himself to repent of His threat.
Thus thousands of penitents were saved.

I also come hither, on a similar footing,
And warn you, unless you repent for your misdeeds,
And wash them away through remorse and heartache and tears,
And seek to wean your desires from worldly vanity,
And accept this mercy, offered to you today;
The stern judge shall, from his high throne,
Judge you, as He did your forefathers, wicked of life,
When, in His wrath, while they remained stubborn,
He justly plagued them, and gave them into their enemies' hands.
*It is set before you: the blessing or the punishment.*

<summary>This page compares the corruption of Jerusalem to that of ancient Nineveh, using the story of Jonah to illustrate how true repentance and humility can avert divine judgment. It concludes with a direct warning to the reader to choose between penance and punishment.</summary>
<keywords>Nineveh, Jerusalem, Jonah, King Solomon, repentance, penance, divine judgment, sackcloth and ashes, mercy</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of the urgency of repentance, shifting from the general example of Nineveh to the specific, humble ministry of John the Baptist as he begins his work at the Jordan River.</meta>

<header>J O H N .</header>

To choose; he heeds neither rewards nor threats:
He comes for punishment, and demands the principal with the interest. <note>John warns that divine judgment is like a debt collector who demands not only the original sin (principal) but the accumulated weight of a life lived without repentance (interest).</note>
Happy is the soul that, poured out before him,
Penitently embraces the good that is offered to her.

<margin>John offers his service to everyone.</margin>
To accomplish this through means, in God’s name,
I offer my service, a support to strengthen you,
And will, in this region, along the banks of the Jordan,
Station myself to answer every person’s question.
Whoever wishes to learn, let them ruminate on my daily counsels.
Whoever finds themselves defiled, may let their body bathe.
My office requires that I, through my lesson and small gift,
Received from God, prune the rough and then polish it, <note>John describes his role as a spiritual craftsman, smoothing the "rough" character of the people to prepare them for God.</note>
Prepare the penitent, and immerse them in the pools,
Until the Savior comes to wash the soul and the body,
And purify them in the fountain of water and of spirit,
Even clearer than Naaman ever was before, <note>Naaman was a Syrian commander who was healed of leprosy after washing in the Jordan River seven times, following the instructions of the prophet Elisha.</note>
When he trusted in the word of the disciple of Elijah. <note>original: "Helias". In the Dutch text, Elijah is referred to as Helias.</note>
My service points, like a hand, to the arrival of the Messiah,
Revealing himself at the river in this region.
I see thousands already, like willows by a brook,
Sprouting day after day, and bearing fragrant blossoms.
May I, through my service, please the eye of the Anointed One, <note>original: "gezalfdens". This refers to the "Christ" or "Messiah," both of which mean "The Anointed."</note>
To win souls for him and bring them to his honor;
That is the one and only thing I desire from God.

<margin>and departs.</margin>
Thus John concluded, and fell flat upon his face,
Prayed secretly for a while, then finally rose again,
And, blessing everyone, stepped straight ahead, through a cloud
Of the joyfully cheering and everywhere-pressing people,
Toward his desert cave, followed by many of the pious,
To come forth again in the morning with the day.

# JOHN
## THE PREACHER OF REPENTANCE.
### The third book.

#### S U M M A R Y.

*John, having risen with the sun, baptizes and teaches,*
*Rebukes hypocrisy, and threatens it with God’s rod.*
*Soldier and tax collector are converted by his lesson,*
*And Christ’s comfort is promised, a firm and certain protection.*
*The heavenly council assembles; the Father announces that counsel*
*Regarding the Son’s journey to the desolate Quarantania,* <note>Mount Quarantania is the traditional site of the Temptation of Christ in the wilderness.</note>
*So that John may wash and bathe him in the water,*
*And they go to the festival on the banks of the Jordan.*
*The Spirit drives Christ, after the baptism, into the wrestling ring.* <note>The "wrestling ring" is a poetic metaphor for the wilderness where Christ struggled against temptation.</note>
*The Jews send ecclesiastical legates to John,* <note>original: "kerckgezanten". These were the priests and Levites sent from Jerusalem to investigate John's authority.</note>
*To ask who he is; they press him hard and fast.*
*He keeps himself humble, and, to plant his Master’s honor,*
*Points them and everyone to the Lamb, who bears humanity’s crime,*
*And crushes the power of the abyss, which plagues soul and body.*

---

<margin>John appears the next day again, to teach and to baptize.</margin>
The dawn everywhere dispelled the dismal shadow.
The stars sank, and the sun rose from the horizon
Of the earth, with a glow of gold and diamonds,
And shone over mountain, desert, and the water’s edge
Of the regional river, from whose cold waters, on its right side,
A stream flows in, which, passing by Jericho,
Refreshes the barren heath, lacking in cattle and fodder,
Until it flows down into the lap of its mother. <note>The "mother" here likely refers to the Dead Sea, where the Jordan and its tributaries eventually terminate.</note>

# JOHN.

To choose; he pays heed neither to reward nor threats:
He comes to punish, and demands the principal with interest. <note>This financial metaphor suggests that God requires not just the "debt" of original sin to be paid, but the "interest" of a life spent in devotion.</note>
Happy is the soul that, poured out before him,
Penitently embraces the good that is offered to her.

<margin>John offers his service to everyone,</margin>
To accomplish this through means, in God’s name,
I offer my service, a support to strengthen you,
And wish, in this region, around the banks of the Jordan,
To remain, standing ready to answer everyone’s questions.
Whoever wishes to learn, ruminate upon my daily counsel.
Whoever finds himself defiled may let his body be bathed.
My office requires that I, through my lesson and small gift
Received from God, prune away the rough and then polish, <note>John compares his ministry to a craftsman or gardener, refining the rough character of the people.</note>
Prepare the penitent, and dip them in the pools,
Until the Savior comes to wash the soul and the body,
And purify in the fountain of water and spirit,
Even clearer than Naaman ever was before,
When he trusted in the word of the disciple of Elijah. <note>original: "Naman" and "leerlings van Helias". This refers to the Syrian general Naaman who was cured of leprosy in the Jordan River by following the instructions of the prophet Elisha, Elijah's successor.</note>
My service points, like a hand, to the arrival of the Messiah,
Revealing himself at the river in this region.
I see thousands already, like willows by a brook,
Sprouting day by day, and bearing fragrant blossoms.
May I please the Anointed One’s <note>original: "'s gezalfdens", meaning the Christ or Messiah.</note> eye through my service,
Winning souls for him and bringing them to his honor;
That is the one and only thing I desire from God.

<margin>and departs.</margin>
Thus John concluded, and fell flat upon his face,
Prayed secretly for a while, then rose at last again,

Toward his desert cave, followed by many of the pious,
To come forth again tomorrow with the light of day.

*Reproves hypocrisy, and threatens it with God's rod.* <note>The "rod" is a traditional symbol for divine correction or punishment.</note>
*Soldier and tax collector are converted by his lesson,*
*And Christ is promised as comfort, a firm and certain protection.*
*The heavenly council gathers. The Father declares that council's plan*
*For the Son's ascent toward the desolate Quarantania,* <note>Quarantania refers to the Mount of Temptation in the Judean Desert, where Jesus fasted for forty days.</note>
*So that John may wash and bathe Him in the water,*
*And they may go to the feast upon the banks of the Jordan.*
*The Spirit drives Christ, after the baptism, into the wrestling-ring.* <note>A metaphor for the spiritual struggle of the Temptation in the Wilderness.</note>
*The Jews send church envoys to John,*
*To ask who he is. They press him hard and strong.*
*He keeps himself humble, and, to establish his Master's honor,*
*Points them and everyone to the Lamb, who bears mankind's crime,*
*And crushes the power of the abyss, which plagues soul and body.*

THE dawn drove the dismal shadow away from everywhere.
The stars sank, and the sun rose from the horizon
Of the earth, with a brilliance of gold and diamonds,
And shone across the mountain, wilderness, and the water-banks
Of the country's stream, from whose chill, on its right side,
A vein flows in, which, passing by Jericho,
Refreshes the barren heath-border, deprived of cattle and fodder,
Until it flows down into the lap of its mother. <note>In poetic terms, the "mother" refers to the larger body of water—the Jordan River or the Dead Sea—into which the smaller stream flows.</note>

# JOHN.

Then John came toward the mouth of this brook,
Upon the bank of the Jordan, out of his secluded region,
And deep rock-cave, followed by so many people,
As early, before day, before dew, wish for his arrival,
Each equally eager to hear what the Spirit
Pours into his heart. One prays, and the other reads.
Another sings for joy; another weeps in sorrow.
<margin>A Simile</margin> Just as a farmer prepares himself early for 
His tillage, and hitches the ox with zest into the plow-yoke:
So John sets himself industriously to plow the land,
The uneven ground of hearts, for the pleasure
Of his Landlord <note>original: "lantheer." Here referring to God as the owner of the "field" of humanity.</note>, who shall sow the seed;
To break the soil with the blade and the coulter <note>A coulter is the vertical blade on a plow that cuts the earth before the plowshare turns it over.</note> of God's word;
To smother the thistle of vice, as is proper;
To set the meager soul right with the manure of discipline:
And to wash and sprinkle the body, long defiled,
So that, as soon as the All-Blesser <note>original: "d'alzegenaer." A poetic title for Christ as the source of all blessings.</note> appears here,
Man—likened to an overgrown desert
And arid wilderness—may change into an Eden,
A heavenly paradise, where God is worshipped
In the original integrity, just as the Creator made him,
Before he too recklessly lost his salvation and status.
The teacher, having climbed here to the top of the hill,
Looked out over the host that, gathered from all sorts of
Ways, could see and hear him where he stood.
At last they all seemed, as if from a single mouth,
To lift their voices from the depths up toward the hill,
While neither winds nor the rustling of trees disturbed them,
And the water drifts slowly along the foot of the hill.
<margin>Address of the audience, positioning themselves to be taught.</margin> "Faithful teacher, who firms and strengthens
The faint hearts, who can bruise the stubborn, and humbles
What vainly exalts itself; faithful shepherd, since
Yesterday you led and grazed us in a meadow
Of teaching, which carries the scent of the heavenly air,

### THE THIRD BOOK.

Kindled a desire in every heart, to live by your counsel.
We do not wish to oppose your will, or God’s will,
But willingly, following your lesson, leave the crooked track
That deviates from God’s highway. We pray, incline your ear
To each individual: for each feels his own burdens.
The failing is not the same for everyone. The lapse of good morals
Clings less to this person than to that one. Understanding
And judgment differ, and are ripe either early or late.
You, like a healing physician, know how to treat each nature
And sickness as required. To walk wisely 
In darkness, where no footprint was ever laid,
One needs a staff of certainty, and light of wise direction.
You, driven by a pure impulse of heaven’s spirit,
Are given to each as a staff and light from God.
Enlighten and support us then, as it pleases Him,
And serve the counsel-less first with this question:
What do you demand in general from these troubled troops,
Called from above by your voice to this region?

# J O A N N E S.

## 48

O brood of vipers <note>original: "adderengebroet"; a direct reference to Matthew 3:7, where John the Baptist rebukes the religious leaders for their insincerity.</note>, why do you feign your guilt?
The Knower of Hearts, who spared you with patience,
Sees through the hypocritical garment. What do you pride yourselves upon?
You fly the sail of pride at the masthead, and let it endure,
Now that the storm of His punishment roars and approaches,
And tosses the soul, until mast and prow crack.
An abyss gapes there, to swallow you alive.
What use is this boasting: "We are father Abraham's offspring,
Bred from his stem"? O reckless generation,
Why do you imagine that the Most High cares for ancestry?
He is not at a loss for sons of that father,
And can—for the Highest takes uncommon paths—
Raise up holy seed for Himself from barren rocks,
That resembles Abraham. This boasting comes too late.
The axe of punishment now threatens to strike tree and branch to dust,
To fell them down, and the trunk with shrub and root
To chop away completely, if the tree bears no fruit.
The fruitless foliage of the law, which pleases you,
Displeases the Landlord <note>A reference to God as the owner of the vineyard or world, coming to inspect the harvest.</note>, who comes to surprise the unfruitful,
And shall burn them to ashes in the eternal fire.
I seek to immerse the penitent in the <term>moist element</term> <note>A poetic circumlocution for water, specifically the water of baptism.</note>;
But He comes and approaches near here,
Whose sandal-strap this hand does not dare to venture,
Because of its unworthiness, to unbuckle or to tie.
This one shall immerse and wash you in that flood,
By sanctifying it, all at once in the glow
Of heaven’s spirit and power, which can destroy stains,
And deliver to God only purified and clean souls,
Refined by the ordeal. Thus they can stand
In the court, where none but the refined may enter.
Wake up: the judge comes. Now neither favor nor profit counts,
Nor lineage, nor blood. He has the <term>winnowing fan</term> <note>original: "graenwan"; a tool used to toss grain into the air so the wind blows the light chaff away, leaving the heavy grain behind.</note> of God's judgment
Ready in His hand, and shall, severe and stern,
Cleanse the <term>threshing floor</term>; the grain of virtue and the chaff

To separate the wickedness, to gather wheat into heaven's barn;
To consume the chaff in the fire, which endures even the years
Of eternity. Let everyone take heed.
Here the crown shines in the eye: there heaven's rod threatens you.

<margin>A Comparison</margin>
When the high court of the deity wishes to punish
The people's godlessness, and the violence of rising wickedness
Provokes heaven, then the Father, gentle by nature,
First shows His children the rod: a star with a tail, <note>A comet. In the seventeenth century, comets were widely interpreted as "prodigies" or omens of divine warning and impending disaster.</note>
As fiery red as blood, the harbinger of God's wrath:
Then all that lives is terrified, and makes itself heard mournfully
With groans and lamentation, tears upon the face:
Then each feels with remorse the neglect of their duty,
And seeks counsel from the wise and the godly,
Asking if God might spare the rod, and approach with mercy.

<margin>The fruit of John's teaching.</margin>
So it stands here, as soon as the messenger of repentance threatens them.
Each, kindled to penitence, and inclined
To turn away that fearful storm of the Most High,
Seeks advice, and begs, and wishes to learn from the teacher's mouth
The readiest means to escape such a plague:
Therefore he finally addresses the sorrowful ones thus.

<margin>He exhorts them to love and charity toward the needy.</margin>
It pleases me if you feel the point that grieved you.
Yesterday you heard how the chief commandment of love
Briefly encompasses the whole law and its prophets.
Now I point you toward no altar of atonement, that seeps
And floats with the blood of goats, and calves, and oxen. <note>John contrasts the "Old Law" of animal sacrifice with the "New Law" of internal transformation and social charity.</note>
You can best deliver your soul in distress through love,
And the work of charity shown to the needy.
In this one honors God, who exalts and crowns virtue.
If you have two coats: let the naked use one,
And feed the hungry bellies from your lap. <note>A direct paraphrase of Luke 3:11: "He that hath two coats, let him impart to him that hath none."</note>
Diminish your own needs, so that you may give more generously,
As one who lives less for yourself than for your neighbor.

When on a summer's day a breeze comes sailing by,
And blows lovely in a sea of ears of grain,

# JOHN.

## 50

<meta>catchword: As</meta>

Then the heavy ear of grain bows its head upon its stalk:
So the heart of the people bowed to this sweet sound
Of the messenger's voice, as if moved by the breath of God.
<margin>The penitence of some listeners, tax collectors,</margin>
The proud were seen bowed down to the earth.
The tax collector, who devours the people as rust eats iron,
And as cancer consumes the flesh, confesses his guilt with sorrow,
Seeks counsel, before punishment upon punishment overwhelms the cities,
And prays that he might wash him, and dip him in the water.
"Oh master," they all cry, "how shall we be saved?"
He answers: "Keep to the imperial law.
Be content with your wages, and do not tax the citizens
Beyond your commission. That is what it means to circumcise the heart."
<margin>and soldiers.</margin>
Soldiers, trained in wantonness and violence,
Arsonists, robbers in the cities and in the fields,
And ravishers of women stand bewildered and forsaken.
"Oh," they cry, "what counsel? How can we soldiers
Stand in the approaching judgment of the Anointed Head?" <note>A reference to the Messiah, the "Christos" or Anointed One, whom John heralds as the coming judge.</note>
He answers: "If you wish to be best advised,
Refrain from violence: be content with your pay,
Before God summons you to judgment for your tyranny."
<margin>John teaches and baptizes.</margin>
Thus John serves, according to the needs of each conscience,
Answering everyone’s question, and dips into the flood
The influx of people, gathered from all sides.
He comforts them sweetly, those who confess their guilt,
With the arrival of the 'Comfort of the Fathers' <note>A poetic title for the Messiah, the long-awaited hope of the Old Testament patriarchs.</note> near at hand,
Who salves all wounds, and who will overlook
Ignorance and error; washing away what was previously 
Done wrong, and blotting out and forgiving the debt.
With this he left the people, and dove into his cave,
While the shadows rose and the sun sank into the water.
<margin>God summons the council of heaven.</margin>
But above [where no night follows on the heels of day,
Where never dark mists nor driving rains fell
To obscure the light that shines and radiates eternally,
Wherein the spirit-realm draws free breath,]
