<meta>This page transitions from the earthly ministry of John the Baptist to a grand celestial scene. Having concluded the second book with the setting sun, the poet now opens the third book by lifting the reader's gaze to the highest heavens, where God gathers His angelic hosts for a divine council.</meta>

# BOOK THREE.

## 51

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Each</meta>

When the Supreme [Who drives all the starry circles
Around the earthly globe, and keeps them eternally bound
To their law of necessity, once and for all established,] <note>A reference to the "noodtwet" or natural law that governs the cosmos, reflecting the 17th-century view of a divinely ordered universe where celestial bodies move in fixed, eternal orbits.</note>
Had ascended to the summit of the heavens, accompanied
By Michael, the field commander, and a procession of attendants,
Who swirled around Him, before and behind.
He pondered something great, which gave everyone pause,
And immediately dispatched the imperial heralds
To summon the entire heavenly council from the four corners of the universe
To the court, which, fortified with diamond ramparts,
Rises in the center of heaven's circular ring.
They each make their way around the bright ring
Of eternity. One sees here the Dominions,
The Principalities, and the Powers <note>These are specific ranks within the traditional hierarchy of angels, as described by medieval theologians like Dionysius the Areopagite.</note> gliding upward
Through the pure celestial blue, each from his own court.
The field trumpet ahead sounds the praises of its King.
Robes wrap around their limbs—true heavenly attire—
Full of rainbows, richly fashioned from phoenix feathers, <note>The phoenix, a mythical bird that rises from its own ashes, was often used in Baroque poetry to symbolize incorruptibility, resurrection, and the exotic beauty of the divine realm.</note>
Set with pearls and sown with the finest gemstones.
The shining stars, set in fragrant hair, grant
The forehead a radiance and divine luster.
He who does not excel here seems dark in the eyes of another.
<margin>They assemble.</margin> With one accord they pass through the opened gates of heaven
To the council, and wait for the Deity, who comes forth
And seats Himself slowly upon a throne of Seraphim. <note>The Seraphim are the highest order of angels, often associated with light, heat, and burning love for God.</note>
Never before was joy seen to shine more beautifully
From those eyes, from which the sun alone draws its light;
The radiant garment of the Deity, sewn with the needle
Of the Cherubim and full of living suns,
Dazzled everything else that shone, outshone by such brilliance.
He brandished a scepter of mercy in His hand,
And surveyed the council, and prepared Himself
To begin, and to unfold to His faithful counselors
What He carried enclosed within His bosom.

# JOANNES.

## 52

Each heard attentively with longing, mute and still.
He revealed in the end His wise will thus:

<margin>God reveals that Christ will enter His office and commands His baptism to be celebrated with triumph.</margin>
You heavenly councils know how we, driven by our own delight, <note>The Dutch "lust" here refers to God’s benevolent desire or holy pleasure in creation.</note>
Created mankind, to share with them a fate and life,
And how they fell through a stain. <note>A reference to Original Sin.</note>
We nevertheless gave Abraham a law of his own,
Chose one lineage out of all other lineages,
And sent to them the Son of the Godhead, for whom they waited,
To save them in that miserable state.
John already shone, like a dawn,
Before our Son; he revealed to everyone, to our praise,
His arrival, and prepared an entrance, in the manner
Taught to him by the Spirit. Now the time has finally come
For God’s Anointed to faithfully fulfill His holy office.
Our purpose is to build and gain a church,
Which must begin first with our Son, the cornerstone,
And the body must grow from consecrated members—
The circumcised first, and then the uncircumcised— <note>Meaning both Jews and Gentiles will be included in the new Church.</note>
Washed of their stain in waters and streams,
Reborn through the Spirit. Thus they can enjoy
And inherit the same salvation granted to you here above.
That is the sole aim of our wisdom, and the point
Upon which the axis of heaven’s counsel turns. My Son below,
Until now in Nazareth (the least among David’s cities),
Has remained as if hidden, staying within the house
Of the royal virgin, His mother, eternally pure.
Today He shall approach the banks of the Jordan,
Where Zechariah’s son prepares the way and the entrance
For Him to the dominion He is to assume:
For Jesus, born of the stem of Jesse and the seed of David,
Is destined for David’s kingdom from all eternity.
As soon as the archangels hear the name of Jesus,
They worship and honor Him, and sing praise and glory,
And fall three times upon their faces out of awe.

### THE THIRD BOOK.

One sees the cherubim and seraphim covering themselves
With double wings; one hears them wake a choral song.
They sing: Holy, thrice holy is the Son;
The rising heir of God’s and David’s crown.
The Father, at the end, let Himself be heard again:
"We desire that the nine angelic choirs join us
In consecrating a feast, now that He steps forward
Toward the water’s edge as to a mirror, to allow Himself
To be immersed by John’s hand. Let that be a day of honor:
And it is right that we celebrate and triumph
In this Jerusalem, expecting a great number,
A harvest of souls, who shall make amends for the eternal fall
Of Lucifer and his rebels.
I expect a church, a bride, who under her feet
Sees the moon set, the dragon’s head crushed, <note>A reference to the Woman of the Apocalypse in Revelation 12, often interpreted as the Church or the Virgin Mary.</note>
Hears the abyss roar, while she, free from blemish
And clothed with the sun, seated upon my throne,
Shall forget her sorrows in the light of heaven."

<margin>*Christ travels from Nazareth to the Jordan.*</margin>
So spoke the Father, and the Son in Nazareth
Obeys this decree, and, offering a prayer
Up to heaven, like a golden bowl full of fragrance,
Steps forth, to now boldly raise His head,
And to the world—so shy of the long-promised light
Of truth—publicly unfold His duty,
To show that He is the Savior of the generations,
For whom the kings and God’s prophets wait,
And all the saints, who, awakened by the report,
Look out steadfastly, lamenting that His coming is delayed.
Wherever He set His feet and arrived, blessing seemed
<margin>*Nature seems to awaken, now that the Sun of Righteousness rises.*</margin>
To fall from the air: just as, after a rain,
Awaited with longing, and for which the parched meadow cried out,
The sun shines much more beautiful, and the grass grows so that it peeps. <note>The original "dat het piep" suggests growth so vigorous it seems to make a sound or happens in the blink of an eye.</note>
A spring of flowers paints the hills and the valleys.
No painter can depict meadow and landscape more beautifully,

### JOHN.

As he mixes a rainbow of a thousand colors.
The birds warble; the joyful lark brings
Its tones along, and follows the throat of the nightingales.
The spring refreshes the greenery with fresh streams of water.
The cedar bows its crown; nature sets her face
Toward joy, and changes into another state.
The winter storms, long thundering, blow over.
The bee, fond of the thyme, flower, and foliage,
Sucks honey from the dew; the sheepfold provides cream.
The hearts leap; joy knows no restraint.
The trumpet of salvation blew ahead through cities and lands,
And woke everyone with this voice: the Savior is at hand.

<margin>An influx of various peoples toward the Jordan.</margin>
The houses emptied; the fieldwork stood still.
One regarded neither poverty, nor favor nor disfavor,
Comfort nor discomfort; one comes striving on foot,
Another driven down the country's stream <note>The "country’s stream" refers to the Jordan River.</note> from the mountains.
One speeds by wagon, and another rides on horseback.
From Hermon they descend and hasten downward;
Those who make their living at the source of the river;
Those who turn over the soil of high Galilee and the lower lands,
And build with the plow; those who on Gennesaret <note>The Sea of Galilee.</note>
Constantly raft and sail, and outwit fish in the net,
Or reside in cities, or on the flat lands;
The people on Gilboa, where Saul fell into [enemy] hands,
And upon his own sword. <note>A reference to the death of King Saul in 1 Samuel 31, who committed suicide during a defeat by the Philistines.</note> From Samaria came
Whosoever was found ignited by no idolatrous flame:
Whatever dwells around the river on both shores
Moved up, and whatever wild things emerge from the forest,
Around the Dead Sea: whoever borders closest to Seir:
Whatever draws breath between Carmel's peak and Hebron.
All Jerusalem and its neighbors
Awaken at that call, and count the days and hours
To see the great light that rises from the east.
One follows the other, the dusty footprint points the way.

### THE THIRD BOOK.

The traveler finds the way, even where no roads are laid,
And fields lie trackless, choked with brush and hedge.
Each early strives to reach the gathering throng.

<margin>Christ comes out of Galilee through Samaria toward Quarantania. <note>Quarantania refers to the wilderness near Jericho, traditionally associated with the forty days of Christ's temptation.</note></margin>
The Savior, who spares no effort for the sake of man,
Having journeyed from Galilee and straight through Samaria,
Arrived in Judea, leaving now upon his right
Jerusalem—the seat of Aaron <note>The "seat of Aaron" refers to the high priesthood and the religious authority of the Temple.</note> and the throne
Where David ruled with scepter and with crown.
Then he crossed the heath and desert Quarantania,
And saw the messenger <note>John the Baptist, who acted as the herald or "messenger" of Christ.</note> upon the Jordan’s banks,
Immersing the people, looking round at the commotion,
Inwardly gladdened that he perceived the pleasant air
Of his Lord; and, with a heart distressed
By his own unworthiness, he prayed to God and went to meet him
With this speech, heard by the pressing crowd:

<margin>John meets him with this speech.</margin>
Emmanuel <note>original: "Emanuël"; a Hebrew name meaning "God with us."</note>, Son of God, all-creating eternal Word,
Redeemer, Mediator, who between God and man
Comes to mediate a peace for which all ages yearn;
Anointed one <note>A translation of the title "Christ" or "Messiah."</note>, and fountain of salvation, which shall forever flow;
How shall You release mankind—who, weighed down by Adam’s guilt
And their own crimes, would have borne their well-deserved
Punishment for eternity—from such a bond?
Had You not humbled Yourself from the Father’s rich embrace,
Donning a body, so that, patient, poor, and bare,
You might suffer, and cleanse Your people—burdened by Moses’ laws
And curses—from their stains through grace!
O Savior, whom I hailed from afar in my youth <note>A reference to John leaping in his mother Elizabeth's womb during the Visitation.</note>,
Do You humble Yourself so low toward me, convinced
Of my unworthiness? May the Father bless Your steps.
May my small power grow and increase upon Your prayers.
Forgive me that I do not humble myself low enough.
Thus he speaks, and worships that dear Lord in the dust,
And David’s descendant, who comes to rule the royal house,

### JOHN.

As he mixes a rainbow of a thousand hues,
The birds warble; the joyful lark adds
Its notes, following the throat of the nightingales.
The spring refreshes the greenery with fresh streams of water.
The cedar bows its crown. Nature sets her face
Toward joy, and transforms into another state.
The winter storms, long blustering, blow over.
The bee, eager for thyme, and flower, and foliage,
Sucks honey from the dew. The sheepfold yields cream.
The stags leap. Joy knows no restraint.
The trumpet of salvation blew ahead through towns and lands,
And woke everyone with this voice: the Savior is at hand.
<margin>An influx of various peoples toward the Jordan.</margin> Houses were left empty; fieldwork stood still.
No one looked to their needs, nor to favor or disfavor,
Neither ease nor unease. One comes hurrying on foot,
Another driven down from the mountains by the river's current.
One speeds by wagon, and another rides on horseback.
From Hermon <note>Mount Hermon, the highest point in the region, located at the northern border of Israel.</note> they descend, and hasten downward;
Those who earn their living at the source of the river <note>The Jordan River, which originates from the base of Mount Hermon.</note>;
Those who turn the high Galilee and the lower lands,
And build with the plow; those on Gennesaret <note>The Sea of Galilee, often called the Lake of Gennesaret in antiquity.</note>
Who constantly float and sail, and outwit fish in the net,
Or stay in cities, or on the flat plains;
The people on Gilboa, where Saul <note>Saul was the first king of Israel; according to the Bible, he committed suicide on Mount Gilboa during a lost battle against the Philistines.</note> fell into enemy hands,
And upon his own sword. From Samaria came
Whosoever found themselves ignited by no idolatrous flame:
Whatever hangs about the river on both its banks
Moved up, and whatever the wild knows how to raise in the woods
Around the Dead Sea; those who border closest to Seir <note>A mountainous region south of the Dead Sea, traditionally associated with the descendants of Esau.</note>;
Whoever draws breath between Carmel’s peak and Hebron.
All Jerusalem and her neighbors
Awaken at that call, and count the days and hours
To see the great light that rises out of the east.
One follows the other; the dusty footprint shows

### THE THIRD BOOK.

The traveler finds the way, even where no roads lie,
And the field remains untracked, full of thickets and hedges.
Each person strives eagerly to reach the place where the crowds gather densely.

<margin>*Christ comes from Galilee through Samaria toward Quarantania.*</margin>
The Savior, who does not spare himself for the sake of man’s salvation,
Having traveled out of Galilee and straight through Samaria,
Entered into Judea; he now left upon his right side
Jerusalem—the seat of Aaron <note>A reference to the high priesthood, as Aaron was the first High Priest of Israel.</note> and the throne
Where David once ruled with the scepter and the crown.
Then he crossed the heath and the wild Quarantania <note>Mount Quarantania, the traditional site in the Judaean Desert where Jesus was said to have fasted for forty days and been tempted by the devil.</note>,
And saw the messenger <note>John the Baptist.</note> on the banks of the Jordan,
Baptizing the people, who looked around at the rumors;
John rejoiced inwardly that he perceived the pleasant presence
Of his Lord, and, his heart troubled
By his own unworthiness, he prayed to God and stepped toward Him
With these words, heard by the press of the people:

<margin>*John greets him with these words.*</margin>
Emmanuel, Son of God, all-creating eternal Word,
Redeemer and mediator, who, between God and man,
Comes to broker a reconciliation for which all ages have longed;
Anointed One, and source of salvation, who shall flow forever;
How shall you save mankind, which—oppressed by Adam's guilt
And its own crimes—was never released from such a bond,
And would have borne its justly deserved punishment for eternity,
Unless you humbled yourself from the Father's rich bosom,
Clothed yourself in a body, so that you might patiently, poor and naked,
Suffer—and cleanse your people, burdened by Moses’ laws
And curses, from their stains through your grace!
O Savior, whom I cheered from afar so early on,
Do you humble yourself so low toward me, though I am convinced
Of my own unworthiness? May the Father bless your steps.
Let my small power grow and increase through your prayers.
Forgive me that I do not humble myself low enough.
So he speaks, and worships that dear Lord in the dust,
This descendant of David who comes to rule the ancestral house,
Closing the gate <note>original: "den draeiboom fluiten," literally "whistling the turnstile," a metaphor for John acting as the final point or "closer" of the long era of Old Testament prophecy.</note> of the long-lasting era of the prophets.

To suspend the bloody slaughter and burnt offerings <note>A reference to the transition from Old Testament animal sacrifices to the spiritual grace of the New Testament.</note> of cattle,

But Jesus spoke to this: enough of my will.
Fulfill the office with which you are charged, and remain silent:
Though you judge yourself unworthy of this work:
It is fitting for me first—who shall serve as an example
And mirror for the whole world—to step forward in obedience.
Where the head leads well, it is more willingly followed
By all other members, in the strengthening of God’s laws.
Thus a teacher best washes the student of his stains.

<margin>John sets himself to obey.</margin>
John thereupon bowed himself three times, worshipped him,
And went before Jesus. Countless souls stand
Gripped by wonder at these two.

Out of his place, he seems to sink below the level

Open on high, in honor of that King,
High Priest, and Prophet <note>These represent the "Threefold Office" of Christ: King, Priest, and Prophet.</note>. A heavenly vision

Greeted the Word-made-flesh, the son of the virgin.

Into the air in a radiance and reflection. Thus seemed
The Spirit to anoint the head of the holy generations.

# JOANNES. H

That you gave passage to the twelve tribes;
Elijah, whose chariot gallops to the stars,
And Elisha, his pupil, who let you step with dry feet
Upon your glassy floor; that you the rotting limbs
Of Naaman, the Syrian and royal prince,
Bathed so pure, and stripped the leprous crust
From his stinking body in your pools of water:
But now that the Purest has let himself be washed in your spring,
And the holy body of the Anointed, never defiled,
Has, by touching you, through the Word sanctified 
The watery element, as far as it flows around 
The globe of the earth and through its veins, no one 
Can speak of your honor according to its worth: for the Nile, and the Danube,
The king of rivers; and the Araxes, which can suffer neither 
Bridle nor bridge <note>A classical allusion to Virgil’s *Aeneid* (VIII, 728: *pontem indignatus Araxes*), describing a river so fierce it destroys any bridge built over it.</note>; the Rhine, and the Ganges, from which the Moors <note>In the 17th century, "Moors" was a general term used for people from Africa or India; here it refers to those living along the Ganges.</note>
Drink, shall listen silently to your wonders—
How you, in your sanctified ford, can bathe many thousands 
Pure of their blemish, in both soul and body.
The <term>Sacrament</term> <note>Original: *Geheimnisteken*, literally "mystery-sign," referring to the Sacrament of Baptism.</note> that can give rebirth to mankind,
Shall serve as a livery for the reborn throngs,
Turning marble over time into a font
Which—clearer than the Sea <note>The "Brazen Sea," a large bronze basin in Solomon’s Temple used for the ritual washing of priests (1 Kings 7:23-25).</note> that stood in God’s temple
Upon twelve oxen to cleanse the priesthood—
Washes the emperors pure from the cause of all plagues:
The guilt of Adam, and the actual guilt of each man;

<margin>The Holy Spirit drives Christ to the desert, where He, after forty days of fasting, is tempted and remains the victor.</margin>
Though the abyss roars against this sign of grace,
Which circumcises not the flesh, but the heart, so long contested
And piteously oppressed by many desires.
The Spirit drives Jesus out from the press of the people, alone,
To arm himself with fasting and prayers,
In the desolate wilderness, among mindless beasts,
Where snakes hiss and specters hover about him.
Here he follows Moses and Elijah, to the praise of God,
Eating no food for forty days and nights <note>Original: *etmael*, a period of 24 hours.</note> in his solitude.

# THE THIRD BOOK.

After which hunger begins to torment and tempt the heart.
The spirit <note>Refers to Satan.</note>, who first came to entice Adam through eating,
Now attacks the second man <note>A theological reference to Jesus Christ as the "Second Adam" who succeeds where the first failed.</note> three times fiercely;
But is also three times by the invincible Word
So vigorously turned away, that he must retreat:
Whereupon archangels, from heavenly palaces,
Greet that victorious hero after that battle,
And serve at his beck and call continually night and day;
While John taught the people on the other side,
Converted a multitude near Bethabara,
And immersed them in the stream, so that each heart, prepared,
Followed the Savior on the path of his obedience.

<margin>The Jews dispatch priests and Levites to John to ask who he is.</margin>
Thus laboring to fulfill his duty for Emmanuel <note>A Hebrew name meaning "God with us," referring to Jesus.</note>,
He sees a dark cloud of priests and Levites
Rising up from the stream straight toward the other side.
They land at last, and take their stand together,
Right before him, who awaited them, and asked the reason
For their coming: "For it appears by your clothing,
And outward countenance," he said, "that you, consecrated
To the altar, are priests or ministers of the sanctuary."
They answered: "If you deign to grant us your speech,
We, dispatched together from Jerusalem,
By order of the high priest and the whole Great Council <note>Original: *breeden raet*, referring to the Sanhedrin, the supreme judicial and religious assembly of the Jews.</note>,
Appear to hear from you in what state
And rank you place yourself: for all kinds of rumors,
Scattered everywhere through the land, do not yet fully
Reveal to us who you are; whether this Anointed Head,
Promised to the fathers so long ago through Moses's mouth,
As the people's redeemer; or a zealous Elijah,
The great harbinger of the coming Messiah.
We stand ready to recognize you according to the law,
In such a dignity as the Highest has placed you;
As it is just to give everyone their due honor.
Are you the Messiah, or Elijah, strict of life?"

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the delegation from the Sanhedrin (the Jewish high council) and John the Baptist. Following the previous page where the priests and Levites arrived at the Jordan River to question John's authority, this section captures his humble refusal to claim any messianic or prophetic titles for himself.</meta>

->### JOHN.<-

"Let us hear your words, so that we may fulfill our duty."
<margin>John's modesty in quietly declining offered honors.</margin> John listens, and fixes his eyes 
With modesty upon the ground, averse to the thought 
Of titles of honor, which exalt him too high, 
And might suppress Christ, who is already hated by the Pharisees. 
Ambition is usually inclined to draw benefit 
From the shadow of another’s name and honor. 
The hero, so that he might piously turn away this Pharisaism <note>A reference to the perceived hypocrisy or pride of the religious elite.</note>, 
Accepts the buckler <note>A small, round shield; here used metaphorically as a defense against pride.</note> of humility, stands firm, 
And answers: "Do not place me upon such a high step: 
I am not so great. I do not wish to hide my smallness, 
Nor present myself through words or thoughts as greater 
Than my true worth. To speak plainly, I am not that Christ <note>Meaning the Messiah.</note>, 
Nor do I seek that His honor should be diminished by my elevation." 
Yet they persist with questions: "Tell us how one 
Must regard you, and by what name you shall be called. 
Are you Elijah <note>Original: *Helias*. According to Jewish tradition based on the book of Malachi, the prophet Elijah was expected to return before the coming of the Messiah.</note>, who through the air was carried 
In a chariot: for so the rumor and the news of the land suggests." 
He says: "I am not he." They say: "We must know 
One thing or the other: are you one of God’s prophets?" 
John answers: "No." The youth, in truth 
A great prophet, desires to disdain this honor, 
And would much rather still obscure his own prestige 
Than tarnish Christ’s dignity and its brilliance. 
They say finally: "So that we may understand each other rightly, 
And we may depart with a clear report and conclusion 
To give an account to those who lawfully sent us here; 
What do you say of yourself, so that we may at last be released 
From our strict duty. Who are you? Speak plainly." 
<margin>He testifies humbly of himself and his office.</margin> Then John came to the final conclusion, 
And spoke: "I am the voice <note>Original: *galm*, meaning an echo, reverberation, or sound; a reference to Isaiah 40:3.</note> of God in these deserts, 
Only a bare voice, which shall quickly disappear: 
As Malachi’s <note>A prophet in the Hebrew Bible; the author is linking John's identity to Old Testament prophecy.</note> mouth, now centuries ago, 
Prophesied of me, who am a voice, and only [a voice]..."

De <meta>Catchword for the following page</meta>

<summary>John the Baptist is interrogated by a delegation from Jerusalem regarding his identity; he humbly denies being the Messiah, Elijah, or a prophet, identifying himself only as a "voice" crying in the desert as prophesied by the ancients.</summary>

### THE THIRD BOOK.

The forerunner of God’s Son, to whom I led everyone
Through that proclaimed cry: He comes now; let one prepare
The highway for the coming of God, the eternal Word.
Let the uneven places be leveled, and humble, as is proper,
The high hills, and raise the sunken valleys.
All humanity shall henceforth see God’s glory shine.

<margin>They persist, however, with questions.</margin>
They ask further: "If you are then not the Christ,
Nor Elijah, nor consecrated as a prophet;
How have you then presumed to take up this power,
That you dare to plunge the multitudes into the streams?" <note>Referring to the ritual of baptism in the Jordan River.</note>

<margin>He distinguishes his office from Christ’s office, which is so much more worthy, and turns them away from himself toward Christ.</margin>
John says, so that he may be excused in this:
"I wash the body only with water; but God’s Son,
Appearing in your midst—let yourselves be advised—
Shall bathe both body and soul from all stains.
My office reaches no further than that I, for God’s praise,
Point everyone away from me, only toward God the Savior;
A worthiness neither to be known nor found outside of Him,
And for whom I would fear even to loosen the shoelace." <note>John uses the metaphor of a lowly servant’s task to emphasize his inferiority to Jesus.</note>
Thus this conversation ends. So they part from him,
And return across the river back to Jerusalem.

<margin>Praise for John’s humility.</margin>
O rare example of humility! O wonder!
How many stars set before your rising,
You who, like a morning star, shine before Christ’s coming!
You rise as high as you sink through lowliness,
And allow neither the miter-crown <note>A reference to the ceremonial headdress of the high priesthood, to which John had a hereditary claim through his father.</note>, nor Aaron’s titles of honor—
All vanities—to charm or tickle your heart,
But you reject all the advantage offered to you,
And deem the Anointed One’s honor alone your own honor and crown.

<margin>The next day he points to Christ, the Lamb of God, the savior of mankind, and assures that this is God’s Son.</margin>
When he, the next day, burned again with zeal,
And saw Jesus, who approached him at a favorable hour,
Zechariah’s son pointed to the Light that came toward him,
And spoke: "Ah, behold, that is the God-consecrated Lamb,
Doomed to the slaughter-bench <note>An allusion to the sacrificial altar; John identifies Jesus as the ultimate sacrifice.</note>: according to the cry of Isaiah.
The Passover lamb has for so long pointed only toward God the Messiah,

<meta>This page continues the narrative of John the Baptist's testimony. Having previously established his role as a voice in the wilderness, John now identifies Jesus as the sacrificial Lamb of God and addresses the growing competition between his own followers and those of Jesus.</meta>

# JOHN

A Passover lamb, that frees you from the angel’s sword, <note>A reference to the Exodus story where the blood of a lamb protected the Israelites from the Angel of Death.</note>
And atones for the world’s misdeeds. No priest, no Levite,
Nor all those sacrificial cattle can wash you of your stains,
Nor I, by plunging you into these pools of water:
But Christ, who bears the burden of all the world,
Is the only one whose blood wipes your heart clean of stains.
That shall be clear to you, when He with opened veins
Pours out a fountain of blood, and, as a price and atonement for the Father,
Offers Himself on the mountain with His final voice.
The Spirit of divinity descended from a cloud upon Him,
In the appearance of a dove, and strengthened Him with this sign
That He is God’s Son, who shall never lack the power
To purify the soul through the fire of heaven’s Spirit.
John, who could not be won by favor, nor was he afraid
Of hatred, persisted day after day in testifying thus,
Therefore his disciples bow before the world’s Savior,
And follow Him as He travels toward Galilee.
Thus His name spread steadily through all the cities.

<margin>*Christ, having traveled to Galilee and returning to Judea, baptizes through His disciples.*</margin>
While John was steadily bathing people in Aenon, <note>Aenon near Salim was a site mentioned in the Gospel of John as a place where John the Baptist performed baptisms because "there was much water there."</note>
And pointed the people from his bath to the washing-bath of grace,
Signified by all the purifications of Moses' law,
Jesus came again from the Galilean territory,
Whereupon the crowds, eager for salvation, overwhelmed Him;
They let themselves be plunged into the water by His disciples.
Thus the following of the Savior grew mightily.

<margin>*John’s disciples are resentful that Christ increases in honor and following: but John...*</margin>
John’s disciples, burdened with their master’s honor,
Stir up a dispute over which of the two
Purifies people most purely: but the priest’s son <note>John the Baptist was the son of Zechariah, a Jewish priest.</note> settled
The mutual quarrel, and shows the disciple
That he received no higher power from a higher hand:
That he is no head, before whose power one must bow,
But one who shines before, to testify of the source-light: <note>original: "moederlicht", literally "mother-light," referring to the ultimate source of light/divinity.</note>
That he is the companion, and not the bridegroom,
Whose rise rejoices him. The disciple stands silent.

## THE MESSENGER OF REPENTANCE.
### The fourth book.

*Wary of the Pharisees, swollen with hatred and envy.*

*Visits the desert-dweller, who punishes the blood-shaming. <note>original: "bloetschoffeeren." This refers to the illicit and incestuous nature of Herod Antipas's marriage to Herodias, his sister-in-law, which John the Baptist famously condemned.</note>*

*John stills the people, to prevent misfortune,*

*Is strictly imprisoned, which appalls the whole land.*

<margin>Lucifer, terrified by Christ's ascent, summons all the hellish powers.</margin>
Hell was appalled by such a word of thunder.
All the abyss trembled. The rusty iron gate

The grand prince of the night, fearful for his state,
Immediately summoned all subterranean councils,
Which hurried to the court through serpent-winding paths,

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

There, right in the center of the earth, equidistant
From the south and north poles, the court hangs on chains, and cuts
The axis of the world exactly into two equal parts.
What is gruesome flows here through a thousand black throats
Toward the harsh blast of the grim court trumpets.
The god Lucifer appeared at the council, and then sat
Upon the high throne, for which the subject necks
Of vermin and dragons serve as prop and support.
He wrapped a crown of adders around his misshapen head,
And, brandishing the staff of steel, split at the point,
He cast glowing glances. The lamp, full of pitch and sulfur,
And basilisk <note>A mythical reptile said to be the king of serpents, whose breath or gaze could kill.</note> fat, illuminated all around the navel
Of the wild council-den, thick and fatly overgrown with soot.
The seating was occupied by this cursed brood,
Each according to his rank. They sat still, as if mute,
And he began to rumble thus, like a bell.

<margin>Simile</margin>
Just as a clock weight drives the snail wheel <note>A "sneckrad" or fusee wheel, part of an early modern clock mechanism used to equalize the pull of the weight or spring.</note>,
The snail wheel drives the center wheel; the center wheel, strengthened
In its faster pace, comes to strike the crown wheel;
The crown wheel, by its momentum, drives the escape wheel,
And the escape wheel keeps the balance in constant motion back and forth:
So does the care of state now keep the royal lord
And the priestly head in unrest; and the turmoil
Continues its course night and day: each man deliberates how to bolster 
His seat of power against the approaching danger that he imagines
<margin>Caiaphas warns the Sanhedrin <note>The supreme council and tribunal of the Jews during the Roman period.</note> to stop Christ and John.</margin>
Is looming. Caiaphas, pressed by anxiety, commands
The Great Temple Council to watch the maneuvers
Of the newcomers, who contradict the book of the Law,
Who grow in number and roam through city and country day after day,
Overshadowing the authority of the fathers with their new teaching—
And through a show of holiness, each in his own fashion;
This one <note>John the Baptist</note>, by being strict in his conduct and abstaining
From food and wine; that one <note>Jesus</note>, through his familiarity
With all sorts of people, easily misleading them
With the appearance of miracles and the healing of their ailments.
He urges them to take heed, not to stray recklessly
From the Emperor’s favor, and to look only to him <note>The Roman authority, specifically Caesar.</note>, 
The one authorized to command, and to no other head.
It was still fresh in their memories how, when they hoped for the best,
Pompey’s <note>Pompey the Great, the Roman general who captured Jerusalem in 63 BC.</note> army demolished gate and wall and towers:

<page-num>69</page-num>
# THE FOURTH BOOK. #

And though Pilate paid no heed to Levi's <note>Likely a representative of the priestly tribe or the Sanhedrin.</note> complaint,
He stayed within, and secured his guard,
Quietly noting down, by order of the majesties,
The Council's <term>Sanhedrin</term> <note>The supreme religious and legal assembly in Jerusalem.</note> error, which would be troublesome to defend before the emperor.

<margin>The council in Jerusalem is divided. Christ avoids the traps of the Pharisees and Lawyers. Herod plots against John. A Simile.</margin>
So they struggle amongst themselves in high Jerusalem.
The council sits half divided, and anxious, not daring to give
Its voice to seize the son of priest Zechariah,
Regarded as a great prophet; and the Messiah
Avoids their traps time and again in Galilee,
Herod's realm, or retreats elsewhere,
To bring light to the people, plunged in darkness,
And to edify them with his miracles and teachings.
The king, who does not rest, spies for an opportunity
To catch this wilderness-dweller, having waited a while,
And to get him into his clutches with a fair pretense:
Just as the cunning fox stalks a nest of turtle doves,
And unexpectedly snatches away their innocence.

<margin>Herod arrives with Herodias to capture John where he teaches and baptizes. A Simile.</margin>
The wilderness-dweller <note>John the Baptist.</note> continues the task he has undertaken,
And reaches the bank of the Jordan on the far side,
Where Prince Herod borders on rocky Arabia,
The realm of Aretas <note>Aretas IV, King of the Nabataeans and former father-in-law to Herod Antipas.</note>. The son-in-law, spurred on
By the Pharisees just at the right moment, comes with Herodias
To meet and greet the divine hero,
Who prostrates himself thrice in the dust at their feet,
With his disciples, in the midst of the people,
Gathered from everywhere, surrounding God's teacher with a cloud
Of thousands: just as a swarm of bees
Clings to its king <note>In the 17th century, the leader of a bee colony was often mistakenly identified as a "king" rather than a queen.</note> and presses upon him from all sides,
Where buckwheat blooms, or in an oak forest.

<margin>Herod speaks to him alone.</margin>
Antipas with his beloved steps down from the court carriage,
And beckons John out from the thick press of the crowds
To the side, where an oak, very high and thick with leaves,
Shields the hot sun and spreads a cool shadow.
The courtly train withdraws, and leaves them alone for a while.

    *   *Apollion*: The "Destroyer" (Abaddon), here appearing as a demonic tempter/ghost.
    *   *Clockwork terminology*: Sneckeradt (Fusee), Bodemradt (Main wheel), Kroonradt (Crown wheel), Strijckradt (Escape wheel/contrate wheel), Onrust (Balance wheel/pendulum). This is a very elaborate 17th-century simile.
    *   *Piepend gras*: Literally "squeaking/whistling grass." Likely refers to the early growth of something that needs to be cut down before it matures, or simply the ground under them.
    *   *Herout en voorbode*: John the Baptist (the herald) and Jesus.
    *   *Pompeius*: Pompey the Great, who took Jerusalem in 63 BC.

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

And though Pilate did not concern himself with Levi’s complaint,
He stayed within, and secured his guard,
Quietly noting down, by order of the majesties,
The Council’s mistake, making it difficult to plead his case before the Emperor.

Held to be a great prophet; and the Messiah

Evades their snares time after time in Galilee,
The realm of Herod, or goes elsewhere,
To bring light to the people, plunged in darkness,
And to edify them with his miracles and teachings.

The king, who does not rest, watches for an opportunity
To catch this desert-dweller <note>John the Baptist, known for living in the wilderness.</note>, now long-awaited,
With a fair pretext into his clutches:

The realm of Aretas <note>King Aretas IV of Nabataea, whose daughter Herod Antipas had divorced to marry Herodias.</note>. The son-in-law, arriving just in time

In its faster course, comes to stir the crown wheel;

Meanwhile, the religious authorities in Jerusalem are in a state of indecision and fear regarding the growing influence of both John and Jesus.</meta>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

And though Pilate did not heed Levi's complaint, <note>Levi refers to the priestly tribe or representatives of the Sanhedrin; Pilate ignores their initial legal maneuvering.</note>
He stayed inside and secured his guard,
Quietly recording, by order of the imperial majesties,
The Council's error, which would be difficult to defend before the Emperor.

<margin>In Jerusalem the council sits divided.</margin>
Thus they wrestle among themselves in high Jerusalem.
The council sits half-divided and anxious, and dares not
Give its voice to seize the son of the priest Zechariah, <note>John the Baptist, whose father was the priest Zechariah.</note>
Regarded as a great prophet; and the Messiah
<margin>Christ evades the snares of the Pharisees and Scribes.</margin>
Evades their snares time after time in Galilee,
Herod’s realm, or retreats elsewhere,
To enlighten the people, who are plunged in darkness,
And to edify them with his miracles and teachings.
<margin>Herod lies in wait for John.</margin>
The king, who does not rest, watches for an opportunity
To get this desert-dweller, now awaited for a while,
Into his clutches with a fair pretext ::
<margin>Simile</margin>
Just as the cunning fox lurks by a nest of turtledoves
And unexpectedly betrays their innocence.
The desert-dweller continues his accepted task,
And reaches the bank of the Jordan on the other side,
Where Prince Herod’s land borders rocky Arabia,
The realm of Aretas. <note>Aretas IV, the King of Nabataea, whose daughter Herod Antipas had divorced to marry Herodias.</note> The son-in-law, just at the right time
<margin>Herod comes with Herodias to capture John, where he teaches and baptizes.</margin>
Urged by the Pharisees, comes with Herodias
To meet and greet the divine hero,
Who throws himself three times into the dust at their feet,
With his disciples, in the midst of the people
Gathered from everywhere, surrounding God's teacher like a cloud
<margin>Simile</margin>
Of thousands: just as a swarm of bees
Clings to its king, and from all sides
Heaves about him, where buckwheat blooms or in an oak forest.
<margin>Herod speaks to him alone.</margin>
Antipas with his beloved descends from the court carriage,
And beckons John out of the thick press of the crowds
To the side, where a very tall oak, thick with leaves,
Shields the hot sun and spreads a cool shadow.
The court retinue withdraws and leaves them for a while in solitude.

## JOHN.

->They seat themselves upon a field-carpet of sod.<-
The youth, although they respectfully invite him
To sit, refuses it, and, with his right hand
Leaning firmly on a staff, holds his ground before them.
Then the king began, most graciously and amiably:
<margin>Herod's flattering speech.</margin> "O heavenly prophet, how lovely, how pleasing
Did your speech, countenance, and teaching satisfy the ear,
Eye, and judgment, when we came to hear
Your lessons, more than once, in the wilderness;
Yet you remain unknown to the common people, who, like a burr, <note>original: "klisse"; a sticky seed-case. The metaphor implies the people cling to John's every word and movement.</note>
Constantly cling to you, and, purified and converted,
Honor your holy face as if it were the face of an angel.
Vices diminish; golden morals prevail.
One sees the pious embrace you and love you,
Just as the wicked—whom you do not spare and whose faults
You never overlook—fear you, while you crown virtue with honor.
We have wished for a while to speak with you in person.
What is the reason that you are compared to Elijah? <note>original: "Helias"; the Hebrew prophet Elijah, who was expected to return before the Day of the Lord.</note>
The whole land speaks and thunders with your fame.
Did he rise again in your appearance, or do you carry Elijah’s name?
Or are you a Samuel, or rather Isaiah,
Comforting the people with the coming of the Messiah,
Who, in the name of the Most High, after so much misfortune,
Would one day slide the foreign yoke <note>A reference to the Roman occupation of Judea.</note> from their necks?
Inform us, so that we, peaceful and undeceived,
May rightly honor you, according to your status, with true knowledge."
<margin>John's modest answer, instruction, and rebuke of the father and son.</margin> John, highly enlightened by the spirit of divinity,
Sees clearly through the pretense, and beams with his gaze
Into the heart of the hypocrite—a nest full of hellish snakes—
Who is plotting to catch the hero with a semblance of justice,
And to find material in the man’s speech
Through which this matter might be glossed over before the people.
He answers, nonetheless, directly and undaunted:
"Unworthily has God bestowed that title upon me,"

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between John the Baptist and King Herod Antipas. John responds to the King’s flattery and his questions about whether John is the reincarnated prophet Elijah. John begins to explain his true mission: preparing the way for the Messiah, while simultaneously confronting Herod with the bloody legacy of his father, Herod the Great.</meta>

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

And honored with the name of Elijah in the ancient pages.
It suits me then to keep the strict and righteous path of Elijah,
To hold it, without bending justice for favor or for hate.
I work no miracles: but many wondrous deeds testify
To Elijah’s greatness. He fought against idolatry,
And hid from the tyranny of Jezebel and Ahab,
In caves and mountain caverns, as if buried alive,
Fed by angels and hungry ravens. <note>John references the story from the biblical Book of Kings, where the prophet Elijah was miraculously fed by ravens while hiding from the corrupt King Ahab.</note>
So I also hid, while still a child, with my father in a cave,
Where my angel fed me, when your lord father—mad
And raging—did not shrink from felling innocent children’s souls
By the sword, in order to destroy the King <note>A reference to the "Massacre of the Innocents." According to the Gospel of Matthew, Herod the Great ordered the execution of all male children in Bethlehem to eliminate the infant Jesus.</note>
Who was honored by the wise men from the east
In a poor stable: yet one who rules over all
Protected David’s son, <note>original: "Davids zoon"; a messianic title referring to Jesus as the descendant of King David.</note> who did not yearn for earthly dominions
Or flaming crowns: since He, in order to suffer,
Clothed Himself in a body, and, descending from the Father’s bosom,
Wishes to honor the Father, obedient unto death.
And I, bound to God, strive for no status or praise,
Nor seek anything else than to point all people toward Him,
To confirm that He is anointed as <term>High Priest</term> <note>original: "aertspriester"; literally "arch-priest," referring here to the Messiah's spiritual authority rather than a political office.</note> and prophet
And king, and now comes forward,
To redeem everyone through His blood, to unfold God’s will,
And to build an eternal kingdom in the hearts of men.
A line of kings longed for this era.
Now He comes as a lamb, who hereafter, as a lion,
Shall roar from on high, from a throne of clouds,
For the punishment of the circumcised and uncircumcised nations. <note>A common scriptural way of referring to both the Jewish people (the circumcised) and the Gentiles (the uncircumcised).</note>
What did your father not decree in his time!
He spared neither high nor low, consecrated nor unconsecrated,
Neither tribe nor lineage, blind in his murdering and destruction
Of Aaron’s offspring <note>The priestly lineage of the Israelites, descending from Moses' brother Aaron.</note> and of David’s blood and descendants,
A hereditary enemy of the Hebrews, from his mother’s womb,
Until he went into his grave amidst the outcry of the cities.

# JOHN.

<margin>He is well received and seated at the table, but remains temperate.</margin>
Full of courage despite his capture, men came to greet John,
And, by the king’s command, reverently washed his feet.
Then the meal was served, and he, as the king’s friend
And God’s prophet, was honored and given the seat of high status,
Served from silver platters with delicacies of the court
And wild game: but this honor could take little hold of him.
He never set his mouth to the food, the banquet, or the wine,
And carried himself in the palace as if it were a wilderness.
His drink was water, and a bit of honeycomb was his food.
Thus the <term>desert-dweller</term> <note>original: "woeſtijnier"; a reference to John’s ascetic lifestyle in the Judean wilderness before his arrest.</note> followed his old ways even in the court.

<margin>Herodias conceals her hatred.</margin>
The Queen, who sat directly opposite the King and was deeply
Offended, showed him her resentment through neither gesture
Nor word: but she bit her tongue and entertained
The youth politely. Whatever radiated from her eyes
Appeared to be affection and friendship. He modestly turned
His gaze aside or downward. That godly presence either edifies
Or provokes this <term>adulteress</term> <note>original: "boelſchap"; implying her scandalous marriage to her husband’s brother, which John had publicly condemned.</note> in her remorseful conscience:
For possessed by that foul spirit of adultery,
And mad with blind revenge and unyielding spite,
The hero's restraint seems to her a silent reproach
Of how many scandals she has linked together, like a chain.

<margin>Herod dines with him and asks questions.</margin>
Herod asked about the lives of the prophets,
Mostly about Elijah: <note>original: "Helias"; the Greek form of the Hebrew name Elijah.</note> how he vanished into the air,
Ascended to heaven, and never appeared as a living man again:
How Jezebel sought him through all kinds of snares.
The youth served him by answering his questions piously.
Thus the hours passed as the night approached.

<margin>Description of the dungeon.</margin>
A round tower rises, surrounded by a triple moat,
Near the <term>Dead Sea</term>, <note>original: "doode meer"; the salt lake bordering the Judean desert, near where the fortress of Machaerus—the traditional site of John’s imprisonment—was located.</note> about a hundred rods
From the court, where many a head, falsely accused, had to bleed.
Herod had built it as a dungeon from the ground up
Of blue ashlar. <note>original: "blaeu arduin"; a high-quality, dense blue-grey limestone used in prestigious 17th-century Dutch architecture, here used to describe the king's fortification.</note> The air that rose from the Dead Sea
And entered through the bars was unhealthy.
Birds of prey avoided it, lest they catch a plague in their bodies,

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<margin>Herod orders John to be led to the dungeon.</margin>
As shadows began to nestle in the walls, the meal came to an end. 
"Steward," says the prince, "conduct my table-guest 
To some quiet chamber: for this man, chosen by God, 
Seems born more for solitude than for the court."
The torch is lit. John, at the side 
Of the royal steward, follows the light calmly and gladly. 
Straight toward that tower they go. The copper door creaks open. 
He enters the dungeon, where snails and toads crawled, 
And where only a rush mat covers and shields his naked limbs 
As he lies on a bundle of straw, protecting him from the cold. 
The dungeon is guarded by fearsome dogs, 
And sentries, keeping their rounds hour by hour. 
The disciples stand the next day before the gate 
And the iron bars; they see with heavy hearts their master, who rots 
Amidst stench and mold, yet they are afraid to speak a word. 
Thus one once saw Joseph, cast into a <term>jail</term> <note>original: "giool"; an archaic term for a prison or dungeon.</note> long ago, 
Entirely poor and comfortless, yet richly comforted in God. 
O ambition, lewd lust, O Pharisaic pack!

<margin>A simile, depicting the rumor of John’s capture.</margin>
Just as a thunderbolt comes rolling with a roar, 
Shaking mountains and towers, setting cities to flight, 
And striking a hundred thousand in a single moment 
With dismay, bewildering them and leaving them deathly afraid: 
So the news startled from their dreams all who were in doubt. 
The cry of the land bursts forth: John is captured.

<margin>The differing judgments of men regarding this matter.</margin>
Herodians, Essenes, and Pharisees 
Forget their old quarrels and melt into one, 
To quiet the land and give this horror a fair appearance. 
Men strive, ask, and wail: what crime has the man committed? 
One hears hypocrisy, how it barks against him: 
"Had he rebuked his majesty more prudently, 
As <term>Nathan</term> <note>A reference to the prophet Nathan, who used a parable to confront King David about his adultery with Bathsheba and the murder of Uriah, rather than a direct attack.</note> did David, though David was guilty of two scandalous acts— 
Both murder and adultery—Herod would have patiently 
Excused the youth. How did it suit a prophet, 
To bite the king so sharply to his face?"

And his queen? Can the court swallow this bitter morsel?
The common folk would take offense with gravity and good reason.
He wins a king’s heart, who punishes and edifies with virtue.
The Savior, returning from Judea, yields
To this rumor, and returns to Galilee, out of the sight
Of the lawyers <note>The "wetgeleerden" refers to the Scribes and Pharisees, the experts in Jewish Law who were often in conflict with Jesus.</note>, great in prestige and power.

<margin>A simile, depicting the inequality between John and Christ in their labors.</margin>
When the morning star promises the day to mankind
Before the mother-light, which it announces, then
The rising light gradually extinguishes its fire through stronger rays.
The sun rises from the east, to descend again in the west,
Enlightening all eyes, warming, awakening, and giving soul
To what lay parched and dead, buried, and destroyed.
At times a cloud or mist shields its radiance:
Yet it breaks through again, and gradually appears
With a more beautiful luster, and draws all living things,
Whatever has eyes in its head, spontaneously and gently to itself.
It keeps a steady course throughout the bounds of heaven,
And does not rest from steadily procreating and working,
Until it perfectly completes its circle at the end,
And closes the starry belt, as far as the highway of heaven extends,
Assigned to it by God's providence, for the benefit 
Of mankind; a goal as useful as any heart could wish.

<margin>Depicting the rising of John and Christ, and their works.</margin>
It went with the Word's herald and the Word <note>The "Word" (Dutch: *het Woort*) refers to Jesus Christ, based on the Prologue of the Gospel of John: "In the beginning was the Word."</note> in this manner;
A small and a larger light, to the honor and praise of heaven,
Rising to enlighten the souls of the world.
The echo of the forest <note>original: "bosgalm"; a poetic reference to John the Baptist as the "voice crying in the wilderness" whose message echoes through the land.</note> shines ahead, but must yield to the arrival
Of the eternally shining Word, from which he borrows his light,
And by whose rising he is extinguished and diminished.
The Savior meanwhile travels through lands and through cities,
Wandering now high, then low again,
To lead everyone to God through miracles and teaching;
Though his teaching, clouded and mocked,
Must suffer from the wicked and the hypocritical.
He is the ear of the deaf, the clear eye of the blind,

# JOHN
## THE MESSENGER OF PENITENCE.
### The fifth book.

#### C O N T E N T S.

->*Herodias sternly demands punishment for the man of the desert.*<-
->*Herod keeps him closely confined. The teacher in the prison*<-
->*Sends two disciples to seek a message from Christ.*<-
->*They ask who He is. You see the miracle-worker,*<-
->*Says Christ. Go, witness all that you see and hear.*<-
->*He exalts John: this vexes the temple-consecrated <note>The "temple-consecrated" refers to the high priests and religious authorities who opposed both John and Jesus.</note>,*<-
->*Who push for the death penalty through the servant Malchus <note>In Vondel's epic, Malchus acts as a messenger or tool for the religious leaders; he is the same figure who later has his ear cut off in Gethsemane.</note>,*<-
->*At the birthday feast where the princes rejoice,*<-
->*The father, after the daughter’s dance, amidst the drinking,*<-
->*Confirms with an oath to grant her desire.*<-
->*She demands the prisoner’s head. The executioner struck*<-
->*It off in the prison. She serves it for the lords,*<-
->*On a platter, to her lord father at the table,*<-
->*Where everyone mocks the head and is godlessly merry.*<-

<margin>A Parable,</margin>
Just as a festering wound <note>original: "borstquetzuur"; literally a "chest-wound-sour," referring to a deep, neglected abscess or ulcer.</note> in the breast, neglected and deeply sore,
By a physician <term>physician: the original uses 'heelmeester' (healing master) and later 'wondenmeester' (wound master), common 17th-century terms for surgeons</term> who wishes to turn back the cancerous evil
And stop its spread, is touched more firmly by the hand,
So much more sensitive is the pain and the burden
Of the sick woman, who shrinks from being healed with pain,
And is too impatient to follow the healing commands
Of the master of wounds for her own salvation.
<margin>Portrayal-</margin>
Thus it went with Herodias, wicked, proud, and lustful,

<meta>This page continues the narrative of John the Baptist’s imprisonment, focusing on the psychological and political motivations of Herodias. It picks up from the previous page's introduction of her resentment toward John for his condemnation of her marriage to Herod.</meta>

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>The disposition of Herodias, since John rebuked Herod; Parables.</margin>
She who suffered under a wound of incest,
And, touched by a soul-healer <term>soul-healer: a translation of 'zielheeler,' referring to John the Baptist’s role as a physician of the spirit through his calls for repentance</term> in the wilderness,
By the remedy <note>original: "middelvloeckt"; literally 'middle-curse,' a 17th-century term for a harsh rebuke or a medicine that causes pain while curing.</note> that heals an obedient heart.
Rightfully hated by her true husband, she fears
Lest the false one might kick her from the court in shame;
Thus revenge broods in her heart: just as constant dripping
Hollows out the stone: and like a smoldering fire
First smokes, then flames, and in the end, at the people’s cry,
Sets a palace and entire city ablaze, so that no streams
Can tame the flames rising to the sky.
She holds the punisher fast and sure in her claws:
But she regrets that he still lives in the dungeon’s darkness,
Amidst hardship and stench; she deems, to save herself,
A thirst for revenge higher than the pleasures of the bed.
Neither opportunity nor time does she neglect, and speaks by night

<margin>She urges Herod for revenge at night.</margin>
To her love, while a fit of passion overpowers him:
"I have suffered so much scorn for you and your love,
And never asked my bedfellow for a single gift.
You know how deep a wound I carry in my heart.
If the beauty of this body pleases you,
And the many delights enjoyed through my grace:
I have, for your sake, cast away my name and fame,
My husband and father, and all my father's house and blood
Made my enemies. Such was the power of a stinging glow
Of love: and do you still allow [to your eternal shame]
That a wild man dares to reproach me to my face
With how I, your concubine <term>concubine: the original "boelschap" implies an illicit or immoral sexual relationship</term>, sit with you in incest?
Does a king tolerate this in his own domain?
You should have felt that vividly long ago.
Do you shrink from cooling my courage with a hand full of blood?
Then I do not lie as deep in your heart as you pretend."
Thus she spoke sighing, and he sighed for her pain:
She felt this, and received him lovely in her arms.
The glow of love began to kindle and to warm.

# JOHN.

The chosen and caressed one: and the fire
Ignited every vein, the marrow, bone, and innards.
Then they melted together in such a glow of luxury;
Thus stood a brief pleasure, which Eve imagined for herself,
A mouthful of apple juice, enjoyed against God,
And His clear will, and express Commandment,
Consigning the companion of her lust and herself, and their corrupted kin
To the shipwreck of both soul and body and eternal life.
The king, thus bound by the enjoyment of beauty,
And overcome, saw in the floating vision
The three Graces <note>original: "dry bevalligheên"; Herod sees Herodias as the personification of the Charites or Graces of Greek mythology.</note>, and spirits of love hovering,

To risk the crown for a desert-dweller <term>desert-dweller: a reference to John the Baptist’s ascetic life in the wilderness</term>, a defenseless man,

You saw during his capture how little they cared for crown or laws:

You wound the apple of God’s eyes, as was cried out

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

The governor Pontius, and we ourselves, have shown you.

Who, richly anointed by God, was promised to the fathers;
Or if all tribes were yet to wait for another,

They followed the master's command, and asked just at the right moment,
Where he, very busy, healed a distressed crowd,

### JOHN.

Acknowledge how a row of lepers, long gnawed
By a cureless evil, see themselves wiped clean of stains.
The corpses wake up, and this hardened Jewry <note>A historical reference to the Jewish establishment of the time who resisted Jesus's teachings.</note>,
Poor in heavenly things, becomes rich through our message of grace.
Happy is he who does not stumble on that stone <note>A reference to the "stumbling block" mentioned in the Gospels; the idea that many would find it hard to believe that God would appear in human form.</note>,
That God, embodied from heaven, appeared to everyone.

<margin>Christ's testimony and eulogy of John.</margin>
They <note>John's disciples.</note> return, and the greatest oracle of God's truth
Began John's praise thus, in full clarity,
To unfold before the people, gathered from everywhere:
Is Zacharias's son <note>John the Baptist was the son of the priest Zacharias and Elizabeth.</note> now small and lowly in your eyes,
Since he sends to kindle light from me?
He was compared to the small light, and I to the great light,
The mother-light, from which all who need light draw their fire.
If you think little of him, why then did everyone wander
So far out of the cities into the wild wilderness?
Was it to see a wavering reed on all sides
Bending to the wind? That is not John.
He stands unshakeable, like a column, fearing
No kings, though they be drowned to the neck in adultery
And hellish horrors. He disciplines his body in caves
And dungeons, and lives on meager food.
Or did you want to see one dressed soft and warm against the cold,
And magnificently laid out in silk and purple?
Look for splendor and pomp in the court: there courtly vices swarm;
There the world is served with flattery <note>original: "pluimstrijckery"; literally "feather-stroking," referring to those who sycophantically groom or praise those in power.</note>;
There one keeps kings as friends for the sake of base pleasure,
And pushes God’s law and God aside for the sake of status.
Or did you seek a prophet, who dedicated himself to the truth?
That he was, and is still, and more than a prophet,
Who, when sought for honor and prestige, confessed me
As God’s Anointed <term>Anointed: the English translation of "Messiah" (Hebrew) or "Christ" (Greek)</term>, and called himself a voice crying in the wilderness <note>original: "boschgalm"; literally a forest-echo or forest-sound.</note>,
A trumpet of repentance <note>original: "boetbazuin"</note>, who would soon fall silent and vanish:
And truly he is more than any ancient prophet:
For Malachi foretells how he fulfills his office,

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

And, like an angel in a body, he would exhort everyone
To pave the highway for the coming of David’s son <note>A traditional title for the promised Messiah.</note>:
Indeed, no prophet sent before him equals him;
No mother ever bore a son whose radiance was his match.
I myself, descending from the Father’s bosom from on high,
Seem in this holy era less than he in the eyes of men.
He burns with zeal: from that divine fire <note>original: "godtsbrant"</note> radiates his glow;
Therefore the Pharisees wished to lift him to the heights
Of honor above me, to smother my own light:
But pointing away from himself to the Word, the firstborn,
And the holy sacrificial lamb that bears humanity’s sin,
He fell into the hatred of hypocrisy <note>original: "schijndeught," literally "sham-virtue"—referring to those who perform piety for show.</note>, which plagues him
In the claws of the dungeon, where, driven by the Spirit,
He does not fear to give testimony of the eternal Word.

<margin>Soldiers, tax collectors, and public sinners are converted here and baptized; yet lawyers and Pharisees remain stubborn. Christ illustrates their obstinacy through a parable.</margin>
Soldier and tax collector, and those who walk with stains,
But are now washed in the flowing Jordan,
Exalt God for this, who deems them worthy of His grace,
And mercifully justifies whoever sighs in repentance:
But scribes and never-converted Pharisees
Refuse, against God’s counsel, to enter the bath through penance.
Therefore Truth <note>Referring to Christ as the personification of Truth.</note> paints their defect with these strokes:

To whom is this brood best compared?
They are like children in the marketplace with this cry:
We piped our best, and you never joined the dance <note>original: "ten rey," a reference to a traditional folk dance or round dance.</note>.
We sang a lament, and you never shed a drop of tears.
John came to faithfully rouse and exhort you,
He never ate wheat, nor drank strong drink or wine;
Yet they called him a demon <note>original: "droes," a term for a devil or malevolent spirit.</note>, disguised with an angelic appearance.
Mary’s son <note>Jesus.</note> appeared sociable and lovely,
And joined as a guest at the table, pleasing to ear and eye;
Yet you called him a glutton and a wine-god <note>An allusion to Bacchus; a "wine-bibber."</note>, everyone’s friend,
Helped and served by tax collectors and harlots:
Yet the wise praise God, the universal Father,
Equally near to each, a generous provider and satisfier,

<meta>This page continues from the previous section where Christ delivers a high-praise eulogy for John the Baptist. It describes how this divine endorsement restores John's reputation among the common people while simultaneously provoking the religious and political authorities to conspire against him.</meta>

### JOHN.

Who neglects no remedy or wise instruction,
To lead everyone into the heavenly paradise.

<margin>John's name and reputation are further burdened by slanderous tongues during his imprisonment:</margin>
Just as, in times of adversity and trial,
The name and fame of the oppressed must suffer under a cloud
By all who flatter and seek favor at court;
So John, a captive hero, was cursed,
And his honor was touched by god-forgotten stains.
The waverers, who easily paint a piece of slander
To please tyrants, so they might live
Without trouble, allowed the Saint to suffer a blow
To his honor and esteem, and even set their seal
To the dishonorable slandering. The upright man, who followed the rule
Of virtue and truth, and would never yield,
Had to suffer bravely. Such is the world and its loyalty:

<margin>Yet Christ’s eulogy keeps his honor aloft.</margin>
But when the oracle of wisdom and truth <note>A reference to Jesus Christ.</note>
Praised the messenger of penance and God, with such clarity
He dispelled the clouds and the mist of slander,
All the world looked on. Such great prestige strengthened
The nerves of all the weak, though they were little thankful.
What was first denied became recognizable and acceptable:

<margin>A Comparison</margin>
Just as genuine currency, rejected for a season,
Rises in value, and regains honor once recognized.

<margin>This embitters the Herodians, Pharisees, and Essenes. Caiaphas and Annas send Malchus to Herodias to incite her.</margin>
This speech of praise, which gradually began to gain more ground,
Disturbed the priesthood of Aaron <note>The traditional Jewish high-priesthood.</note> beyond measure,
The Herodians, the refined Essenes, and Pharisees.
This fame thundered through the court, the country, and the cities.
The High Priests, Caiaphas and Annas, conferred together
To consider how best they might shame this rise in popularity,
And dispatched their attendant Malchus <note>Usually identified as the servant of the High Priest; in this literary context, he acts as a messenger.</note> by night,
A bitter zealot, who cared for neither God nor truth,
So long as he might safeguard his masters' honor at the cost of his soul.
He arrived at the palace at Machaerus <note>The fortress where John the Baptist was held prisoner.</note> riding across the bridge,
With a confidential letter for Lady Herodias.
Her heart leapt with joy when she read its contents.

<meta>catchword: They</meta>

<meta>This page transitions from the general public's renewed respect for John the Baptist to the specific political reaction of Herodias and King Herod. ### THE FIFTH BOOK.

She is warned in time to guard her honor and status,
And to quickly smother two who raised their heads too proudly:
First the squire <note>Likely referring to a disciple or messenger of John who spread his teachings.</note>, and then the master and his authority.
It now touched her crown. If she neglected it, the day
Would bring her regret. There was no need for pretenses
To teach the unrestrained tongue to mock so freely
At majesties, and so great a queen.
She took these words deep into her reflection,
And answered that letter to the choir-master <note>The "koorknaep" or leader of the religious conspirators.</note> briefly and powerfully:

<margin>
Herodias thanks the high priests, and complains about Herod’s weak-heartedness, and how he appeases her with words.
</margin>
"I thank the masters for their loyalty and favor. Truly,
They watch over us and our state like fathers.
One honors them as fathers for their care: but what counsel is there?
The king, until now, could be brought to no decision.
Night and day we lament our heart’s grief, and mix
Our tears into the lamentation. He kisses the tears away,
And wipes them from the cheek, but remains strict and stern
In refusing his beloved’s request and first prayer.
It does affect him: but I am appeased with this speech:"

"My beauty, I want to accomplish for you what I can:
But how? *John* is a stern and holy man,
Righteous according to the law, a zealot for his customs.
Is it out of bounds? Remember, the teaching office brings this with it:
And our conscience witnesses how we stand together.
Nature has gone outside the bounds of the teaching.
It sounds royal indeed not to bind oneself so narrowly:
For *David*, *Solomon*, and the first patriarchs were
Not so pure themselves. Our transgression is also within reason:
But never has God’s prophet suffered this unpunished.
We ought not to wake the sleeping too abruptly.
If all who curse us began to take up arms,
Just as *Europe* once flew toward *Asia* in the past:
I would see *Troy* in *Machaerus* <note>The fortress-palace where John was imprisoned.</note>, and *Paris* and *Helen*
Reborn in me and you, and *Menelaus* again
In *Philip* <note>original: "Flips"; refers to Herod Philip I, Herod's brother and Herodias's former husband.</note> my brother; and *Homer* with his pen..."

# JOANNES.

To strive for this matter is to seek a blood-drenched Iliad <note>An allusion to the Trojan War, suggesting the coming events will be of epic, tragic proportions.</note>.
I saw Machaerus <note>The fortress where John the Baptist was imprisoned.</note> in smoke, and fire, and flame.
Is your honor then well guarded? Keep silent, and let this rest.
Thus I sit deep in shame, and defenseless, without a weapon,
And swallow with impatience that horrible reproach.
<margin>Herodias is consumed by vengefulness.</margin>
Thus she spoke, and howled with vengefulness and spite,
And shook her head thrice, and let her tresses swing.
In vain Malchus <note>The temple official or "choir-master" acting as a messenger for the High Priests.</note> seeks to comfort and appease her.
The resentment knows no end, yet finally, somewhat calmed,
She resumes her word: my thirst for revenge whets the sword,
Or mixes poison. I sit defamed and slandered,
But I swear not to suffer this insult long unavenged.
<margin>And desires that Malchus still await the birthday feast.</margin>
If it pleases you, stay a little while in our court.
My husband's birthday returns. Let us see what material
Shall serve to atone for this irreconcilable resentment,
Even if one were to break the crown, and body, and soul into pieces here.
Await the happy feast. I might make use of
Your advice, and take everything to heart that can help.
The choir-master Malchus allowed himself to be persuaded by her plea.
<margin>Preparations to celebrate the birthday.</margin>
The day appeared now high in the month of spring. Already
Everything was gathered that could serve the honor of this feast.
Never did Jerusalem see the court of Solomon
Shine more, even when gold was scooped from the King's gold-fleet <note>A reference to the legendary wealth Solomon acquired via ships from Ophir.</note>.
The entire court household found work. Each toiled, each sweated, each busied
Their hands: yet the court still lacked for time and people.
<margin>Princes and princesses gather for the banquet.</margin>
The princes go to court, and the ladies
Are welcomed and received, according to their rank and worth,
By the realm’s steward in the front hall. Thus gathered
The festive company, fast as one heart and one soul,
Where one met and entertained the other courteously
With dignified state conversation and all manner of news,
Nor did they spare the name of Christ, spread everywhere:
Whether this was the King, the true Son of David,
To—by redeeming the people—topple Tiberius from Numa’s throne <note>original: "Tybeer uit Numaes troon". A poetic way of describing the Roman Emperor (Tiberius) being removed from the ancient seat of Roman power (Numa Pompilius was the second king of Rome).</note>

## JOANNES.

The crowd pressed in: for the King’s birthday celebration
Held open court, and wished neither the commoners nor citizens
To be restrained, but to welcome each with equal courtesy.
More tables were set out, and every hall was opened.
The sails had to be full, and every flag at the masthead.
<margin>Here is an abundance of splendor and delicacies.</margin>
Another Ophir <note>original: "Ofir", a biblical region famous for its gold (1 Kings 9:28)</note> flowed into the King’s chest and treasury.
Now field and forest seemed emptied of game.
Lemon, olive, pomegranate, and golden oranges hang
On branches, dripping their sweetness <note>original: "de joffer", a poetic term here referring to the succulent juice or "maidenly" nectar of the fruit</note> into the mouth.
A paradise-like air, perfectly temperate and healthy,
Refreshed the hearts of all who sat here together.
Luxury and excess, now simultaneously unleashed,
Met one another in mutual favor.
The preciousness of the materials had to yield to the artistry
And labor bestowed upon the gold and silverwork,
<margin>John in the dungeon is forgotten.</margin>
Jewels, and festive garments. No thought was given to the prisoner,
Who in the dungeon almost perished from the stench and want,
And yet clung faithfully to God, the Creator of all things;
Though he seemed pitiably abandoned by God and everyone.
<margin>The poverty of worldly riches, and the richness of godly poverty.</margin>
Alas, how things go in worldly states!
Diogenes <note>A Greek Cynic philosopher who famously lived in a barrel and told Alexander the Great to move out of his sunlight, valuing virtue over material wealth.</note> sits poor, sheltered in his tub,
Yet rich; he will not beg a gift from a monarch
Who blocks the rich sun from him—something the king,
For all his power, cannot give. He weighs death and life
In one scale, content with whatever pleases God,
Who gives life and takes it. The second Elijah <note>A reference to John the Baptist, who was seen as the spiritual successor to the prophet Elijah.</note> asks
Nothing of the threats of any Jezebels or Ahabs <note>Jezebel and Ahab were the corrupt King and Queen of Israel who persecuted the original Elijah.</note>.
He gives what he possesses to the richest Lord as interest:
His rest, his blood, his soul, and his good and bad reputation.
He gives it all for the best, for the honor and name of Jesus,
To establish humanity's salvation in its full power.
Let the princely court now freely disguise a villainous deed;
Let High Priests, Pharisees <note>A Jewish religious sect often depicted as legalistic opponents of John and Jesus.</note>, and all who crucify the truth
Secretly gloat <note>original: "lachen in zijn vuiſt", literally "laugh in his fist", meaning to laugh in one’s sleeve or chuckle privately.</note>:

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

A searcher of hearts sees through the fox’s skin and pretense,
And recognizes the courtly schemes, the aims, and secret thoughts
Of the scripture-twister <note>original: "schriftverdraeier," referring to those who manipulate holy texts to justify the King's illicit marriage</note>, who boldly executes his fantasies
And hems his long robes with verses and proverbs.
Oh, how peaceful it is to shelter in a lowly hut,
Counting neither shield nor weapon, neither lineage nor royal family trees
From a line of ancestors; instead, to feel the joy
Of a single drop of grace and comfort flowing from God!
That is worth more than ancestry, shield, and crest.
Thus one rises cheerfully, and thus one goes cheerfully to sleep.
But let us finish the season and the role
Of this courtly stage-play of Helen <note>A reference to Herodias, comparing her to Helen of Troy as a woman whose beauty and actions lead to ruin</note>, blind and driven mad.
The banquet continues. The mistress <note>original: "boel," a term for a paramour, referring here to Herodias</note> lies in wait.
The spirit of adultery that possesses the court shall soon depart
With the stench of murder, serving as a mirror to all.

<margin>It becomes evening.</margin>
The spring sun, which had long shone upon the courtly banquet,
Began to set where the Nile, with its seven full mouths <note>original: "keelen," literally throats</note>,
Comes roaring into the sea, parting the salt waves
With foam and a powerful current born in the Moorish mountains <note>The Ethiopian highlands, historically considered the source of the Nile</note>,
Whose proud peaks defy the high heavens.
Shadows approached, rising with dark mists.
Then, light and day were sought from a hundred golden lamps,
Hung for splendor from the high vaulted ceiling of the court.
The King, in high spirits, called for a royal chalice
From which his father, Herod <note>Herod the Great</note>, used to drink,
And commanded his Ganymede <note>In Greek mythology, the cupbearer to the gods; here used as a courtly term for a young servant or page</note> to fill it with delicious nectar.
Everyone fell silent. He spoke joyfully, so that all could hear him:

<margin>Herod invites the guests to a festive toast on his birthday.</margin>
"Today is the festive anniversary—God willed it—when the sun
First shone upon us in our nurse’s lap. Then, the entire court
Approached the throne and loudly wished the lord father and his son
Good fortune to the sounding trumpets of Fame.
Everyone cried: 'May he inherit your virtue as well as your name,
For the blessing of the kingdom, that it may increase under him!'
Expect now, all of you, something from your noble lord..."

Heavenly music echoed through the great palace.

Crowns are placed upon the king and queen.
</margin>
