### THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>Princess Salome reappears to dance.</margin>
While thus occupied, the newly-attired Salome enters,
As playfully adorned as Flora <note>original: "Flora," the Roman goddess of flowers and spring; in some traditions, she was also associated with a wealthy courtesan who left her fortune to the Roman people.</note>, who in Rome
Enriched the citizenry according to her last will.
The dancers clear the floor. Song and play fall silent.
She swings a lute of white ivory, ready to play,
And while dancing, to stroke the ear with throat and string,
To satisfy every eye with the flourish of her dance, now thus,
Now otherwise. The father approaches her
With a kiss, and can hardly restrain himself from this Pandora <note>Pandora: in Greek mythology, the first woman, endowed with all gifts but also the bringer of unforeseen trouble.</note>;
The mother kisses her as well, and weeps, letting her tears—
Which roll down her cheeks like dew and pearls—fall

<margin>She asks her lord father for permission.</margin>
Out of joy. The daughter asked for permission. The father gave
The kneeling girl leave to show off before him and the court
With her talents, from which charms radiate.
She is by nature perfected for praise, from head to toe,

<margin>Her beauty is depicted.</margin>
As if precisely carved from the face of her mother,
Like a statue, and beautiful of form; and all her limbs
Move themselves naturally according to her innate character.
Her eyes float like two stars, in which a fire
Of diamonds plays. Were Apelles <note>Apelles: the most famous painter of ancient Greece, known for his realism.</note> here now,
His paint would lack the fire to paint this face;
Indeed, Solomon, however wise and grey, would yet grow wild,
Become entangled, and degenerate into a pagan, mad with love,
To deny the divinity, and swing the incense
For a goddess. But the feast-guests wait;
It is time that we begin and lead the dance.

<margin>She begins first to play, and thereafter to play, sing, and dance. A simile.</margin>
She steps first slowly, as a prelude, back and forth,
Gradually becoming swifter on the lute-string, time after time.
After the prelude she begins first to play and to sing,
And knows how to compel her feet to the tone and pace,
Yet lightly, without constraint, so that festive dance and music
Of throat and string resemble a lovely marriage,
Or rather no marriage, but a noble courtship.
The rose-wreath around her head, the swaying plumage,

<meta>This page continues the description of Salome as she prepares to dance before King Herod. The previous page ended by noting her perfection and likeness to her mother, Herodias. # JOHN.

The snow-white silk dress, sown with lilies of gold,
The hanging hair, bedewed with the scent of nectar,
The pearls at her ear, the pure necklaces,
And the forehead, where a constellation of fine diamonds
Shines upon her heavenly countenance, do not charm as strongly
As she enchants all who surround her in the arena.
But listen to how she sings: believe then that mermaids
Are intent on shipwreck by the caressing of the senses,
Driven and urged by tones following their rhythm.
All that is most beautiful, captured in a single point, enraptures and strikes.

<margin>Her song.</margin>
She sang: Antony could forget Fulvia <note>Fulvia: the politically powerful first wife of Mark Antony.</note>,
As soon as Cleopatra had once possessed his heart.
He watched her eyes and her mouth by day and night.
The Roman hero, who bound the sword full of courage to his side
To go eastward to wage war against the Parthians <note>Parthians: an empire in the Near East (modern-day Iran) that was a major rival to Rome.</note>,
Laid the sword down gently at the beckoning of her eyes.
She hung upon his side thereafter like a broadsword,
Enchanted by her love and dear idolatry.
When she met the invited hero on the stream of Cydnus <note>Cydnus: a river in Asia Minor where Cleopatra famously arrived on a magnificent barge to meet Mark Antony.</note>,
She resembled Thetis <note>Thetis: a sea nymph and goddess in Greek mythology, mother of Achilles.</note> in a seashell, greeting him.
He melted, like Adonis, in Venus’s playful lap.
Theatrical play, masquerade, and wherever lust summoned him
To the hunt or court banquet, he followed it, as if consumed.
Had Caesar won Rome, she had won Caesar.
She sang how Caesar hung chained around her neck;
How Danaë received Jupiter, disguised in gold <note>original: "Jupijn," an older Dutch form of Jupiter. The myth refers to Jupiter visiting Danaë as a shower of gold to conceive Perseus.</note>;
How Mars clung to Venus, and how she with blushing cheeks
Saw the gods from high above a cloud looking on,
And laughing at the deception of the most beautiful bride <note>This refers to the myth where Vulcan, Venus's husband, caught her and Mars in a fine net while they were together, exposing them to the ridicule of the other gods.</note>.
The trembling of her throat, full of organs, and the lute—
A grave for the nightingale <note>A poetic metaphor suggesting her voice is so beautiful that the nightingale dies of envy.</note>—followed by the spirited swaying
Of her feet to and fro, and the wanton dance of her fingers,
All equally spirited and skilled upon the lute strings,
Portrayed the meaning of the words, in varied harmony.

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>Simile</margin>
Like the rainbow, with many-colored hues,
Which here advance, there fade away, and die.
Now she traced yet another ribbon with her dancing in this space,
Wherein Herod’s name stood spelled with letters.
Then the lute fell silent, and seemed to pause for a very short while.
Meanwhile one heard violins, harp, and flutes,
Shawm, and trombone <note>original: "schuiftrompet", literally "sliding trumpet," an early form of the trombone used in the 17th century</note> mingling with her voice,
And echoes, which gradually faded, soft and noble.
Then the song surged, then seemed to give up:
<margin>Simile</margin>
Like the night-light burns in the tube, then loftily
Flares up, then succumbs, and, piteously exhausted,
Gives up the ghost with a sob and a flash, and suddenly vanishes.
At last she comes springing quickly out of the dancing arena,
<margin>Simile</margin>
Like the fox, in order not to reveal the den and its young
To the hound <note>original: "brack", a scent-hound used in hunting</note> that follows the scent through the grass.
Herod sensed this, and Lady Herodias,
And it tickled his spirit. So she fell before his feet.
One heard a clapping of hands, and all the guests had to
Confess that never was a dance danced on the Nile,
Nor even a Cleopatra, with such flair and style,
Performed for the hero <note>Referring to Mark Antony, mentioned on the previous page</note> whose heart she mastered,
As did this princess Salome. Everyone praised her: everyone honored
The beauty of the soul, within the body, beautiful in its youth.
<margin>The father embraces her,</margin>
The father <note>Herod Antipas was actually Salome’s stepfather and uncle</note> took her up in his arms, while she modestly
Prayed for innocence, and that the dance might please him.
He kissed her mouth. The fellow-guests saw
The spirit and power of the wine surging up. He promises
<margin>and swears to give her whatever she shall demand.</margin>
And swears by his crown, and simultaneously by his head,
After the Eastern fashion, that whatever she should desire,
Even were it half the kingdom <note>original: "halve kroon", literally "half crown"</note>, he would readily bestow it upon her.
She respectfully accepts this grace of majesty.
<margin>She departs with the mother alone to</margin>
The mother seizes this word, all-rejoicing, and leads
The daughter into the chamber. There she goes, before it becomes too late
And he becomes sober, to consult quietly with Malchus <note>Malchus: likely a reference to a trusted advisor or servant, though in some traditions, he is associated with the High Priest’s servant</note>.

<margin>

The maiden is instructed on what gift she should demand.
She enters the hall, emboldened by the grace offered to her,
And demands John’s head, at this magnificent banquet,
Upon a platter; at which Herod recoiled,
And turned as pale as a sheet <note>original: "wit, gelijck een doeck", literally "white as a cloth"</note> at the thought of the head.
He regretted the solemnly sworn oath. Granting her
The request gave him deep cause for reflection,
Not out of a godly heart, oh no; but for fear of damaging
His honor and reputation among the gathered people,
Who revered this desert-dweller <note>Referring to John the Baptist</note> like a messenger from heaven,
And who, provoked by such recklessness,
Might break out in vengeance and all manner of malice.
Though a king may command from his high seat;
He is but a man, no more, and must stand in awe of man.
The crown, the purple, the bodyguard, and the weapons
Are nothing but glimmers at which children gape:
But men know, however much one may strive and wish for this:
That all these vanities do not touch the essence of the man
But, like bark, clothe him from the outside;
And the slightest opportunity of place and time
Can be seized; even a woman, like Semiramis <note>A legendary Queen of Assyria known for her power and violence</note>
Or Judith <note>A biblical heroine who decapitated the general Holofernes</note>, can deliver the blow
To him in bed, or while sitting at the table; also, in the midst of his processions,
Just as Pausanias <note>The assassin of Philip II of Macedon</note> came to greet Philip
From behind, in the middle of a triumphant feast.
What counsel is there from this side? On the other side, the bloodhound <note>Referring to Herod</note>
Who does not fear to bark at heaven, fears
That the whole court might despise and rebuke him
For faithlessness and perjury. What counsel?
The scales of deliberation tip back and forth, and do not
Hang in balance. Then shame rose up:
What shame? A hellish shame in a drunken head, dusty
And clouded by wine: and Apollion <note>The Greek name for the "Destroyer" or Abaddon, a demonic figure</note>
<margin>
He comes to a decision.
</margin>
Secretly whispers in his ear: "Go forth." Then he overcame—

# THE FIFTH BOOK.

<margin>
The king yields, and gives the order to behead John.
</margin>
Drunken shame, reason, suspicion, and terrors contend within him.
He forces open his mouth with a ghastly gaze
And cries: "Steward, onward! Go forth and seize him now.
Let Salome be served the slanderer's head upon a dish."
"A desert-dweller <note>Referring to John the Baptist</note> respected neither God nor majesties.
He carries his own sentence; what need is there for lengthy pleading?"
The high steward departs, with Malchus <note>A name often used for a servant or guard in passion plays</note> at his side,
Leading him toward the tower. There he finds two or three
Disciples, who kept a fearful watch over their master
And never, through joy or sorrow, betrayed the prisoner's honor.
They were even permitted a light, as a kind of threat:
To see their own reflection in the slanderer’s bloody end.
The dungeon is unlocked by night at the high command.
John hears them with a cheerful heart and, undismayed,
<margin>
John is told of his death,
</margin>

"Prepare yourself quickly for death; your sentence is passed."
John answers: "I have prepared myself for a long time,
Finding comfort in death’s stroke. The Messiah <note>original: "Meſſias"</note> comes to crush 
The head of this serpent, Death; thus true life begins."
He sees his disciples standing there in tears,
<margin>
and comforts the disciples.
</margin>
And says: "My children, is this a time for weeping?"

"Bear witness to your Savior of my humble duty,
And how the morning star <note>John refers to himself as the herald of the coming Christ</note> vanishes before the great Mother-Light,
And how Moses’ law and Aaron’s ceremonies

<margin>
Malchus urges the execution forward.

<margin>
John kneels,
</margin>
Speaking with a soul more innocent and blameless than Abel’s,
Even when Cain’s brotherly malice fell upon him in a mad rage:
"May the Lamb of God be my guide, forever and always.
Like Elijah <note>original: "Helias"</note>, I have walked strictly before the eyes of God;
I have rebuked incest and violence, and have committed no wrong."

### J O A N N E S.

<margin>and is beheaded.</margin>
"O God, receive my spirit." The captain struck and hit.
The head bounced three times upon the earth in the bloody dust. <note>A common legend in hagiography suggests that where a martyr's head struck the ground three times, holy springs would flow.</note>
<margin>Malchus rejoices at his death.</margin>
And Malchus <note>A name traditionally given to a servant or guard, often associated with the servant whose ear Peter cut off; here he represents the hostile Jewish establishment.</note> cried: "So must it go for those who hate Zion,
Slander the high priests, and the royal estates."
He dipped a cloth in the warm and smoking blood,
And spat in the face of the gaping head, full of rage.
<margin>It seemed as if one heard an angelic song.</margin>
It seemed one heard a song and resonance of angelic tongues,
As if they sang praise to God and "Alleluia" <note>original: "aleluia"</note>
To Him who values the blood of the Saints so highly and dearly.
<margin>The disciples comfort themselves over their master’s glorious death.</margin>
The disciples walk mournfully through the city,
And lament, weeping: "What a drunken madness can do!"
John is born today, or rather reborn
To the martyr's crown, reserved for him in Jesus' kingdom.
Two unequals stand equal only in time
Regarding their birthday feast. <note>This contrasts Herod’s literal birthday, celebrated with sin, with John’s "heavenly birthday"—the day of his martyrdom.</note> Posterity shall,
If Jesus' radiance once manages to penetrate the world,
See this holy martyr’s feast celebrated year after year,
With rose garlands, and festive tones, and church ceremonies.
<margin>Salome enters with this head on a golden dish into the banquet hall, and serves it on the table.</margin>
A golden dish caught the head. It was given to Salome,
Who, joyful for this gift and a welcome to her mother,
Enters the high hall, where the banquet is merry.
She serves it exultantly to her father upon the table,
As a royal banquet dish, and seats herself at the side
Of her mother, in high spirits, and never before so happy:
<margin>The mother is cheerful, and thinks little of what calamity nears her daughter.</margin>
But little did she think what would befall her daughter,
Who, dancing on the ice, would feel from that ice a sharper sword
As she sank, when God's righteous scythe
Mows the godless weeds, and comes to demand an account
Of such a horrific deed, with which the table now boasts. <note>This refers to a popular medieval legend that Salome died while crossing a frozen river; the ice broke, and as she fell through, the jagged ice decapitated her.</note>
<margin>Salome pricks John’s tongue with her hairpin.</margin>
The wanton dancer pricks with her golden needle
The tongue of the holy head, and dares to speak revengefully:
"I prick an adder, that has bitten my lady mother
So fiercely in the heart; a thing that least suits a man
Who would establish laws, and modesty, and shame."

# THE FIFTH BOOK.
N
<meta>catchword: JOAN-</meta>

<margin>
The dinner guests mock the head.
</margin>
The dinner guests mock: here is the Water-pourer. <note>original: "Waterstorter." A derogatory pun referring to John’s role as the Baptist by comparing him to the constellation Aquarius, the Water-bearer.</note>
Let him be set among the stars. This man is a head shorter <note>original: "houtvoet korter." A Dutch idiom of the period meaning to be beheaded or "shortened" by a head.</note>
Than before, and he himself predicted that he must decrease. <note>A dark play on the biblical verse John 3:30: "He must increase, but I must decrease." The guests mockingly apply his prophecy of humility to his decapitation.</note>
The platter is pushed around the table. How could
The heavens, which watched by night with a thousand eyes
Of bright stars, still tolerate this spectacle!
But what wickedness does God not permit before He punishes!
Thus ended the scene of the banquet, so hellishly served.

It seemed one heard a song and resonance of angelic tongues,
As if they sang praise to God, and <term>Alleluia</term> <note>A song of praise derived from the Hebrew "Hallelu Yah," meaning "Praise the Lord."</note>

<margin>The disciples comfort themselves over their master’s glorious death.</margin>

And lament, weeping: “What a drunken fury <note>original: "droncken toren." Likely a reference to Herod’s intoxicated rage or the "drunken tower/palace" where the feast occurred.</note> can bring about!”
John is born today—or rather, reborn—

Two different birthdays are now equal in time alone. <note>A reference to the contrast between Herod’s earthly birthday celebration and John’s "birthday" into eternal life through martyrdom.</note>

If Jesus’ radiance once manages to break through,
This holy martyr’s feast celebrated year after year,
With wreaths of roses, and festoons, and churchly ceremonies.

The wanton dancer pricks with her golden needle <note>original: "perruick-naelt." A decorative pin used for securing wigs or elaborate hairstyles.</note>
The tongue of the holy head, and dares to speak vengefully:
“I sting an adder, which has stung Lady Mother
So fiercely in the heart; a thing that least becomes a man
Who would plant laws, and modesty, and shame.”

# THE SIXTH BOOK.

To put [the law] into effect, and to smother the new plague <note>Meaning the teachings and influence of John the Baptist, which the authorities viewed as a social contagion.</note>,
[So they called it] before their ruin could be born.
They both welcomed the choir-boy in his cloak <note>original: "falet," a type of 17th-century head-covering or veil, here used to describe Malchus’s official attire.</note>,
And asked what he brought for the comfort of Moses' law.
"That storm," spoke Malchus, "no longer blows so angrily.
The servant is already beheaded: the master <note>Referring to Jesus.</note> shall soon follow."
<margin>He confirms the joyful news with the handkerchief, dipped in John’s blood, and mentions how Herodias considers herself bound to the high priests.</margin>
At once he showed the handkerchief to the sun,
Dyed in holy blood. "I came, I saw, and I won" <note>A reference to Julius Caesar’s famous "Veni, vidi, vici."</note>.
The vanguard lies in the sand: we shall triumph.
Thus one draws blood, so that the fever of mutiny might be bled away.
The queen thanks the princes of the church,
For their faithful watch. She takes it as a mark
Of your affection for her crown and estates,
And swears you may rely on Herod’s power.
If she at court, by the king, her lord,
Can favor you, for the growth of your honor;
She remains your servant, from now on, for all time,
To the defiance of the enemies who envy Aaron's crown <note>Referring to the high priesthood, as Aaron was the first High Priest.</note>.
He told them of the dance, and of the birthday feast;
Of Herod’s joy, and oath, and of Salome’s spirit;
How he helped Herodias direct the plot;
How the wild man <note>John the Baptist, so called because of his life in the wilderness.</note> had to prepare for death;
And how many miles of field the church-carriage
Had raced by night, over mountain, and hill, and through forest,
And bramble, to greet the superiors of the priestly consecration,
At the rising of the sun with the first joyful news,
With a wish to remain in their favor.
<margin>The high priests praise his zeal, but find it advisable not to mention this to the people.</margin>
He added: "If it be deemed advisable by your holiness
To capture Joseph’s son <note>Jesus.</note> by soldiers;
Place me at the front: I am willing to give my life for your crown;
Though they cry out: [He is] scavenged from Idumaean stench" <note>A derogatory reference to Herod’s Idumaean (Edomite) heritage; the Judean elite often looked down on the Idumaeans.</note>.
They accepted this news with jubilation and thanks:
But they thought it risky to bring it to light before the people
That the high priests dared to meddle in this plot.

# THE SIXTH BOOK.

To put into force, and to smother the new plague,
[So they called him] before their ruin might be born.
They welcomed the choir-servant <note>Malchus, a servant of the High Priest.</note> into the parlor,
And asked what he brought for the comfort of Moses' law.
That storm, said Malchus, blows no longer so enraged.
The servant is already dead; the master <note>Referring to Jesus.</note> shall soon follow:
<margin>He confirms the joyful news with the sweat-cloth, dipped in John’s blood, and reports how Herodias considers herself bound to the High Priests.</margin>
At once he showed the sweat-cloth to the sun,
Stained in holy blood. I came, I saw, and I conquered. <note>original: "ick quam, en zagh, en won" — a reference to the famous "Veni, vidi, vici" of Julius Caesar.</note>
The vanguard lies in the sand; we shall triumph.
So one taps blood, so that the fever of mutiny should not increase.
The queen thanks the princes of the church,
For their faithful watch. She takes it as a mark
Of your affection for her crown and estates,
And swears you may rely on Herod's power.
If she, at court, with the king her lord,
Can favor you, to the growth of your honor;
She remains your handmaid, from now on, for all time,
In defiance of the enemies who envy Aaron's crown <note>The "crown of Aaron" represents the authority and dignity of the High Priesthood.</note>.
He reported to them the dance, and of the birthday feast;
Herod’s joy, and his oath, and of Salome’s spirit;
How he helped Herodias direct the plot;
How the wild man <note>John the Baptist, known for living in the wilderness.</note> had to prepare for death;
And how many miles of field the church-carriage
Had sped by night, over mountain, and hill, and through forest,
And bramble, to greet the superiors of the priestly consecration
At the rising of the sun with the first joyful news,
With a wish to stand in their grace.
<margin>The High Priests praise his zeal, but find it advisable not to mention this to the people.</margin>
He adds: if it be found advisable by your holiness
To capture Joseph’s son <note>Jesus.</note> by soldiers;
Let me be placed at the front: I will give my life for your crown:
Even if they cry: "scum gathered from Idumean stench." <note>A derogatory reference to Herod’s lineage; the Idumeans were considered outsiders or "half-Jews" by the Judean elite.</note>
They accepted this news with jubilation and thanks:
Yet they deemed it dangerous to bring to light
That High Priests dared to involve themselves in this plot.

<meta>This page follows Malchus's return to Jerusalem. The High Priests, relieved that John the Baptist is dead, now approach the Roman Governor, Pontius Pilate, to manage the political fallout and potential unrest during the festival.</meta>

## J O A N N E S.

They were pleased that he had acted with such caution,
Keeping himself unknown within the banquet court,
Lest he reveal his masters, who had sent him there,
And wake the thousands lulled into a quiet sleep. <note>The High Priests were glad Malchus stayed undercover to avoid a public riot while John was being executed.</note>
Now they thought it wise to tell the Governor <note>Pontius Pilate, the Roman prefect of Judea.</note>
This news themselves, before the rumors of the deed
Spread through the public. They set out toward the Roman court,
And stately climbed the steps of polished marble stone.
The choir-servant <note>Malchus, who acted as an attendant to the High Priests.</note> stepped ahead, to learn at once
If an audience with Pontius, this early, was convenient.
He let the two Archpriests be brought in without delay.
They followed promptly, where the courtiers led the way.
Pilate welcomed them within the long gallery,
Supported by fifty court-columns on one side.
He listens, and he questions, and after weighing well
The unexpected news, he answers their report.

<margin>He remains neutral in their religious disputes,</margin>
We have taken note of the rumors of this wilderness man,
Yet never once opposed his zealous, driving spirit.
About foreign religions and **Hebrew school-disputes**
Rome does not trouble herself; these may, if they so wish,
Follow their own free course, and bicker till they break,
So long as the bickering does not touch the Capitol’s <note>The "Capitol" symbolizes the ultimate authority and power of the Roman state.</note> command.

<margin>and excuses John,</margin>
This wilderness man never forbade the paying of taxes,
Nor has he ever encouraged his following in their zeal
To set themselves against Prince Tiberius: <note>The Roman Emperor Tiberius (reigned 14–37 AD).</note> for this reason
One cannot attach any slander to a defenseless man,
Who disciplined the soldiers, and recognized the authorities;

<margin>but blames the followers of Judas of Galilee, and threatens them.</margin>
But one from Gaulon <note>Refers to Judas of Galilee, a rebel leader who founded the "Fourth Philosophy" or Zealot movement, which resisted Roman taxation.</note> hatched a civil misery,
By the introduction of a fourth temple-sect,
Which, in its zeal, will recognize no imperial decree,
Nor Emperor, but seeks to set itself in freedom.
They come here for the feast. You cannot prevent it.
We swear, however, to trample them soon at the heart:
Even should one mix the blood of sacrifice with the blood of Galileans. <note>A historical and biblical reference (Luke 13:1) to Pilate’s brutal suppression of Galilean protesters at the Temple.</note>

<summary>The High Priests inform Pontius Pilate of John the Baptist's death. Pilate dismisses John as a harmless religious figure but expresses his intent to violently suppress the more politically dangerous Zealot faction from Galilee during the upcoming festival.</summary>

<margin>Pilate wishes not to involve himself with Christ, so long as he does not incite rebellion.</margin>
...mingle at your festival. This is not for us to endure.
As for Joseph's son <note>Jesus of Nazareth</note>: we neither fear nor envy
His rise, so long as you cannot prove
That he withholds the image of Augustus and the state's chosen coin
From the imperial tax collector, or wishes to abolish
The law of the fasces and axes <note>The Roman symbols of judicial authority and the power to execute.</note>, to establish here
The high jurisdiction <term>halsrecht: the legal right to impose the death penalty</term> according to your own fashions.
Those who follow him know no lord
But Caesar, and are poor, accustomed to fishing
<margin>He thinks little of Christ's disciples.</margin>
Upon the Galilean sea, and, focused on their master's teaching,
Look for no offices nor worldly government.
<margin>He judges Herod’s sentence harsh, and deems him not innocent of crime, yet wishes to stay out of this dispute.</margin>
Antipas’s <note>Herod Antipas, the Tetrarch of Galilee.</note> sentence, freshly delivered, sounds hard and cruel.
Roman law forbids incestuous adultery,
Just like your own law: and he, a half-Jew,
Refuses, as it appears, rebelliously to follow the commandment
Of your Moses, as he ought:
But I let this rest, and would rather not speak
Another word about such a capital judgment, executed by night:
For I have (so he interprets it) already involved myself
Too deeply in his state, and, eager for trouble,
Gave a crack to the neighborly friendship that was hardly expected.
With this he dismissed them. The high priests departed again,
And Malchus followed their trail from behind, and went down.
This sentence, upon an innocent desert-dweller <note>John the Baptist, who was recently executed by Herod.</note>;
Inflamed Jerusalem like a flying fire,
Everyone weighed it in the balance of their judgment,
Whether light or heavy, or according to their passion and personal gain.
<margin>Chuze, Herod’s court-steward in Jerusalem, defends John and Christ to Counselor Joseph.</margin>
Upright Chuze <note>Chusa, mentioned in the Gospel of Luke as Herod's steward.</note>, a man bold in justice and truth,
And to whom Herod’s court and court-care
In Zion were entrusted, had already heard this from the Lord of Arimathea <note>Joseph of Arimathea, a member of the Sanhedrin.</note>,
And sat grieving over his master's tyranny,
Committed against that righteous man during a fit of drunkenness;
And, entering into conversation, he thus began with Joseph:
How precarious it is to punish a prince <note>Meaning a ruler like Herod.</note> for a proven truth,
Even when it is clear as midday brightness!

# JOHN.

<margin>of Arimathea.</margin>And most troublesome is the female kind, delicate
In dealings, yet so easily offended in her honor.
She hates the bridle of lust, and, running wild,
Regards neither God nor men. Though she sets a kingdom to ruin,
And sends thousands upon thousands to their deaths, she cares not,
Nor even for herself, when the heart is possessed by blind vengefulness:
<margin>Simile</margin>Just as a kettle, placed on the fire, begins to foam,
Seeks the air, overflows, and forces the water to leave
The space that was ample enough to contain more liquid.
Another Ahab behaves again like a tyrant,
For Naboth's vineyard <note>A biblical reference to King Ahab, who seized a vineyard through the murderous intrigue of his wife Jezebel; here used to contrast the greed of rulers with the poverty of Christ.</note>: but neither John nor the Messiah
Possess land nor sand, and, poorer than Elijah,
They wander the backcountry, severely pressed by poverty.
The fox has his hole; the bird keeps his nest;
But both these naked pilgrims, mocked by everyone,
Go begging with their disciples out of need and hunger.
I permit my dear spouse Salome to follow
The Messiah, and in his poverty
Support him with her possessions, like the other Marys:
Yet I tell her to remain hidden, to avoid all public outcry.
It is difficult for an official of the court,
A court as wicked as this, to serve without providing cause
For disgrace, when justice seeks to prevail.
John knew how to interpret for my spouse—as if he saw it happening—
His master's path to the cross, and how she, faithful unto death,
Would still go to anoint God's body on the third day,
If she did not find him glorified and risen.
It is no wonder that John was condemned
When one considers the resentment of Pharisaism <note>The strict, often legalistic adherence to Jewish law associated with the Pharisees, which the speaker views as hypocritical or vengeful.</note>:
For recently, hatred has whetted the murderous knife;
Because the Messiah dared to dispatch his twelve disciples
Through the land to plant his teaching,
And clearly strengthen it everywhere through miracles,
Which no high priest or legal zealot <term>wetyvrae: a person zealously and jealously obsessed with the letter of the law</term> will tolerate,

# THE SIXTH BOOK.
<meta>catchword: Sick-</meta>

As long as they do not lack authority or prestige;
Therefore I believe—no matter how they try to suppress the truth—that they have plotted this murder in secret,
And I intend to investigate this privately. Observe
What fire smolders here. One can smell and sense it by the scent
Of lukewarm blood. The people still live who stained Aaron's threshold <note>A reference to the lineage of the High Priests; the speaker suggests the current religious leaders are as bloodthirsty as those who historically defiled the Temple.</note>
With the blood of Zechariah <note>Referring to the priest Zechariah, who according to tradition was murdered between the sanctuary and the altar.</note>, and who did not hesitate
To cruelly and boldly desecrate God’s temple and sanctuary,
No matter how zealously they build monuments to the prophets.
Alas, what use is gold spent on choirs and churches,
When the heart is desecrated by godless deeds!

I cannot excuse my master <note>Herod Antipas, the Tetrarch of Galilee.</note>, who so boldly
Abducted his brother’s wife and keeps that prize from him.
Philip <note>Herod Philip I, the slighted brother of Herod Antipas.</note>, by nature righteous, wise, and modest,
Does no man wrong, but bears himself quietly and peaceably,
And, content with his lot, scorns useless luxury.
No long train of servants follows him. He listens to the complaints
Of the oppressed man, and, though seated on his horse,
Will dismount to immediately grant everyone their rights.
He crowns virtue and punishes crime. Go now,
And seek this fairness among the proud Pharisees,
Who hate Philip and encourage Herod’s wickedness.
I see a dark cloud drifting here from the east:
Aretas <note>Aretas IV, the Nabataean King of Petra, whose daughter Herod Antipas divorced to marry Herodias.</note>, seeking vengeance for his daughter, is on the move,
Uniting the Galilean territory with Arabia
To venture a heavy battle on Herod's soil.
What a price a lustful desire pays in blood and defeat!

<margin>Herod, having sobered up, laments the verdict.</margin>
To Machaerus <note>The desert fortress where John the Baptist was held and executed.</note> there came, very late after the sun and day had risen,
Herod, sobering up from sleep, and he saw
What he had committed, reckless, mad, and drunk:
He does not regret that the man’s head was struck off,
But rather that, to keep his drunken oath to the end,
He sees all the people incited and insulted to the point of revenge,
And thousands rising up everywhere in throngs
To strike at the shield of the guilty slanderer.

<summary>The speaker critiques the hypocrisy of the religious elite and Herod Antipas, contrasting Herod's corruption with the virtue of his brother Philip, while noting the approaching military retaliation from King Aretas. Herod is depicted in the aftermath of John the Baptist's execution, fearing political ruin more than moral guilt.</summary>

# JOHN.

Around the banks of the Jordan, where he shone
With holiness before God, in his rocky cave.
In the meantime, commit yourselves safely to our guidance.
Then he <note>Referring to Jesus</note> sailed across the lake that separated Galilee
From the land of Gilead, the heritage of Manasseh,
To hide from Herod's eye in the distance
Of a wilderness, where people from all cities,
Following him, heartily wished for his rising.

<margin>The poet intends to describe John’s descent into hell <note>In 17th-century theology, "hell" often referred to Limbo (*Limbus Patrum*), the place where the righteous souls of the Old Testament awaited the Messiah.</note>,</margin>
But I have already remained silent too long about what path
John’s soul, released from the body, has gone
To the spirits of the fathers, in the shimmering groves,
To announce how God the Messiah, now at hand,
Would soon deliver them and lead them to heaven:
For on that sorrowful day when Adam, failing
In his faithfulness, was rightfully cast out of the garden,
Not only did paradise below remain closed,
But also the golden gate of the heavenly palace,
For all his descendants. To now follow
John's journey to hell <note>original: "hellereis"</note>, and bring it into the clear light,
The angelic choir needs to join in this song,
And strengthen my timid voice with its melody.

<margin>and requests the help of the heavenly muses: whereupon Raphael guides and instructs him.</margin>
    You heavenly choirs, who, enraptured in the Highest’s praise,
Are accustomed to rolling your resonance through the arches
Of heaven’s cathedral vault, descend from on high,
And help me—as one from the body here below
Released—to enter upon the trail of the fathers
Toward the subterranean realm. Grant me, so as not to stray,
The guidance of Raphael <note>The archangel Raphael is traditionally a guide for travelers.</note> in these mysteries.
He descends and instructs me. I listen to the report
That leads the way, and yields neither to ghost nor hellish spirits.

<margin>Description of the entrance to hell.</margin>
    The pit, where Lucifer came to stifle up to his neck,
Gapes wide, and stretches the maw wide open to the ears.
One travels into it quite easily, with horses and carriage,
First over pebbles, and then through thickets,

# THE SIXTH BOOK.

<meta>catchword: Each</meta>

<margin>Similes.</margin>
And hedges, wild and desolate. The path in its winding
Runs narrower, differently than spiral staircases turn,
Or like conch shells that roll down to a single point.
The nurturing light, granted to the entrance at first,
Fades gradually, and as it forgets to penetrate so deep,
One approaches twilight and evening shadows:
Just as when the sun, fallen below the horizon,
Still leaves a glimmer that radiates across the sea for a while:
Then it is neither night nor day, or day and night mingled,
And darkness and light are simultaneously entwined.
One walks here as if in moonlight by night,
When the heavenly host <note>The stars, personified as an army of angels.</note> sets its watch in order
And keeps its rounds along diamond walls,
From which the ambition for the crown <note>Referring to the fall of Lucifer and the rebel angels.</note> fell headlong—
Those who, with bands of brothers, rising throne by throne,
Dared to fly at God’s own crown.

<margin>The abode of hellish spirits, and the punishment of damned souls.</margin>
The Prince of Night holds his court in flames, at the navel
Of the abyss <note>original: "afgronts"; the "navel" refers to the very center or deepest point of Hell.</note>, shunning the sun, in smoke and smog and sulfur;
A gruesome, desolate expanse, full of ghosts and overcrowded
With the godless, each stuffed according to their merit
Into burning ovens or rotting, stinking sewers.
The executioners, to whom the law of damnation was commanded
To be strictly carried out in the name of the god of the abyss,
Each hold their office and honor the nocturnal decree.
One brings sulfur there, in whole sulfur-boats,
And rows through thick vapor as far as the cargo can float
On a stream of flames, now there, now here:
Another feeds the greedy fire with fish-oil and oil:
One pinches the blasphemous tongue with glowing tongs:
Another scourges, on a rack <note>original: "wip"; a torture device like a strappado.</note>, the half-hanged man
With scorpions <note>A type of heavy, knotted whip used for painful punishment.</note>, so that the blood drips down:
One blows into the hellish fire: another stokes or shovels
The coal and ashes out of the pit, and cries out for fuel
For the fire, brought to him by a brood of vipers,

<meta>This page continues the descent of the soul into the underworld. Following the description of the Prince of Night’s court, the text now depicts the monstrous guards and the specific torments assigned to the seven deadly sins within the abyss.</meta>

# JOANNES.

Each more misshapen than the last, blended from man and beast,
Many visages stirred into one, with a certain flair
Resembling wolf, and lion, and dragon, and bear, and wild boars;
All arsonists, ready with murder-daggers, and poisons,
With noose, and club, and musket, and axe, and heavy jack. <note>original: "dommekracht"; a mechanical lifting tool or jack, here likely used as a heavy weapon or instrument of torture.</note>
If there be any light anywhere; it is eternal night here;
Save for the surging of the smoke, driven with force,
Which, long in labor, gave birth to give the fire some air.
One howls from chilly cold due to the nipping of the frost,
Or pants for breath's end, from unquenchable thirst,
As the parched throat burns within from the heat.
<margin>The punishment of the seven deadly sins.</margin>
Where rolls the wine-barrel now, upon which the drunkard sat,
As if in his saddle, for a full day and night, never weary,
Until the drunken hero shattered into pieces from his horse?
What profits the savings-pot of the miser, possessed
By money, scraped together with a damned conscience?
Where does envy hide, whose heart is consumed by spite
For a neighbor's prosperity, which causes him no harm?
What help do cruelty and revenge provide for the shedding of blood,
When they sink into blood that flows around them in streams,
They who, never drowned, hear a clarion <note>A shrill, narrow-tubed war trumpet.</note> that, enraged,
Sounds violence, and revenge, and murder loudly in their ears?
What help now is incest and the violation of God’s virgins,
Who by their purity pleased the purest eye?
For a short tickle of lust, one pays the penalty here
On iron spikes, with feet in the fire.
Sloth, spurred by unrest, can endure nowhere,
Because of the reckless neglect of unrecoverable hours.
With what a hook is the misdeed seized!
How sadly the guilt is lamented! How they wish to have fasted it away,
Being poor, in haircloth with vigils and prayers,
To atone for the guilt; if only one could ascend from below
Upwards into grace, and be restored in mercy,
Reflecting on how time melts away in moments!

<summary>This page describes the monstrous guardians of the abyss and details the specific infernal punishments for the seven deadly sins, emphasizing the vanity of earthly vices compared to eternal suffering.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the underworld. Following the punishments for the seven deadly sins mentioned on the previous page, the poet now transitions from the eternal punishments of the damned to the realms of Purgatory (the "milder punishment") and Limbo (the "third ring" for those with original sin).</meta>

->### THE SIXTH BOOK<-

But all in vain: whoever is once separated from God
Never thunders back up throughout eternity
From this pit of misery. The path is far too steep,
The hell-guard too cruel, who—themselves excluded 
From hope and salvation—grant no one relief in their suffering,
Nor let the ancient sun shine upon them for even a moment.
I pass over the crimes and the many kinds
Of punishment to be mentioned in a burning heat of lamentation.
One could unroll a list here of tyrants,
And idolaters, and blasphemers, striving 
Out of malice against God, nature, and the Law of Moses;
All convicted and clearly overcome by their stain,
To the hellish rack, each one wrung by their neck:

<margin>The dwelling of the tainted souls, yet to be purified, according to the testimony of the second book of the Maccabees.</margin>
But let us ascend. A circle, pushed out even higher,
Restrains the souls in a milder place of punishment,
Where they, for the purification and penance of their stain,
Waiting for the time, hope for a blessed outcome
When the clockwork of pain has run down:
This is why the general of the famous Maccabees <note>Judas Maccabeus, leader of the Jewish revolt (167–160 BCE). The text refers to 2 Maccabees 12, often cited in Catholic tradition to support the doctrine of Purgatory.</note>
Brought together a treasure of silver for their relief,
To pour out prayers in Jerusalem as an atonement for the dead,
Who were tainted with heathen and idolatrous treasures,
According to the measure of this number;
A sign that the soul shall rise again in the body.

<margin>The dwelling of those unpurified of original sin.</margin>
The third ring mercifully guards the innocents,
Who, unpurified and counted as guilty of original sin <note>The "first father's stain" refers to the sin of Adam, which in traditional theology prevents unbaptized souls from entering the highest heaven.</note>
Due to the first father’s stain, do not ascend to the arch
Of pure light, where God—seated so deep in light, so high 
Upon a throne of bright Cherubim—
Reveals His face, and dims all sunshine:
Just as the sun extinguishes the shining stars
That burn like carbuncles <note>A deep red gemstone, often used in older literature to describe glowing or fiery light.</note> in the midnight sky,
As soon as she lifts her head from the waters.
No pain is felt here, no bundle of serpent-rods <note>A classical reference to the whips of the Furies; the poet emphasizes that while these souls are denied the "Beatific Vision" of God, they do not suffer physical torture.</note>

<meta>catchword: Ge-</meta>

<summary>The poet describes the permanence of Hell before transitioning to the higher circles of the afterlife, specifically Purgatory—where souls await purification—and the Limbo of Infants or the Fathers, where those with original sin remain without physical pain but are denied the sight of God.</summary>

->### JOANNES<-

...merely locked within a soul that, joined to a corrupt branch <note>A metaphor for original sin or the fallen nature inherited from Adam.</note>, possessed no free will, no understanding, nor the use of reason. When the Almighty shall strip this great universe of its old skin, she <note>The soul.</note> hopes through God's grace that she might rise glorified to His praise, beyond the horizon, into a newly created paradise.

<margin>The dwelling of the blessed patriarchs, before and under the Law, who long for the Messiah.</margin>
The uppermost circle preserves, after temporal suffering, the spirits of the fathers from before and since the times of Noah, who have fallen asleep in God and are unburdened from the yoke of the body. They were here in their pleasant garden <note>original: "waerande," a term for a park or pleasure ground.</note>, and hoped firmly in God the Messiah, the comfort and refuge of all the pious, crying out: "Saviour, will You not appear? The time previously foretold to the ancestral line has passed: put Your word into effect—the promise, the prophecy, and the omen, in which our ears and eyes have clearly seen and heard You for centuries. Let all shadows finally give way to the light. Enlighten us with Your radiance and Your blessed countenance."

<margin>John appears to them.</margin>
Thus they prayed, when suddenly the radiance of the morning star, a herald of God’s sun—John—descended swiftly from afar beneath the earth and shone into these gardens, before whom the fading shadows vanished.

<margin>A simile, depicting the joy of the blessed souls.</margin>
Just as in the morning, when the day begins to break, the nightingales—who scent the life-giving sun—are awakened from their dreams and begin to sing in the woods, freshly bedewed with silver drops like pearls. The song begins to penetrate the soul through the ear. One sees them leaping among the laurel branches and myrtle twigs. The flock of birds, enamored with the cheerful woodland echoes and their own song, runs a great risk and shall—unless it restrains itself in its flighty rejoicing—suffer shipwreck in a sweet sea of melodies.

So it went when God’s herald, at the sound of their cheering voices, reached the prison door, which quickly shut behind him,

And no one stayed away; he came drifting in, along the path
Previously marked. He held a standard-banner,

In this pleasure garden <note>original: "waerande," referring to the Limbo of the Patriarchs as a place of rest.</note>, all that was of worth here, from all around,

To the loud triumphal shout of leaping tongues,
<margin>The hellish spirits rise up like thunder.</margin>
All the black haunting spirits come thundering from their hollow,
And roar, and snort, and howl, and foam at the mouth, cruel and mad,

A star shining forth from his forehead, like the sparkling

<margin>Simile</margin>

With his staff, drives them out of the stall, strong and fierce:
So they go slinking away, and chew again on their old grudges,
And lick their tails, tucked in behind.
But how can one best describe how unrestrainedly happy
<margin>Simile</margin>

In a besieged city [surrounded from all sides,
Stormed, and struck with the ram, and fiercely shot
With terrifying sling-fire,] who constantly day and night
Wait for the long-promised relief from their protector:
If the vanguard should once break through with force,
And hang the king's standard-banner from the tower,
Then the heart of the people rises [weary from great longing,
And tortured by hope] even unto heaven.
One sees the field-banner waving on gate and fortress,
And hears the war-trumpet <note>original: "krijghsklaeroen"</note> already crowing triumph.

->### JOHN ###<-

To show himself, upon a height and hill, in noble standing,
The standard-banner held in his left hand,
The right covers his heart: just as he was accustomed to teach,
And in his wilderness, to bring the wildness <note>original: "veraerden," implying the wild nature of the beasts.</note> of wolves, bears,
And even tigers, under the restraint of his tongue,
What a jubilant shout of souls is heard, old and young,
And of every kind of status, to behold that herald <note>John the Baptist acts as the "herout" or messenger announcing the arrival of the King.</note>,
Each one equally fervent, and supported with firm trust
In God, who through grace strengthens his holy promises!
One sees there how one spirit works within so many spirits,
<margin>Simile</margin>
And sets them in order: just as they, under high guidance,
Once before in the body, through the wilderness and barren heath
Marched toward the promised and fruitful Canaan;
A glorious order, and a harmonious team under the law:
And here it was necessary that they stepped along him in order:
So that his face could best satisfy all eyes.
Now let us look out toward that great procession,
And how John’s soul could gather many souls, without coercion,
In a single moment, with one voice:
<margin>Simile</margin>
Just as a master’s hand unites many throats, many strings,
Into a common whole by keeping the tempo,
A conflict of sounds, sweetly melted into one.
<margin>Adam and Eve come to the front.</margin>
At the front steps Adam, the first-created, and chosen
For an immortality, destined for him and his seed.
His spouse Eve treads all-rejoicing at his side,
And welcomes John with her lord, equally glad,
Who sees two Adams exactly resembling one another <note>A theological comparison between the "First Adam" (the first man) and the "Second Adam" (Christ, represented here by his herald John).</note>.
They cry: "Welcome to the subterranean realms.
What tidings do you bring us in the twilight here below?"
John says: "He comes to crush the head of the serpent <note>A reference to the 'Protevangelium' in Genesis 3:15, the first promise of a Savior.</note>.
The great Savior comes to the subterranean places.
The holy Jubilee year cannot complete its cycle,
Before he shall burst out of the light [remember what I promise]
Into hell, snatching from the abyss its prey."

By force they seize victory, and, as a sign of triumph,

Directly behind them, like a prototype of God's Lamb,

<margin>Noah and his contemporaries.</margin>

Willingly ourselves before that source of grace,
With our services. May He appear not too late.
No law-giving, nor sin-offerings, freshly slain,
Reconciles God. No blood can wash away our soul-stain,"

<meta>This page continues the sequence of Old Testament figures in Limbo. Having previously seen Moses, Miriam, and the Judges, the focus now shifts to King David and his prophetic psalms regarding the coming of Christ (the "Anointed") and the liberation of the souls from the underworld.</meta>

# THE SIXTH BOOK #

And prophecies, according to His holy promises, strengthen.
Heavenly grace outshines the wonders
Of creation, and the power known to the fathers;
A power that, tearing through the foaming element <note>The Red Sea.</note>,
Led the house of Abraham out of Pharaoh's darkness
Into Canaan. We sighed here below
In this dungeon, for so many centuries on end.
Here silence and gloom dwell together. No
Sound of birds, jumping from branch to branch,
Is heard; nor could the sun's ray ever penetrate
Into these enclosures where we were: and no dew
Ever refreshed grass and flower. This region, too tender and faint,
Bears flowers without color, and fruits without life.
The realm of the sighing is given to the silent,
In hope of one day beholding the New Jerusalem,
Where one shall give God praise with a common voice,
Century in, century out, from a single mouth.
How can I cease to think of my old song,
That the Most High, so faithful in His promise,
Through His Anointed, would smash these gates of brass <note>original: "metaele poorten." A reference to Psalm 107:16, "For he hath broken the gates of brass, and cut the bars of iron in sunder."</note>
To pieces, and break open the dungeon;
Unfetter my soul from the violence of the hellish regions,
And lead His spoils into the heavenly fatherland.
Archangels sang long, followed by my melody:
Open up, lift up the gate: open the court of courts <note>A reference to Psalm 24:7, "Lift up your heads, O ye gates; and be ye lift up, ye everlasting doors; and the King of glory shall come in."</note>.
The Lord of lords, cast off by everyone like a curse,
Trodden like a worm, refreshed with gall and vinegar <note>Allusions to the suffering of Christ in the Psalms, specifically Psalm 22 ("I am a worm") and Psalm 69 (gall and vinegar).</note>,
Appears, so that He may avenge Himself on all those who hate Him,
And sit at the Father's side, where the angels, in the midst
Of the light, so deep in the light, incense Him and worship:
While arch-enemies, the Heathens and hell,
Prostrate themselves before the Victor’s <term>footstool</term> <note>original: "voetſchabel." A footstool, used here as a biblical metaphor for the total subjection of enemies under a king's feet.</note>.
I have, by a higher hand and driven by the Spirit,
Long sung the wedding song <note>A reference to Psalm 45, traditionally interpreted as a wedding song for Christ and the Church.</note> and His triumphs:

### JOHN ###

“But it would please me,” spoke the king <note>King David.</note>, “to behold
God’s image in the body from your mouth: for you can
Paint Him from life with the colors of your tongue.
The whole gathering stands still. The divine rays
Of the risen sun of salvation shone upon you,
On the fairest of her days. Faithful youth, now
Let us hear what He is like, for whom all ages long,
Whom I called upon my harp the fairest of men.” <note>A reference to Psalm 45:2, "Thou art fairer than the children of men."</note>

<margin>John describes Christ from life.</margin>
John spoke: “O light of ancient Jerusalem,
How could I refuse to follow your royal voice?
Though you painted Him from life in song,
Long before God called me to build up all that lies
Wild and desolate, and to show a flat and level path
To the Hero upon whom your music strikes! <note>John refers to his role as the "voice crying in the wilderness" preparing the way for Christ.</note>
He is neither short nor tall, but slender and of middle height,
Distinguished in age, venerable and very stately
In appearance. Whoever sees Him, loves Him, and stands in awe:
While He draws one in with love, He commands even as He draws.
His hair, chestnut brown, is smooth down to the ears;
The locks, from the ears down, have a bluish-black sheen, waving
And curled in front, with a luster about the neck and shoulders.
The hair parts in the middle of the head,
After the fashion of the strict Nazarenes. <note>original: "Nazareen." This refers to the Nazarite vow (Numbers 6), which included the practice of leaving one's hair uncut as a sign of dedication to God.</note>
The forehead, joyful and simple,
Is like His countenance, upon which no blemish or wrinkle clings,
And is mingled more or less with a glowing radiance.
The beard falls youthfully thick, and, gently cleft, flows down
Upon His breast, though not too long; it is the color
Of the hair on His head, or just like ripe wheat.
His gaze is steady, and—not slow in observing,
But swift—it teaches alertness; and Jesus’ eyes are
As blue as heavenly blue, not lacking in luster,
Shadowed like arches by eyebrows black and dark.
Thus He teaches with kindness, and rebukes with great authority.
Expect Him soon. My star announces the day to you.” <note>John describes himself as the morning star that precedes the rising sun (Christ).</note>

# JOHN. #

"But I would take delight," the king <note>King David, often identified in this context by his harp.</note> spoke, "to see from your mouth God’s appearance in the body: for you are able to paint Him to the life with the colors of your tongue. The whole company stands still. The divine rays of the risen sun of salvation shone upon you, at the very beauty of His day. Faithful youth, let us hear now what He is like, He for whom all ages long, called upon my harp the 'fairest of men' <note>A reference to Psalm 45:2, "You are the most handsome of the sons of men."</note>."

<margin>
John depicts Christ to the life.
</margin>
John spoke: "O light of ancient Jerusalem, how could I refuse to follow your royal voice? Although you painted Him to the life in song long before God called me to build up all that lies desolate and wild, and to show a flat and level path to the Hero upon whom your music strikes!

He is neither short nor tall, but well-proportioned and of medium stature; respectable in his prime, venerable and very stately of countenance. Whoever sees Him loves Him, and yet stands in awe: while He draws one in with His loveliness, that very loveliness commands. His hair, chestnut brown, is straight down to the ears; from the ears down, the locks are dark <note>original: "blaeuachtigh," literally bluish-black.</note>, waving and curled in front, with a luster about the neck and shoulders. His hair is parted in the middle of the head, after the manner of the strict Nazarenes <note>Referring to the Nazarite vow or the traditional depiction of Jesus with a center part.</note>. The forehead, cheerful and simple, is like His face, upon which no blemish or wrinkle takes hold, and is more or less mixed with a healthy glow. The beard falls youthfully thick and, softly cleft, flows down upon His chest, though not too long; it is the same color as the hair on His head, exactly like ripe wheat. His gaze is steady and, not slow in observing but quick, it teaches alertness; and Jesus’s eyes are as blue as heavenly blue, and not lacking in luster, shadowed by the arch of eyebrows black and dark. Thus He teaches with kindness, and rebukes with great authority. Expect Him soon. My star <note>John the Baptist is often metaphorically described as the morning star (the Forerunner) that precedes the Sun (Christ).</note> announces the day to you."

"But it would please me," spoke the king, "to hear from your mouth
And behold God’s form in the body: for you can
Paint Him to the life with the colors of your tongue."
The entire procession stands still. The divine rays
Of the risen sun of salvation shone upon you,
In the beauty of its day. Faithful youth, now
Let us hear what he is like, for whom all ages long,
Whom I called upon my harp the fairest of men <note>A reference to Psalm 45:2: "Thou art fairer than the children of men."</note>.

<margin>*John depicts Christ to the life.*</margin>
*John* spoke: "O light of ancient Jerusalem,
How could I refuse to follow your royal voice:
Though you painted him to the life in song
Long before God called me to build up all that lies wild
And desolate, and to show a level and smooth path
To the hero upon whom your melodies strike!
He is neither short nor tall, but slender and of medium height,
Respectable in age, venerable and very stately
Of appearance. Whoever sees him, loves him and respects him:
While he draws one in lovingly, and in drawing, he commands.
The hair, chestnut brown, is straight down to the ears;
The locks, from the ears down bluish, waving and
Curled, with a gloss around the neck and shoulders.
The hair is parted, after the manner of the strict Nazarene <note>original: "Nazareen." This refers to the "Nazirite" tradition of letting hair grow long and parting it, which was the standard artistic depiction of Jesus based on the apocryphal Letter of Lentulus.</note>,
In the middle of the head. The forehead, cheerful and simple,
Is like the face, upon which no spot or wrinkle takes hold,
And is mixed more or less with a healthy glow.
The beard falls youthful and thick, and, softly cleft, flows down
Upon his chest, though not too long, and is of the same color
As the hair on the head, or exactly like ripe wheat.
The gaze is steady, and—not slow in observing,
But quick—it teaches alertness: and *Jesus'* eyes are
As blue as heavenly blue, also not lacking in luster,
Shaded arch-like by eyebrows black and dark.
Thus he teaches kindly, and rebukes with great authority.
Expect him soon. My star heralds the day to you."

Having previously described the first three "beasts" of the heavenly chariot (traditionally the lion, ox, and eagle), the text now describes the fourth beast (the man) and transitions from the prophets of the Old Testament to the arrival of the New Covenant, represented by Simeon and John the Baptist.</meta>

### JOHN ###

That he increases the team <note>Referring to the four winged creatures of Ezekiel's vision, often interpreted as the Four Evangelists.</note>, and is deemed worthy
To set the Triumphant One in his power,
Carried to heaven with tireless wings.
The fourth and last appears as a man, and while running,
A spirit with wings, grown from the back.
The heavenly blue garment, full of stars, flutters swiftly
And lightly around the limbs. One greater than Elijah <note>John the Baptist, whom Jesus described as the "Elijah who was to come."</note>,
<margin>Malachi.</margin>
Is thus carried on high towards God: and Malachi,
The last in all that throng, cried loudly: I see no man,
But an angel, God’s herald, the desire of angels <note>In Hebrew, the name "Malachi" means "My Messenger" or "My Angel."</note>.
The messenger of repentance appears, to pave the way for God.
<margin>The heroes, and Maccabees, and high priests, and their followers.</margin>
Then the heroes revealed themselves with their banners.
There one saw princes, and famous Maccabees,
High priests, and a train mixed together.
Thus the procession of the Old Covenant was closed,
And gave way to the new era, which led forth
Its companions of God. The first was the aged Simeon,
<margin>Old Simeon.</margin>
Who took God <note>The Christ child during the Presentation in the Temple.</note> in his arms, rejoiced that such a sun
Came to enlighten the blind Gentiles from on high.
Salvation, the healing of God, looked out from his eyes
And face. He cried: John, do you come here?
I recognize that name on the standard, and honor that banner of the Lamb.
How does that red cord come to shine around your neck? <note>A poetic reference to John's decapitation; the "red cord" represents the mark of martyrdom.</note>
How dared Herodias strike your head from your body?
O first arch-martyr, what fame follows your death!
How many churches are seen dedicated in your name!
How many heroes, summoned to the field for God’s praise,
Will you yet dub as knights with your blessed battle-sword! <note>A reference to the Sovereign Military Order of Saint John (the Knights Hospitaller or Knights of Malta), who took John the Baptist as their patron saint.</note>
You shall, century after century, protect the people of Abraham
From Ishmael <note>Historically used here to refer to the Ottoman Empire and Islamic forces, seen as descendants of Ishmael.</note>—and also those who, according to God’s promise,
Are counted as descended from that patriarch’s stock,
Though not so much according to the flesh <note>Refers to Christians, who in theology are considered the spiritual "seed of Abraham" regardless of ethnicity.</note>. Blood shed for God
Is highly esteemed by God. John seemed gladdened
By such a knighthood, dedicated to heaven;

<meta>This page continues the catalog of holy figures encountered in the afterlife. ### THE SIXTH BOOK ###

<margin>*The Holy Innocents, murdered by Herod.*</margin>
When a joyful flight of innocent child-souls,
Who fell, struck down by Herod’s sword,
Rushed forward, waiting for Mary’s son
To come and crown them with the radiance of the first martyr's crown.
They sang Jesus’ praise, not without bowing low,
Like Cherubim <note>A high order of angels; the poet compares the martyred children to these heavenly beings.</note> who bear witness to God’s praise on high.

<margin>*Anna the prophetess.*</margin>
One saw there Anna, the aged and God-consecrated widow,
Who, attending the choir night and day, spent nearly a full century <note>The biblical Anna was a prophetess at the Temple who recognized the infant Jesus; the text notes her long life of devotion.</note>
Fasting in chastity and prayer.
But when John <note>John the Baptist.</note>, in the shadows here below,
<margin>*Zechariah and Elizabeth,*</margin>
Came to see his parents amidst so many woodland echoes,
He fell upon the necks of his father and mother.
All three were seen weeping with great joy,
<margin>*and Joseph.*</margin>
And Joseph joined them. He then accompanied them
With mutual conversation, as the righteous are accustomed to do.
Thus the procession <note>original: "ommegangk" - refers here to the ceremonial parade of holy souls.</note> was finally concluded.

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the poetic narrative of the "Sixth Book"—which described the gathering of holy souls like the Holy Innocents and John the Baptist—to a prose address. Here, the author justifies the use of poetic license and creative expansion when retelling sacred biblical stories, comparing the poet’s task to that of a painter.</meta>

### To
## THE READER.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'H' decorated with scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

*Although it is well enough known to the lover of poetry <note>original: "dichtkunste"</note> in what manner poets have been accustomed from of old to expand and portray their histories <note>original: "geschiedenissen"; here referring to narrative accounts or stories</note>: nevertheless, since many, trained only in our mother tongue and less understanding of the colors of eloquence <note>original: "verwen der welspreeckenheit"; a rhetorical term referring to the use of metaphors and stylistic ornaments</note>, might easily be surprised when they hear and see a holy history presented more extensively than it was described by the sacred pen of the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>; so I thought it not unadvisable to remind them how the poet, like the painter, is free to set up his work according to requirement and propriety, and to clothe it richly: for to unfold a history, one must not only invent the conditions and appearances of places and persons, but also other circumstances that best fit the occasion. One should not think, therefore, that the painter wishes to persuade the viewer that his drawing and brush followed the life of ancient histories so exactly that all his inventions and arrangements did not differ from the matter itself. Whoever considers this freedom of art to be grounded in reason will also calmly grant it to the poet, who by right may graze so much more widely, as thoughts can reach higher and deeper than the eyes can see; provided that in biblical subject matter <term>biblical subject matter: original "bijbelstoffe," referring to the specific material or content drawn from the Bible</term> he generally keeps himself within the bounds of a reverent godliness <note>original: "godtvruchtigheit"</note> and modesty <note>original: "zedigheit"</note>.*

<summary>In this preface, the author defends the use of poetic embellishment in religious literature by comparing the poet to a painter who must add detail and "color" to a scene to make it vivid. He argues that as long as the poet remains pious and respectful, they are free to expand upon the brief accounts found in the Gospels.</summary>
<keywords>poetic license, artistic freedom, biblical narrative, eloquence, piety, painting vs. poetry, Evangelists, aesthetics</keywords>
